"Wooed and married and a'."

"Edith!" said Margaret, gently, "Edith!"

But as Margaret half suspected, Edith had already succumbed to sleep. She lay curled upon the sofa in the back drawing-room of Harley Street, looking exquisitely lovely in her white muslin and blue ribbons. If Titania had ever been attired in such simple finery and allowed to drift off upon a crimson damask sofa within a private parlour, Edith might easily have been mistaken for the fairy queen herself. Margaret was once again struck by her cousin's undeniable beauty. Having grown up together since infancy, everyone had remarked upon Edith's prettiness save for Margaret, who had remained largely indifferent to it until recently. Only now, with the impending loss of her constant companion looming on the horizon, did Margaret begin to truly appreciate every sweet quality and charm Edith possessed.

They had been discussing wedding dresses, ceremonial details, Captain Lennox, and his descriptions of life awaiting Edith at Corfu, where his regiment was stationed. They had also debated the formidable challenge of keeping a piano in tune during the Scottish travels that would inevitably follow their marriage. However, Margaret's voice had gradually softened into a drowse, and after a brief pause, she realized that despite the lively chatter spilling from the adjacent room, Edith had rolled herself into a soft sphere of muslin, ribbon, and silken curls, drifting peacefully into an afternoon nap.

Margaret had intended to confide in her cousin about her own visions for the future: a life in the country parsonage where her parents resided, a place where her brightest holidays had always been spent, even though for the last decade Aunt Shaw's house in London had served as her home. Yet, lacking a listener, she was forced to brood silently on the coming changes. It was a happy contemplation, albeit tinged with regret at being parted indefinitely from her gentle aunt and beloved cousin. As she imagined the joy of becoming the sole daughter in Helstone parsonage, fragments of conversation drifted from the next room.

Aunt Shaw was speaking with the five or six ladies who had dined there, whose husbands remained in the dining-room. These women were familiar acquaintances of the household; neighbors whom Mrs. Shaw termed "friends" simply because she dined with them more frequently than others, and because requests for favors between the households were exchanged casually before luncheon. These ladies and their husbands had been invited specifically to share a farewell dinner honoring Edith's upcoming nuptials. Edith had initially objected to the arrangement, noting that Captain Lennox was expected to arrive via a late train that very evening. However, though she was a spoilt child, she lacked a strong will of her own and yielded when she discovered her mother had strictly ordered the seasonal delicacies traditionally believed to alleviate excessive grief at farewell banquets. Content with this concession, Edith leaned back in her chair, idly fiddling with her food, appearing grave and absent-minded, while those around her delighted in the witty remarks of Mr. Grey, the gentleman who invariably sat at the foot of the table at Mrs. Shaw's gatherings and had requested that Edith provide music later in the drawing-room. Mr. Grey proved particularly agreeable tonight, causing the gentlemen to linger downstairs longer than usual—a decision that proved wise, judging by the snippets of conversation Margaret overheard.

"I suffered too much myself," one lady remarked. "Not that I wasn't extremely happy with the poor dear General, but disparity of age is a significant drawback—one I was resolved Edith should not encounter. Without any maternal bias, I foresaw the dear child was likely to marry early; indeed, I often said I was certain she would wed before nineteen. I had a prophetic feeling regarding Captain Lennox..." The voice dropped to a whisper, but Margaret could easily fill in the gap.

The path of true love in Edith's case had run remarkably smooth. Mrs. Shaw had acquiesced to this sentiment, expressing that she had actually encouraged the match, despite it falling short of the high expectations many held for a young, pretty heiress. Mrs. Shaw declared that her only child should marry for love—and sighed emphatically, implying that love had not been her primary motivation when she wed the General. Indeed, Mrs. Shaw seemed to derive greater enjoyment from the romance of the current engagement than her daughter did. While Edith was thoroughly and properly in love, she would undoubtedly have preferred a substantial residence in Belgravia to all the picturesque allure of life at Corfu. The very aspects that made Margaret glow with anticipation caused Edith to pretend to shiver and shudder; partly for the pleasure of being coaxed out of her reluctance by her devoted lover, and partly because anything resembling a gypsy or makeshift existence was genuinely distasteful to her. Nevertheless, had anyone offered a magnificent house, a vast estate, and a noble title, Edith would have clung to Captain Lennox as long as the temptation persisted. Once the novelty faded, she might well harbor regrets that her fiancé could not embody every desirable trait simultaneously. In this regard, she was merely following her mother's example, who, after deliberately marrying General Shaw out of mere respect for his character and status, quietly lamented her hard fate of being united to a man she could not love.

"I have spared no expense on her trousseau," another voice added. "She possesses all the beautiful Indian shawls and scarves the General gave me, though I shall never wear them again."

"She is a fortunate girl," replied another voice, which Margaret recognized as belonging to Mrs. Gibson, a lady taking a keen interest in the conversation due to her own daughter having married just weeks prior. "Helen had her heart set on an Indian shawl, but when I learned the exorbitant price, I was forced to decline. She will be quite envious to learn Edith has received such treasures. What kind are they? Delhi? With the lovely little borders?"

Margaret heard her aunt's voice again, seemingly rising from her half-reclining posture to gaze toward the dimly lit back drawing-room. "Edith! Edith!" she cried, then sank back as if weary by the effort.

Margaret stepped forward. "Edith is asleep, Aunt Shaw. Is there anything I can do?"

All the ladies exclaimed, "Poor child!" upon hearing this distressing news, and the tiny lapdog in Mrs. Shaw's arms began to bark, agitated by the outburst of pity. "Hush, Tiny! You naughty little girl! You will wake your mistress. It was only to ask Edith if she would instruct Newton to bring down her shawls; perhaps you would go, Margaret dear?"

Margaret ascended to the old nursery at the very top of the house, where Newton was busy preparing laces required for the wedding. As Newton departed (accompanied by muttered complaints) to retrieve the shawls—which had already been displayed four or five times that day—Margaret surveyed the nursery. It was the first room in that house she had known intimately nine years ago, when she arrived, untamed from the New Forest, to share the home, play, and lessons of her cousin Edith. She recalled the dark, somber atmosphere of the London nursery, overseen by an austere and ceremonious nurse who was obsessively particular about clean hands and unblemished frocks. She remembered the first tea served there—served separately from her father and aunt, who dined far below, an infinite depth of stairs away; for unless she was soaring in the sky, the children reasoned, her family must be buried deep in the earth's bowels.

At home, before moving to Harley Street, her mother's dressing-room had served as her nursery. Since they maintained early hours in the country parsonage, Margaret had always eaten her meals with her parents. Oh, how vividly the tall, stately eighteen-year-old remembered the tears shed with such wild, passionate grief by the little girl of nine, hiding her face beneath the bedclothes on that first night! How she had been bid not to cry by the nurse lest it disturb Miss Edith, and how she had sobbed more quietly and bitterly until her newly encountered, grand, and pretty aunt arrived softly upstairs with Mr. Hale to introduce himself to his little sleeping daughter. Then, the little Margaret had hushed her sobs, striving to lie motionless as if asleep, fearing her grief might upset her father, which she deemed improper after the months of hopeful planning and preparation undertaken at home to accommodate her elevated circumstances and her father's temporary visit to London.

Now, the tall girl loved the old nursery, despite its dismantled state, casting a cat-like glance of regret at the idea of leaving it forever in three days.

"Ah, Newton!" she said. "I believe we shall all be sorry to depart from this dear old room."

"Indeed, miss, I shan't for one. My eyes are not as sharp as they used to be, and the light here is so poor I cannot see to mend laces except near the window, where there is a dreadful draft—enough to freeze one to death."

"Well, I daresay you'll have ample light and warmth in Naples. Keep as much of your darning as you can until then. Thank you, Newton; I can take them down yourself—you're busy."

Thus, Margaret descended, laden with shawls, inhaling their spicy Eastern fragrance. Her aunt requested she serve as a living mannequin to display the garments, as Edith remained asleep. No one noticed, but Margaret's tall, elegantly formed figure, clad in a black silk dress worn as mourning for a distant relative of her

"By the soft green light in the woody glade, / On the banks of moss where thy childhood played..."

Margaret Hale stood once more in her morning dress, traveling quietly home with her father, who had journeyed north to assist at the wedding. Her mother, however, had been detained at Helstone by a multitude of vague excuses, none of which anyone truly understood save Mr. Hale himself. He knew perfectly well that all his arguments in favor of a grey satin gown—one that sat uncomfortably halfway between antiquity and novelty—had proven futile. Lacking the funds to outfit his wife anew from top to bottom, Mrs. Hale simply would not appear at the wedding of her only sister's only child. Had Mrs. Shaw divined the true reason for her sister-in-law's absence, she likely would have showered gowns upon her; but it had been nearly twenty years since Mrs. Shaw was the poor, pretty Miss Beresford, and she had genuinely forgotten all grievances save one: the unhappiness born of disparity in age within marriage, a topic on which she could discourse for half an hour at a stretch. "Dearest Maria," she would muse, reflecting on her sister's lot, "married for love, what can she possibly lack in this world?"

If Mrs. Hale spoke truthfully, her answer might have been a ready-made list: *a silver-grey glacé silk, a white chip bonnet, oh! dozens of things for the wedding, and hundreds for the house.* Yet, Margaret merely knew that her mother had deemed it inconvenient to travel. She was not sorry that their reunion would occur at Helstone Parsonage rather than amidst the chaotic whirlwind of the previous few days in Harley Street, where she had been forced to play the role of Figaro, summoned everywhere simultaneously. Her mind and body ached with the recollection of everything she had done and said within the last forty-eight hours. Amongst the hurried farewells, the memory of those she had lived with so long pressed heavily upon her, bringing a sad regret for times that were irrevocably gone. It mattered not what those times had been; they were vanished forever.

As they traveled toward her beloved home—the place and life she had yearned for for years—Margaret felt a heaviness in her heart that she had never anticipated. Just before sleep dulls the sharp edges of sensation, the longing is most acute. She tore her mind away from the past to contemplate the bright, serene future. Her gaze shifted from visions of what had been to the reality before her: her dear father leaning back, asleep in the railway carriage. His blue-black hair was now grey, lying thinly over his brow. The bones of his face were starkly visible—not disfiguring, for his features were finely cut, lending them a certain grace, though lacking the comeliness of youth. His face was in repose, yet it bore the marks of rest following weariness rather than the serene calm of a placid existence.

Margaret was painfully struck by this worn, anxious expression. She traced the lines that spoke so plainly of habitual distress and depression back to the known circumstances of her father's life. "Poor Frederick!" she sighed inwardly. "Oh, if Frederick had been a clergyman instead of joining the navy, and thus lost to us all! I wish I knew the whole truth. I never understood it from Aunt Shaw; I only knew he could not return to England because of that terrible affair. Poor dear papa! How sad he looks! I am so glad I am going home, to be at hand to comfort him and mamma."

She prepared a bright smile, devoid of any trace of fatigue, to greet her father upon awakening. He smiled back, but faintly, as if the effort were unusual. His face settled back into its familiar lines of anxiety. He possessed a habit of half-opening his mouth as if to speak, a gesture that constantly unsettled the form of his lips, giving his visage an air of indecision. Yet, he retained the same large, soft eyes as his daughter—eyes that moved slowly and almost grandly within their orbits, veiled by transparent white eyelids. Margaret was more like him than like her mother. Occasionally, people marveled that parents so handsome could produce a daughter described as "not beautiful at all." Her mouth was wide, not a tight rosebud capable of uttering only "yes" or "no," but a soft curve of rich red lips. Her skin, though not porcelain pale, possessed an ivory smoothness and delicacy. While her general demeanor was perhaps too dignified and reserved for one so young, speaking to her father transformed her; she became bright as the morning, full of dimples and glances radiating childish gladness and boundless hope.

It was the latter part of July when Margaret returned home. The forest trees stood in a dark, full, dusky green; the fern beneath them caught the slanting sunbeams. The weather was sultry and broodingly still. Margaret loved to tramp alongside her father, crushing the fern with a cruel glee as it yielded under her light foot, releasing its peculiar fragrance. Out on the broad commons, bathed in warm, scented light, she saw multitudes of wild, free creatures reveling in the sunshine and the herbs it nourished. This life—at least these walks—realized all her anticipations. She took a profound pride in her forest; its inhabitants were her people. She made hearty friends with them, learned and delighted in their peculiar dialect, embraced their freedom, nursed their babies, and conversed or read aloud to their elders with slow distinctness. She carried dainties to the sick and resolved soon to teach at the school her father attended daily, viewing it as an appointed task. Yet, she was continually tempted away to visit individual friends—men, women, or children—in the green shade of the cottages scattered throughout the woods. Her outdoor life was perfect.

Her indoor life, however, held its drawbacks. With the healthy shame of a child, she blamed herself for her keen insight into the fact that all was not as it should be within the vicarage walls. Her mother, always so kind and tender, seemed intermittently discontented with their situation. She believed the Bishop strangely neglected his episcopal duties by failing to grant Mr. Hale a better living, and she almost reproached her husband for his inability to declare his wish to leave the parish and accept a larger charge. Mr. Hale would sigh deeply in response, stating that if he could do what he ought in little Helstone, he should be thankful, yet every day he appeared more overwhelmed; the world seemed increasingly bewildering. At each renewed urging from his wife to seek preferment, Margaret observed her father shrinking further into himself. During these moments, she strove to reconcile her mother to Helstone.

Mrs. Hale claimed that the proximity of so many trees affected her health. Margaret would attempt to lure her onto the beautiful, broad, upland common, streaked with sunlight and shadow, convinced that her mother had grown too accustomed to an indoor existence, rarely venturing beyond the church, the school, and neighboring cottages. This remedy offered temporary relief, but as autumn advanced and the weather grew changeable, her mother's conviction of the place's unhealthiness intensified. She complained more frequently that her husband—who was more learned than Mr. Hume and a better parish priest than Mr. Houldsworth—should not have secured the preferment those two former neighbors had achieved. This erosion of domestic peace, driven by long hours of discontent, was something Margaret was wholly unprepared for.

She had known, and indeed reveled in the notion, that returning home meant relinquishing many luxuries that had previously been mere troubles and trammels to her freedom in Harley Street. Her keen enjoyment of every sensuous pleasure was finely balanced, if not outweighed, by her conscious pride in her ability to dispense with them entirely if necessary. However, clouds rarely approach from the direction where one expects them. There had been slight complaints and passing regrets from her mother regarding trifles connected to Helstone and her father's position during Margaret's earlier holiday visits, but amidst the general happiness of recalling those times, she had overlooked the small, unpleasant details.

In the latter half of September, autumnal rains and storms descended, forcing Margaret to remain indoors more than she had previously done. Helstone lay at a considerable distance from neighbors sharing their standard of cultivation. "It is undoubtedly one of the most out-of-the-way places in England," Mrs. Hale remarked in one of her plaintive moods. "I cannot help constantly regretting that papa has no one to associate with here; he is so thrown away, seeing nothing but farmers and laborers from week's end to week's end. If we only lived on the other side of the parish, it would be different; there we should be almost within walking distance of the Stansfields; certainly the Gormons would be reachable on foot."

"Gormons?" Margaret asked. "Are those the Gormons who made their fortunes in trade at Southampton? Oh! I'm glad we don't visit them. I don't like shopkeepers. I believe we are far better off, knowing only cottagers and laborers, and people without pretense."

"You must not be so fastidious, Margaret, dear!" her mother replied, secretly thinking of a young and handsome Mr. Gormon she had once encountered at Mr. Hume's.

"No! I consider my tastes very comprehensive. I like all people whose occupations involve the land; I like soldiers and sailors, and the three learned professions, as they call them. I am sure you do not expect me to admire butchers, bakers, and candlestick-makers, do you, Mamma?"

"Learn to win a lady's faith nobly, as the thing is high; bravely, as for life and death—with a loyal gravity. Lead her from the festive boards, point her to the starry skies, guard her, by your truthful words, pure from courtship's flatteries." —Mrs. Browning.

"Mr. Henry Lennox."

Margaret had been thinking of him only a moment before, recalling his inquiry regarding her probable occupations at home. It was *parler du soleil et l'on en voit les rayons*; and the brightness of the sun seemed to flood her face as she set down her embroidery board and stepped forward to shake his hand.

"Tell Mamma, Sarah," she said gently. "Mamma and I want to ask you so many questions about Edith; I am so much obliged to you for coming."

"Did not I say that I should?" he asked, lowering his voice slightly from the tone he had used previously. "But I heard of you so far away in the Highlands that I never thought Hampshire could come into play."

"Oh!" he added, more lightly, "our young couple were playing such foolish pranks, running all sorts of risks—climbing mountains, sailing on lakes—that I really thought they needed a Mentor to keep them safe. And indeed they did; they were quite beyond my uncle's management, keeping the old gentleman in a panic for sixteen hours out of twenty-four. When I realized how unfit they were to be trusted alone, I felt it my duty not to leave them until I had seen them safely embarked at Plymouth."

"Have you been at Plymouth? Oh! Edith never mentioned that. To be sure, she has written in such a hurry lately. Did they really sail on Tuesday?"

"Really sailed, and relieved me from many responsibilities," he replied. "Edith gave me all sorts of messages for you. I believe I have a little diminutive note somewhere; yes, here it is."

"Oh! thank you," Margaret exclaimed. Then, half wishing to read it alone and unwatched, she made the excuse of returning to tell her mother again—that Sarah surely had made some mistake—that Mr. Lennox was present.

When she had left the room, he began, in his scrutinizing manner, to look about him. The little drawing-room was looking its best in the streaming light of the morning sun. The middle window in the bow stood open, and clustering roses and scarlet honeysuckle peeped around the corner; the small lawn was gorgeous with verbenas and geraniums of every bright color. Yet, the very brilliance outside made the colors within seem poor and faded. The carpet was far from new; the chintz had been washed often; the entire apartment appeared smaller and shabbier than he had expected, serving as a humble background for Margaret, who stood before it so queenly.

He picked up one of the books lying on the table. It was the *Paradise* of Dante, bound in proper old Italian white vellum and gold. Beside it lay a dictionary and some words copied out in Margaret's handwriting. They were a dull list, but somehow he liked looking at them. He put them down with a sigh. "The living is evidently as small as she said," he murmured. "It seems strange, for the Beresfords belong to a good family."

Meanwhile, Margaret had found her mother. It was one of Mrs. Hale's fitful days, when everything felt like a difficulty and a hardship. Mr. Lennox's arrival took on this shape, though secretly she felt complimented that he deemed it worth while to call.

"It is most unfortunate!" she cried. "We are dining early today, having nothing but cold meat so the servants may finish their ironing; and yet, of course, we must ask him to dinner—he is Edith's brother-in-law, after all. And your papa is in such low spirits this morning about something—I don't know what. I went into the study just now, and he had his face on the table, covering it with his hands. I told him I was sure Helstone air did not agree with him any more than with me, and he suddenly lifted up his head and begged me not to speak a word more against Helstone; he could not bear it. If there is one place he loves on earth, it is Helstone. But I am sure, for all that, it is the damp and relaxing air."

Margaret felt as if a thin, cold cloud had descended between her and the sun. She had listened patiently, hoping it might relieve her mother to unburden herself, but now it was time to draw her attention back to Mr. Lennox.

"Papa likes Mr. Lennox; they got on together famously at the wedding breakfast," Margaret said quickly. "I daresay his coming will do Papa good. And never mind the dinner, dear Mamma. Cold meat will do capitally for a lunch, which is the light in which Mr. Lennox will most likely view a two o'clock dinner."

"But what are we to do with him till then? It is only half-past ten."

"I'll ask him to go out sketching with me. I know he draws, and that will take him out of your way, Mamma. Only do come in now; he will think it so strange if you don't."

Mrs. Hale took off her black silk apron and smoothed her face. She looked a very pretty, lady-like woman as she greeted Mr. Lennox with the cordiality due to one who was almost a relation. He evidently expected to be asked to spend the day and accepted the invitation with a glad readiness that made Mrs. Hale wish she could add something to the cold beef. He was pleased with everything; delighted with Margaret's idea of going out sketching together; and would not have Mr. Hale disturbed for the world, with the prospect of meeting him at dinner so soon.

Margaret brought out her drawing materials for him to choose from. After the paper and brushes had been duly selected, the two set out in the merriest spirits imaginable.

"Now, please, just stop here for a minute or two," said Margaret. "These are the cottages that haunted me so during the rainy fortnight, reproaching me for not having sketched them before they tumbled down and were no more seen. Truly, if they are to be sketched—and they are very picturesque—we had better not put it off till next year. But where shall we sit?"

"Oh! You might have come straight from chambers in the Temple, instead of having been two months in the Highlands!" Margaret pointed out. "Look at this beautiful trunk of a tree, which the woodcutters have left just in the right place for the light. I will put my plaid over it, and it will be a regular forest throne."

"With your feet in that puddle for a regal footstool!" Mr. Lennox laughed. "Stay, I will move, and then you can come nearer this way. Who lives in these cottages?"

"They were built by squatters fifty or sixty years ago," Margaret explained. "One is uninhabited; the foresters are going to take it down as soon as the other is dead, poor old fellow! Look—there he is. I must go and speak to him. He is so deaf you will hear all our secrets."

The old man stood bareheaded in the sun, leaning on his stick at the front of his cottage. His stiff features relaxed into a slow smile as Margaret approached and spoke to him. Mr. Lennox hastily introduced the two figures into his sketch, finishing the landscape with a subordinate reference to them—as Margaret perceived later, when the time came for packing up water, scraps of paper, and comparing their work.

She laughed and blushed. Mr. Lennox watched her countenance closely.

"Now, I call that treacherous," she said. "I little thought that you were making old Isaac and me into subjects when you told me to ask him the history of these cottages."

"It was irresistible," he replied. "You can't know how strong a temptation it was. I hardly dare tell you how much I shall like this sketch."

He was not quite sure whether she heard this latter sentence before she went to the brook to wash her palette. She came back rather flushed, but looking perfectly innocent and unconscious. He was glad of it, for the speech had slipped from him unawares—a rare occurrence for a man who premeditated his actions so meticulously as Henry Lennox.

The aspect of home was all right and bright when they returned. The clouds on her mother's brow had cleared under the propitious influence of a brace of carp, most opportunely presented by a neighbor. Mr. Hale had returned from his morning's rounds and was awaiting his visitor just outside the wicket gate leading into the garden. He looked a complete gentleman in his rather threadbare coat and well-worn hat. Margaret was proud of her father; she always retained a fresh, tender pride in seeing how favorably he impressed every stranger. Still, her quick eye scanned his face and found there traces of some unusual disturbance, which was merely put aside, not cleared away.

Mr. Hale asked to look at their sketches. "I think you have made the tints on the thatch too dark, have you not?" he said, returning Margaret's drawing and holding out his hand for Mr. Lennox's, which was withheld for a moment, no more.

"No, Papa! I don't think I have," Margaret interjected, peering over his shoulder as he examined the figures in Mr. Lennox's

"Cast me upon some naked shore,
Where I may tracke
Only the print of some sad wracke,
If thou be there though the seas roare,
I shall no gentler calm implore."

He was gone. The house was locked up for the evening, shutting out the deep blues and crimson ambers of the sky. Margaret ascended to prepare for the early tea, finding Dixon in a foul temper, irritated by the interruption a visitor had inevitably caused on such a busy day. He displayed his irritation by brushing away at Margaret's hair with vicious haste, claiming he was rushing to Mrs. Hale. Yet, despite his urgency, Margaret was forced to wait a considerable time in the drawing-room before her mother descended.

She sat alone by the fire, unlit candles standing sentinel on the table behind her, reviewing the day. She recalled the happy walk, the delightful sketching, the cheerful dinner, followed by that uncomfortable, miserable stroll in the garden. *How different men are to women!* she thought. Here she was, disturbed and unhappy, her instincts making a refusal impossible; while he, mere minutes after meeting with a rejection of what ought to have been the deepest, holiest proposal of his life, could speak as if briefs, success, and the superficial trappings of a good house and agreeable society were the sole objects of his desire.

"Oh dear!" she lamented internally. "How she could have loved him if he had but been different—a difference she felt, on reflection, went low, deep down." Then, a fleeting thought crossed her mind: perhaps his lightness was merely assumed, a mask covering a bitterness of disappointment that would have branded her own heart had she loved and been rejected.

Her mother entered before this whirlwind of thoughts could organize itself. Margaret had to shake off the recollections of the day's events and turn into a sympathetic listener to Dixon's complaints about the ironing-iron burning the blanket again, and to reports of Susan Lightfoot wearing artificial flowers in her bonnet, evidence of a vain and giddy character. Mr. Hale sipped his tea in abstracted silence; Margaret bore the brunt of the conversation. She wondered how her parents could be so forgetful, so careless of their companion throughout the day, failing to mention his name. She momentarily forgot that he had not made them an offer.

After tea, Mr. Hale rose, leaning his elbow on the chimney-piece, his head resting in his hand, lost in musing, sighing deeply from time to time. Mrs. Hale departed to consult with Dixon regarding winter clothing for the poor. Margaret prepared her mother's worsted work, shrinking from the prospect of the long evening ahead, wishing only for bedtime so she could revisit the day's events in solitude.

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out suddenly, in a tone of desperate urgency that made her startle. "Is that tapestry work of immediate consequence? Can you leave it and come into my study? I wish to speak to you about something very serious to us all."

*Very serious to us all.* Mr. Lennox had never had the chance for private conversation with her father following her refusal; otherwise, this would indeed be a grave matter. First, Margaret felt guilty and ashamed of growing into a woman enough to be considered for marriage. Second, she feared her father might be displeased that she had taken upon herself to decline Mr. Lennox's proposal. However, she quickly realized this was not about recent complications. He gestured for her to sit beside him, stirred the fire, snuffed the candles, and sighed twice before summoning the courage to speak. It came out abruptly: "Margaret! I am going to leave Helstone."

"Leave Helstone, papa! But why?"

Mr. Hale remained silent for a minute or two, playing nervously with papers on the table, opening his mouth to speak several times only to close it again without uttering a word. Margaret could not endure the suspense, which distressed her father even more than herself. "But why, dear papa? Do tell me!"

He looked up suddenly, then spoke with a slow, enforced calmness: "Because I must no longer be a minister in the Church of England."

Margaret had imagined nothing less than that some preferment her mother had coveted had finally fallen to her father—something forcing him to leave beautiful, beloved Helstone for some stately, silent closes in cathedral towns. Those places were grand and imposing, but to abandon Helstone forever for them would have been a sad, lingering pain. Yet, nothing compared to the shock of her father's announcement. What could he mean? It was worse for being so mysterious. The piteous distress on his face, almost imploring a merciful judgment from his child, gave her a sudden sickness. Could he be implicated in something Frederick had done? Frederick was an outlaw. Had her father, driven by natural love for his son, connived at anything?

"Oh! what is it? Speak, papa! Tell me all! Why can you no longer be a clergyman? Surely, if the Bishop were told all we know about Frederick, and the hard injustice—"

"It is nothing about Frederick; the Bishop would have nothing to do with that. It is all myself. Margaret, I will tell you about it. I will answer any questions this once, but after tonight, let us never speak of it again. I can meet the consequences of my painful, miserable doubts; but it is an effort beyond me to speak of what has caused so much suffering."

"Doubts, papa! Doubts as to religion?" Margaret asked, more shocked than ever.

"No! Not doubts as to religion; not the slightest injury to that." He paused. Margaret sighed as if standing on the verge of some new horror.

"I have been reading today of the two thousand who were ejected from their churches," Mr. Hale continued, smiling faintly, "trying to steal some of their bravery; but it is of no use—no use—I cannot help feeling it acutely."

"But, papa, have you well considered? Oh! it seems so terrible, so shocking," Margaret said, suddenly bursting into tears. The one staid foundation of her home, her idea of her beloved father, seemed to reel and rock. What could she say? What was to be done? Seeing her distress, Mr. Hale gathered his nerves to comfort her. Swallowing down dry, choking sobs that had risen in his heart, he went to his bookcase, retrieved a volume he had recently been reading, and found within it the strength to embark on his course.

"Listen, dear Margaret," he said, putting one arm around her waist. She took his hand tightly but could not lift her head, nor could she attend to his words due to her internal agitation. "This is the soliloquy of one who was once a clergyman in a country parish, like me. It was written by Mr. Oldfield, minister of Carsington in Derbyshire, a hundred and sixty years ago or more. His trials are over. He fought the good fight." He spoke the last two sentences low, as if to himself.

Then he read aloud: *"When thou canst no longer continue in thy work without dishonour to God, discredit to religion, foregoing thy integrity, wounding conscience, spoiling thy peace, and hazarding the loss of thy salvation; in a word, when the conditions upon which thou must continue (if thou wilt continue) in thy employments are sinful, and unwarranted by the word of God, thou mayest, yea, thou must believe that God will turn thy very silence, suspension, deprivation, and laying aside, to His glory, and the advancement of the Gospel's interest. When God will not use thee in one kind, yet He will in another. A soul that desires to serve and honour Him shall never want opportunity to do it; nor must thou so limit the Holy One of Israel, as to think He hath but one way in which He can glorify Himself by thee. He can do it by thy silence as well as by thy preaching; the laying aside as well as thy continuance in thy work. It is not pretence of doing God the greatest service, or performing the weightiest duty, that will excuse the least sin, though that sin capacitated or gave us the opportunity for doing that duty. Thou wilt have little thanks, O my soul! if, when thou art charged with corrupting God's worship, falsifying thy vows, thou pretendest a necessity for it in order to a continuance in the ministry."*

As he read, glancing at passages he chose not to voice, he gained resolution, feeling as if he too could be brave and firm in doing what he believed right. But as he ceased, he heard Margaret's low, convulsive sob, and his courage sank under the keen sense of suffering.

"Margaret, dear!" he said, drawing her closer. "Think of the early martyrs; think of the thousands who have suffered."

"But, father," she said, suddenly lifting her flushed, tear-wet face, "the early martyrs suffered for the truth, while you—oh! dear, dear papa!"

"I suffer for conscience' sake, my child," he replied with a dignity trembling only from the acute sensitivity of his nature. "I must do what my conscience bids. I have borne long with self-reproach that would have roused any mind less torpid and cowardly than mine." He shook his head. "Your poor mother's fond wish, gratified at last in the mocking way over-fond wishes are too often fulfilled—Sodom apples as they are—has brought on this crisis, for which I

"I ask Thee for a thoughtful love, Through constant watching wise, To meet the glad with joyful smiles, And to wipe the weeping eyes; And a heart at leisure from itself To soothe and sympathise." — *Anon.*

Margaret Hale proved herself a remarkably attentive listener to her mother's myriad schemes for alleviating the hardships of Helstone's poorest parishioners. Yet, beneath her polite attention, each new proposal struck a fresh blow to her heart. By the onset of the frost, they would be forced to depart. Old Simon's rheumatism would worsen, his failing sight would go unassisted, and the comforting visits of broth and warm flannel would be rendered impossible—or entrusted to strangers who could not fill the void left by her absence. Little Mary Domville's crippled son would crawl futilely to the gate, searching for a face that would never appear. These neighbors would never comprehend why she had abandoned them, nor would they understand the gravity of the situation. "Father has always expended his entire stipend within the parish," Mrs. Hale explained, her voice heavy with concern. "I fear I am encroaching upon future funds, but the winter promises to be severe, and our people require aid."

"Oh, Mother, let us do everything we can," Margaret urged, her eagerness masking the profound sadness she felt. She clung to the notion that this assistance would be their final act together; "We may not remain here long."

"Do you feel unwell, my darling?" Mrs. Hale asked, her anxiety rising as she misinterpreted Margaret's melancholy. "You look pale and exhausted. Is it this soft, damp, unhealthy air?"

"No, Mother, it is not that. The air is delightful; it carries the scent of purity, a stark contrast to the choking smoke of Harley Street. However, I am weary; surely it is nearly bedtime."

"Not far off—it is half-past nine. You should retire immediately. Ask Dixon for some gruel. I shall check on you once you are settled. I fear you have caught a chill, or perhaps breathed in the miasma from those stagnant ponds—"

"Oh, Mother," Margaret murmured, offering a faint smile as she kissed her cheek, "I am perfectly well. Do not trouble yourself; I am simply tired."

Retiring upstairs, Margaret complied with her mother's request, drinking the bland gruel to ease her conscience. Languidly propped against her pillows, she waited until Mrs. Hale ascended to lock her own door. The moment the latch clicked, Margaret sprang from the bed, draped her dressing gown around her shoulders, and paced the floor until the creaking of the wood warned her to cease. She collapsed onto the window seat in the small, recessed bay. Earlier that morning, the sight of the church tower glowing brightly under a clear sky had filled her with hope, promising a sunlit day. Now, sixteen hours later, she sat paralyzed by a grief so deep it stifled tears, replaced instead by a cold, dull ache that seemed to drain the vitality from her very soul.

Mr. Henry Lennox's visit, his generous offer, felt like a distant dream, severed from her reality. The harsh truth remained: her father had allowed doubt to fester until he became a schismatic, an outcast. All the upheaval of their lives revolved around this single, devastating fact.

Gazing out at the dark grey silhouettes of the church towers, sharp and square against the deep, transparent blue of the night sky, Margaret felt a terrifying emptiness. She stared endlessly into the distance, seeking a sign of God, finding none. To her, the earth seemed more desolate enclosed by an iron dome than exposed to the infinite void. Those endless spaces of serene darkness mocked her more cruelly than any physical barrier, shutting out the cries of suffering humanity. Perhaps those voices would ascend to the divine throne only to vanish forever, lost in the vastness before reaching Him.

In this mood, her father entered silently. The moonlight revealed his daughter in her solitary vigil. He approached and gently touched her shoulder.

"Margaret, I heard you were awake. I could not resist coming in to ask you to pray with me—to recite the Lord's Prayer. It would benefit us both."

Mr. Hale and Margaret knelt by the window. He looked upward; she bowed her head in humble shame. God was present, close and listening to her father's whispered words. Even if her father was a heretic, had not she, moments prior, displayed a skepticism equally absolute? She said nothing, retreating to her bed like a child fleeing from a perceived fault. If the world presented insurmountable puzzles, she trusted that God would reveal the necessary step for the hour. Yet, the memory of Mr. Lennox haunted her dreams. He climbed a mythical tree to retrieve her bonnet, fell, and she struggled in vain to save him, held back by an invisible force. Then, the scene shifted: she was back in the Harley Street drawing room, conversing with him as of old, yet perpetually aware that he had already perished in that fatal fall.

It was a miserable, restless night, a poor preparation for the dawn. She woke abruptly, unrefreshed, sensing a reality far more painful than her feverish visions. The sorrow returned, compounded by a terrible dissonance. How far had her father wandered, led astray by doubts that appeared to her as temptations from evil? She longed to ask, yet dreaded the answer.

Morning broke crisp and clear, lifting Mrs. Hale's spirits at breakfast. She chattered happily about village charities, ignoring her husband's silence and Margaret's monosyllabic responses. Before the meal concluded, Mr. Hale rose, leaning heavily on the table.

"I shall not return until evening. I am going to Bracy Common to speak with Farmer Dobson regarding dinner. I expect to be back for tea at seven."

He avoided looking at either woman, but Margaret understood the subtext. By seven, the announcement must be delivered. Mr. Hale might have hesitated, but Margaret possessed a different constitution. She could not endure the mounting dread weighing on her mind all day; it was better to confront the worst immediately. The day was too short to console her mother with false hopes. Standing by the window, calculating how to begin, she waited for the servants to vacate the room. Just then, Mrs. Hale descended the stairs, dressed briskly for school, her mood unusually energetic.

"Mother, please join me in the garden this morning. Just one walk," Margaret suggested, linking her arm through her mother's waist.

They stepped through the open window. Mrs. Hale spoke, but Margaret could not decipher her words. Her gaze caught a bee diving into a deep bell-shaped flower. When the insect emerged laden with pollen, that would be the signal. It flew out.

"Mother!" Margaret blurted out, unable to contain the news. "Father is leaving Helstone! He is abandoning the Church and moving to Milton-Northern."

There lay the three crushing facts, barely articulated.

"What leads you to say that?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice trembling with surprise and disbelief. "Who has been spreading such nonsense?"

"Father himself," Margaret replied, wishing she could offer gentleness but lacking the words. They reached a garden bench. Mrs. Hale sat and began to weep.

"I do not understand," she choked out. "Either you have made a grave error, or I fail to grasp your meaning."

"No, Mother, I have made no mistake. Father wrote to the Bishop stating that his doubts prevent him from remaining a priest of the Church of England. He must resign Helstone. He has also consulted Mr. Bell—Frederick's godfather—and it is agreed we move to Milton-Northern." Throughout her explanation, Mrs. Hale watched her daughter's face; the shadow there confirmed her belief in the truth of the words.

"I cannot believe it," Mrs. Hale finally whispered. "He would surely have told me before it came to this."

A sudden pang of guilt struck Margaret. Her mother should have been informed earlier. Regardless of her own complaints, it was a failure on her father's part to allow his daughter to discover his spiritual crisis and impending exile through her own superior understanding. Margaret sat beside her mother, cradling her head against her breast, pressing her soft cheek tenderly against her mother's.

"Dearest, darling Mother! We feared causing you pain. Father suffered acutely. You know you are not robust, and the suspense must have been agonizing."

"When did he tell you, Margaret?"

"Only yesterday," Margaret replied, sensing the underlying jealousy in the question. "Poor Father!" She attempted to steer her mother toward compassion for her father's struggle. Mrs. Hale lifted her head.

"What does he mean by 'doubts'?" she asked. "Does he not mean he believes differently—that he knows better than the Church?"

Margaret shook her head, tears filling her eyes as her mother touched the raw nerve of her own regret.

"Cannot the Bishop correct him?" Mrs. Hale asked, half-impatient.

"I fear not," Margaret said softly. "But I did not inquire. I could not bear to hear his response. Nevertheless, it is settled. He departs in two weeks. I am unsure if he has not already submitted his formal resignation."

"In two weeks!" Mrs. Hale exclaimed, tears flowing freely now. "This is strange, improper! I call it unforgivable. He has doubts and abandons his living without consulting me. Surely, if he had confided in me initially, I could have quelled them

"Unwatch'd the garden bough shall sway,
The tender blossom flutter down;
Unloved that beech will gather brown,
The maple burn itself away;
Unloved, the sun-flower, shining fair,
Ray round with flames her disk of seed,
And many a rose-carnation feed
With summer spice the humming air;

The final day had arrived. The house was a hive of activity, filled with packing cases being loaded onto carts at the front door, destined for the nearest railway station. Even the once-pristine lawn beside the house had become untidy and disfigured by straw drifting in through the open doors and windows. Inside, the rooms possessed a strange, hollow echo, and the sunlight poured in through the uncurtained glass with a harsh, stark intensity, rendering the familiar surroundings suddenly alien.

Mrs. Hale's dressing-room remained the last sanctuary untouched. There, she and Dixon were busily folding clothes, frequently pausing to exclaim over forgotten treasures—relics of the children from their younger years—and handling them with affectionate reverence. Their progress was slow, weighed down by nostalgia.

Downstairs, Margaret stood calm and composed, offering counsel to the men hired to assist the cook and Charlotte. These two women, weeping intermittently, marveled at how the young lady could maintain such composure on this last day, concluding privately that she simply cared little for Helstone after spending so long in London. Indeed, Margaret stood there, pale and silent, her large, grave eyes observing every minute detail of the departure. To them, it was incomprehensible that her heart should ache with such a crushing weight, impossible to lift with a sigh, and that constant mental exertion was the only thing preventing her from screaming in pain. Furthermore, if she were to break down, who would remain strong enough to manage the situation? Her father was deep in the vestry, sorting through papers, books, and registers with the clerk; when he emerged, he would need to pack his own personal effects, a task requiring his specific attention. Besides, was Margaret the type to yield to strangers, or even to household friends like the cook and Charlotte? Certainly not.

Finally, the four packers retreated to the kitchen for tea. Margaret moved stiffly and slowly away from her post in the hall, passing through the bare, echoing drawing-room and stepping out into the twilight of an early November evening. A thin, dull mist veiled the world, softening outlines but failing to hide them, casting everything in a lilac hue as the sun lingered on the horizon. A robin sang—a bird, Margaret mused, perhaps the same "winter pet" her father had often described, for whom he had crafted a special house near his study window.

The leaves were more resplendent than ever; the first touch of frost would soon lay them low. Already, one or two drifted down, glowing amber and gold in the low, slanting rays of the setting sun. Margaret walked along the path beneath the pear-tree wall. She had not traversed this spot since walking beside Henry Lennox. Here, at this patch of thyme, he had begun to speak of the thoughts she must suppress. Her eyes had fixed on a late-blooming rose as she attempted to reply, and she had caught the image of the feathery carrot leaves in the midst of his final sentence. Only a fortnight ago! And everything had changed so drastically. Where was he now? In London, navigating the same routine, dining with the established circles of Harley Street or with his lively young associates.

Even as she walked sadly through the damp, dreary garden in the gloom, surrounded by falling and fading foliage turning to decay, he might be joyfully closing his lawbooks after a satisfying day of work. He would likely refresh himself with a run in the Temple Gardens, absorbing the grand, inarticulate roar of tens of thousands of busy men nearby yet unseen, catching fleeting glimpses of the city lights rising from the river depths. He had often spoken to Margaret of these hurried walks, snatched between study and dinner. At his finest moments, he had described them with such allure that the thought had captivated her imagination.

But here, there was no sound. The robin had fled into the vast stillness of night. Occasionally, a cottage door in the distance clicked shut, admitting a weary laborer to his home, sounding impossibly far away. A stealthy, crunching sound among the crisp fallen leaves of the woods beyond the garden seemed almost immediate. Margaret recognized it: a poacher. Sitting in her bedroom earlier that autumn, candle extinguished, she had often watched the silent leaps of poachers over the garden fence, their quick tramps across the dewy lawn, their vanishing into the black shadows. The wild, adventurous freedom of their lives had fascinated her; she had wished them success and harbored no fear. But tonight, inexplicably, she was afraid.

She heard Charlotte closing the windows and securing the house for the night, unaware that someone had ventured into the garden. Suddenly, a small branch—perhaps rotted, perhaps snapped by force—crashed heavily down in the nearest part of the forest. Margaret reacted instantly, running swiftly toward the window and tapping on the glass with a hurried, trembling urgency that startled Charlotte inside.

"Let me in! Let me in! It is only me, Charlotte!"

Her heart continued to flutter until she was safely back in the drawing-room, the windows barred and bolted, the familiar walls enclosing her against the unknown. She sank onto a packing case. Cheerless and cold was the dismantled room; there was no fire, no other light save Charlotte's long, unsnuffed candle. Charlotte stared at Margaret in surprise, and Margaret, sensing the gaze rather than clearly seeing it, rose.

"I was afraid you were shutting me out altogether, Charlotte," she said, offering a faint smile. "Then you would never have heard me in the kitchen, and the doors to the lane and churchyard have been locked for hours."

"Oh, miss, I would surely have noticed your absence soon. The men would have needed your directions. And I've put tea in Master's study; it's the most comfortable room, so to speak."

"Thank you, Charlotte. You are a kind girl. I shall be sorry to leave you. Please try to write to me if I can ever offer you any help or advice. I shall always welcome a letter from Helstone. I will certainly send you my address once I know it."

The study was prepared for tea, with a roaring fire and unlit candles on the table. Margaret sat on the rug, partly to warm herself against the damp chill clinging to her dress and the fatigue weighing on her bones. She balanced herself by clasping her hands tightly around her knees; her head dipped slightly toward her chest, an posture of desolation regardless of her inner turmoil.

However, upon hearing her father's footsteps on the gravel outside, she sprang up. Hastily smoothing back her heavy black hair and wiping away tears that had gathered on her cheeks without her conscious knowledge, she went to open the door.

He displayed far more depression than she did. She struggled to engage him in conversation, forcing herself to speak of topics she hoped would interest him, knowing each attempt required a monumental effort she feared might be her last.

"Have you had a very long walk today?" she asked, noting his refusal to touch any food.

"As far as Fordham Beeches. I went to see Widow Maltby; she is deeply grieved that we did not say goodbye properly. She says little Susan has been keeping watch down the lane for days past."

"Nay, Margaret, what is the matter, dear?"

The thought of the little child waiting endlessly, repeatedly disappointed—not due to any neglect on her part, but because of her absolute inability to leave home—was the final drop in Margaret's cup. She broke down sobbing as if her heart would shatter.

Mr. Hale was profoundly distressed. He rose and paced nervously up and down the room. Margaret tried to compose herself, remaining silent until she could speak with steady resolve. She heard him muttering to himself.

"I cannot bear it. I cannot bear to witness the suffering of others. I think I could endure my own trials with patience. Oh, is there no way to go back?"

"No, father," Margaret replied, meeting his gaze directly, her voice low and firm. "It is painful to believe you in error. It would be infinitely worse to have known you to be a hypocrite."

She lowered her voice at the end, as if the mere suggestion of hypocrisy regarding her father tasted of irreverence.

"Besides," she continued, "I am merely exhausted tonight. Do not think I am suffering because of what you have done, dear papa. We cannot discuss it tonight, I believe." Finding that tears and sobs threatened to overwhelm her again, she added, "I had better go and bring Mother up this cup of tea. She had hers very early when I was too busy to attend to her, and I am certain she would be glad of another now."

Railroad schedules inexorably tore them away from lovely, beloved Helstone the following morning. They departed; they had witnessed the last view of the long, low parsonage home

"Mist clogs the sunshine, / Smoky dwarf houses / Hem me round everywhere." — Matthew Arnold.

The following afternoon, approximately twenty miles north of Milton, the carriage turned onto a narrow branch line leading toward Heston. Heston itself stretched along the coast as one long, straggling street, possessing a distinct character that set it apart from the southern English bathing resorts and those on the European continent. Using a Scottish term, everything possessed a "purpose-like" quality. The country carts were built with more iron and less wood or leather; the pedestrians, though ostensibly on holiday, moved with a busy, industrious mindset. The colors were grayer, more enduring, lacking the fleeting gaiety found elsewhere. Notably absent were the smock-frocks common among rural folk in the south; deemed impediments to movement and prone to snagging on machinery, they had fallen out of fashion. In southern towns, Margaret observed, shopkeepers would lounge idly at their doors when not working, enjoying the breeze. Here, however, even moments of leisure were spent engaging in commerce within the shops—unnecessarily unrolling and re-rolling ribbons.

These contrasts struck Margaret deeply as she and her mother sought lodgings the next morning. Having already incurred costs exceeding Mr. Hale's expectations at local hotels, they gratefully accepted the first clean, cheerful rooms available. For the first time in days, Margaret felt a profound sense of rest, accompanied by a dreamy luxuriance. The distant sea lapped the sands with a rhythmic sound; nearby, donkey boys cried out; unusual scenes drifted before her like paintings she cared not to analyze before they vanished. A stroll to the beach revealed a soft, warm sea-air despite the late November date, with a vast, misty horizon touching a tender-colored sky and the white sails of distant boats glinting silver in pale sunlight. It seemed she could dream her life away in such a state of pensiveness, making the present all-encompassing, refusing to dwell on the past or fear the future. Yet, the future awaited, however stern.

One evening, plans were set for Margaret and her father to visit Milton-Northern the next day to secure housing. Mr. Hale had received correspondence from both Mr. Bell and Mr. Thornton and was eager to clarify his prospects through an interview with the latter. Though Margaret harbored a repugnance toward manufacturing towns and hoped her mother's health benefited from the Heston air, duty prevailed.

Approaching Milton, a deep leaden cloud hung over the horizon, darkened against the pale winter sky—a sign of frost that had already touched Heston. Closer to the town, the air carried a faint taste of smoke, a loss of herbal fragrance rather than a pungent odor. They were whisked rapidly through long, straight, seemingly endless rows of uniformly built brick houses. Occasionally, a massive, multi-windowed factory loomed like a hen among chicks, belching black, "unparliamentary" smoke that accounted for the ominous cloud. Heavy-laden lorries frequently blocked the thoroughfares between the station and the hotel. While Margaret had driven into cities before, the vehicles here differed; every wagon bore cotton, either in raw burlap sacks or woven bales of calico. The populace thronged the sidewalks, dressed in materials that suggested wealth but worn with a slovenly looseness that struck Margaret as distinct from the shabby smartness of London's lower classes.

"New Street," Mr. Hale noted. "I believe this is the principal artery. Bell mentioned that the opening of this street from a lane into a major thoroughfare thirty years ago significantly increased his property's value. Mr. Thornton's mill must be nearby, as he rents from Bell, though I suspect his operations center around his warehouse."

"Where is our hotel, Papa?" Margaret asked.

"Near the end of this street. Shall we eat lunch before or after viewing the houses listed in the *Milton Times*?"

"Oh, let us finish our business first."

"Agreed. I will check for any notes from Mr. Thornton, who promised to inform me regarding suitable properties, and then we shall depart. We will keep the cab; it is safer than wandering and risking missing the afternoon train."

Finding no letters, they proceeded to house-hunting. With a budget of thirty pounds per year, they could have secured a spacious home with a garden in Hampshire. In Milton, however, finding even basic accommodations—two sitting rooms and four bedrooms—proved elusive. After visiting numerous options and rejecting each, they exchanged looks of dismay.

"We must settle for the second option," Mr. Hale decided. "That one in Crampton, isn't it? It had three sitting rooms. Do you recall our laughter at the disparity with only three bedrooms? Nevertheless, I have devised a plan. The downstairs front room shall serve as your study and our dining room. Mamma deserves the cheeriest sitting room possible upstairs. That room with the atrocious blue-and-pink paper and heavy cornice actually offers a fine view over the plain, bending down toward the river or canal. You may take the small bedroom behind, in the projection above the kitchen. You and Mamma can share the room behind the drawing room, and that closet in the roof will make a splendid dressing room for you."

"But what of Dixon and the maid we intend to hire?"

"Wait, Father. I am overwhelmed by my own genius for management. Dixon gets the rear sitting room; she complains so bitterly of the stairs at Heston. The maid takes the sloping attic above yours and Mamma's. Does that suffice?"

"I suppose it will. However, the wallpaper... What taste! And the excessive color and heavy cornices!"

"Never mind, Papa! Surely you can persuade the landlord to re-paper the drawing room and your bedroom, since Mamma will frequent them most. Your bookshelves will conceal much of the gaudy pattern in the dining room."

"Do you truly consider it the best choice? If so, I shall immediately visit Mr. Donkin, referenced in the advertisement. I will escort you back to the hotel for lunch and rest; by the time the meal is served, I shall return. I trust I can procure new papers."

Margaret shared his hope silently. She had never encountered a taste for ornate decoration that rivaled the plain simplicity she considered the true framework of elegance. Mr. Hale guided her through the hotel entrance, leaving her at the foot of the stairs to await his return. Just as she placed her hand on the door of their sitting room, a brisk waiter approached.

"I beg your pardon, madam. The gentleman departed so swiftly I failed to notify him. Mr. Thornton arrived shortly after you left. Believing you would return within the hour based on his instructions, I informed him. He returned about five minutes ago and requested to wait for Mr. Hale. He is currently in your room, madam."

"Thank you. My father returns momentarily; you may inform him then."

Opening the door, Margaret entered with her characteristic straight, fearless, and dignified demeanor. She felt no awkwardness; accustomed to social proprieties, she intended to extend full courtesy to a business associate of her father. Mr. Thornton, conversely, was considerably surprised and disconcerted. Expecting a quiet, middle-aged clergyman, he found instead a young woman of striking, frank dignity, unlike anyone he typically encountered. Her attire was austere: a high-quality straw bonnet trimmed with white ribbon, draped in a large Indian shawl worn with imperial grandeur. Confused by her appearance, he noted her simple, unabashed gaze, which conveyed that his presence held no significance to her beautiful, pale countenance, eliciting no flush of surprise. He recalled hearing of Mr. Hale's daughter but had assumed she was a child.

"Mr. Thornton, I presume!" Margaret said after a brief pause, allowing his hesitation to pass. "Please, sit down. My father brought me to the door only a moment ago but was unaware of your arrival and has stepped out briefly on business. He will return instantly. I apologize for the inconvenience of your double visit."

Though accustomed to authority, Mr. Thornton found himself immediately under her subtle command. Previously impatient due to lost time on a market day, he now calmly seated himself at her bidding.

"Do you happen to know where Mr. Hale has gone? Perhaps I might locate him."

"He has gone to Mr. Donkin in Canute Street. He is the landlord of the house my father intends to rent in Crampton."

Mr. Thornton recognized the house. He had viewed it at Mr. Bell's request to assist Mr. Hale, partly motivated by sympathy for a clergyman displaced under difficult circumstances. Initially, he had deemed the Crampton residence ideal. Now, observing Margaret's superior bearing and movements, he felt a sudden shame for having previously considered it adequate for the Hales, despite noting a certain vulgarity that had struck him earlier. Margaret could not suppress her innate poise, yet features like her short, curled upper lip, round massive chin, and the way she carried her head imparted an impression of haughtiness to strangers. Exhausted, she preferred silence, yet felt compelled to maintain gentleness toward this stranger, who appeared somewhat rough after his exposure to Milton's streets and crowds. She wished he would leave, as he had once threatened, rather than sit there responding to her remarks with curt brevity. Removing her shawl and draping it over the chair back, she faced him squarely, illuminated by the light. Her full beauty confronted him: her flexible white neck rising from a lithe figure, her lips moving slightly without breaking the cold serenity of her face, her eyes holding

"And it's hame, hame, hame, / Hame fain wad I be."

It required the delicate, floral wallpaper of the rooms merely to begin to reconcile Mrs. Hale to Milton. But that was insufficient; it demanded more, and far more than could be supplied. Thick, yellow November fogs had descended upon the valley, obscuring the sweeping bend of the river and sealing off the view of the plain entirely when Mrs. Hale finally arrived at her new residence. Although Margaret and Dixon had labored for two days unpacking and rearranging, the interior of the house remained chaotic, and outside, a dense fog pressed against the windows, driven inward through every open doorway in choking, white wreaths of unwholesome mist.

"Oh, Margaret! Are we to live here?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice filled with blank dismay.

Margaret's heart resonated with the dreary tone of the question. She struggled to summon the composure to reply, "Oh, the fogs in London are sometimes far worse!"

"But then you knew that London itself, and our friends, lay behind it. Here—well! We are utterly desolate. Oh, Dixon, what a place this is!"

"Indeed, ma'am, I'm certain it will be your death before long, and then I know who'll—"

"Stay! Miss Hale, that is far too heavy for you to lift," Dixon interrupted hastily.

"Not at all, thank you, Dixon," Margaret replied coolly. "The best thing we can do for Mother is to prepare her room for rest, while I fetch her a cup of coffee."

Mr. Hale was equally dispirited and sought Margaret out for sympathy. "Margaret, I truly believe this is an unhealthy place. Imagine if your mother's health—or yours—should suffer. I wish I had chosen a country retreat in Wales; this is simply terrible," he remarked, moving toward the window.

There was no comfort to offer. They were settled in Milton and must endure the smoke and fogs for the foreseeable future; indeed, all other avenues of life seemed blocked by a thick fog of circumstance. Only the day prior, Mr. Hale had calculated with alarm how much their relocation and the fortnight spent at Heston had cost, discovering it had consumed nearly his entire reserve of ready cash. No! Here they were, and here they must remain. When night fell and Margaret realized this permanence, she felt inclined to collapse into a stupor of despair.

The heavy, smoky air clung to her bedroom, situated in the long, narrow projection at the rear of the house. Its window, positioned on the side of the oblong structure, faced the blank wall of a similar projection barely ten feet away. Through the fog, it loomed like a great barrier to hope. Inside, chaos reigned supreme. Every effort had been directed toward making her mother's room comfortable, leaving hers in disarray. Sitting on a box, Margaret noticed a direction card that seemed to have been written at Helstone—beautiful, beloved Helstone! Lost in dismal reverie, she eventually resolved to distract her mind. Suddenly, she recalled a letter from Edith that she had only partially read amidst the morning's rush.

Edith described their arrival at Corfu, their voyage along the Mediterranean, the music and dancing aboard ship, and the vibrant new life awaiting her. She spoke of a house with a trellised balcony overlooking white cliffs and a deep blue sea. Edith wrote fluently and well, capturing the salient points of scenes while enumerating enough trivial detail for Margaret to visualize them fully. Captain Lennox and another recently married officer shared a villa perched high on precipitous rocks overlooking the water. Even late in the year, their days seemed spent boating or on land picnics; entirely outdoors, pleasure-seeking, and joyful. Edith's existence appeared like a vast vault of blue sky above her, free—utterly free from flecks or clouds. Her husband attended drill, and she, the most musical officer's wife there, copied new and popular tunes from the latest English scores for the bandmaster; these seemed their most arduous duties. She expressed an affectionate hope that, if the regiment stayed another year, Margaret might join them for a long visit. She asked if Margaret remembered the rainy day twelve months ago in Harley Street, when Edith refused to wear her new gown to a dull dinner, getting it wet and splashed on the way to the carriage—a dinner where they had first met Captain Lennox.

Yes! Margaret remembered it vividly. Edith and Mrs. Shaw had dined; Margaret had joined the party later. The memory of the abundant luxury of those arrangements, the stately elegance of the furniture, the spaciousness of the house, and the peaceful, untroubled ease of the visitors surged forth, standing in stark contrast to the present gloom. That smooth sea of their former life closed up, leaving no trace of where they had all been. The habitual dinners, the social calls, the dance evenings—they all continued forever, even though her Aunt Shaw and Edith were no longer there, and she, naturally, was even less missed. She doubted if anyone in that old circle ever thought of her, save perhaps Henry Lennox. He, too, she knew, would strive to forget her due to the pain she had caused him. She had often heard him boast of his ability to banish any disagreeable thought.

Then she pondered further possibilities. Had she loved him as a lover, accepted his proposal, and then witnessed this shift in her father's opinions and their consequent decline in status, she could not doubt that Mr. Lennox would have received it with impatience. It was a bitter mortification in one sense, yet she could bear it patiently, knowing her father's purity of purpose, which strengthened her to endure his errors, however grave she deemed them. However, the fact that the world regarded her father as degraded, judging him in a rough, wholesale manner, would have oppressed and irritated Mr. Lennox. Realizing what *might* have been, she grew grateful for what *was*. They were at rock bottom now; they could not fall lower. Edith's astonishment and her Aunt Shaw's dismay would have to be met bravely when their letters arrived.

Rising slowly, Margaret began to undress, savoring the full luxury of acting leisurely, despite the late hour, after the frantic haste of the day. She fell asleep, hoping for some brightness, either internal or external. But if she had known how long it would be before dawn broke, her heart would have sunk even deeper. The season was most unfavorable for both health and spirits. Her mother contracted a severe cold, and Dixon herself was evidently unwell, though Margaret dared not burden her further than by attempting to nurse her or provide care. They could find no girl to assist; all were employed in the factories. Those who did apply were sharply rebuked by Dixon for presuming that someone of their station could ever be trusted to work in a gentleman's household. Thus, they relied on a charwoman in almost constant employment. Margaret longed to summon Charlotte, but aside from the financial imprudence of keeping a superior servant, the distance was prohibitive.

Mr. Hale secured several pupils recommended by Mr. Bell or through the direct influence of Mr. Thornton. Most were of an age when many boys would still be at school, yet, according to the prevailing—and seemingly well-founded—notions of Milton, a lad must be caught young to become a competent tradesman, acclimatized to the life of the mill, office, or warehouse. Sending a boy to even the Scottish universities rendered him unsettled for commercial pursuits; how much more so if he went to Oxford or Cambridge, where entry was barred until eighteen? Consequently, most manufacturers placed their sons in positions at fourteen or fifteen, ruthlessly pruning any branches leading toward literature or higher intellectual cultivation, hoping to channel the entire vigor of the plant into commerce.

Yet, there were wiser parents. Some possessed the sense to recognize their own deficiencies and strive to correct them. Indeed, there were a few men in the prime of life who held the stern wisdom to admit their ignorance and learn late what they should have mastered early. Mr. Thornton was perhaps the eldest of Mr. Hale's pupils and undoubtedly his favorite. Mr. Hale developed the habit of quoting Thornton's opinions so frequently and with such deference that it became a domestic joke to wonder what time remained for actual study during the allotted hour, as so much seemed devoted to conversation. Margaret encouraged this lighthearted perspective on her father's acquaintance with Mr. Thornton, sensing that her mother viewed this new friendship with jealous suspicion. While Mr. Hale had previously spent his time solely with books and parishioners as at Helstone, appearing indifferent to whether he saw much of his wife, he now looked eagerly forward to renewing his interactions with Mr. Thornton. Now, it seemed to hurt and annoy her as if he were slighting her company for the first time. Mr. Hale's excessive praise had the usual effect on his audience; they were slightly inclined to rebel against Aristides being constantly hailed as "The Just." After more than twenty years of a quiet life in a country parsonage, the energy that effortlessly conquered immense difficulties dazzled Mr. Hale. The power of Milton's machinery and the power of its men impressed him with a sense of grandeur that he accepted without probing the details of its operation.

However, Margaret ventured less among the machinery and the crowds. She observed less of power in its public manifestation and, coincidentally, found herself associated with one or two individuals who, in matters affecting the masses, must be acute sufferers for the sake of the many. The eternal question remains: Has

"Let China's earth, enriched with coloured stains, / Pencil'd with gold, and streaked with azure veins, / The grateful flavour of the Indian leaf, / Or Mocha's sunburnt berry glad receive." —Mrs. Barbauld.

On the day following his encounter with Higgins and his daughter, Mr. Hale ascended the stairs to the small drawing-room at an unusually late hour. He approached various objects within the room, inspecting them with a deliberate air, yet Margaret perceived that this was merely a nervous habit—a tactic to delay speaking words he desired to utter but dreaded to voice. Finally, the matter emerged.

"My dear! I have invited Mr. Thornton to tea tonight."

Mrs. Hale reclined in her easy chair, her eyes closed, wearing an expression of habitual pain. Yet, at her husband's announcement, she stirred into querulousness. "Mr. Thornton—and tonight! What on earth does that man desire to come here for? Besides, Dixon is currently washing my muslins and laces, and there is no soft water amidst these dreadful eastern winds, which I presume we shall endure throughout the year in Milton."

"The wind is shifting, my dear," Mr. Hale replied, gazing out at the smoke drifting directly from the east. Though he did not yet fully grasp the cardinal points, he adjusted them somewhat arbitrarily to suit the moment.

"Don't tell me!" Mrs. Hale shuddered, tightening her shawl further around herself. "Be it east or west, however, I suppose this man must come."

"Oh, Mother, that proves you have never truly observed Mr. Thornton. He possesses the constitution of someone who enjoys battling every adversity life presents—be it enemies, winds, or circumstances. The harder it rains and blows, the more certain we are to find him. However, I shall assist Dixon. I am becoming quite renowned as a clear-starcher. Furthermore, he requires no amusement beyond conversation with Father. Father, I am genuinely eager to witness the Pythias to your Damon. You know I have met him only once, and we were so perplexed regarding what to say to one another that our interaction was far from successful."

"I cannot say that you would ever find him agreeable, Margaret. He is not a 'lady's man.'"

Margaret arched her neck in a gesture of scornful disdain. "I hold no particular admiration for 'lady's men,' Father. Nevertheless, Mr. Thornton visits us as your friend—as one who has recognized your worth."

"The only individual in Milton," Mrs. Hale interjected.

"Therefore, let us extend him a warm welcome, along with some coconut cakes. Dixon would be delighted if we request she prepares them; and I shall undertake to iron your caps, Mother."

Countless times that morning, Margaret had wished Mr. Thornton were miles away. She had envisioned other pursuits: writing a letter to Edith, reading a substantial portion of Dante, or visiting the Higgins household. Instead, she irons away, listening to Dixon's grievances, hoping that an overflow of sympathy might deter her from recounting her misfortunes to Mrs. Hale. Periodically, Margaret felt compelled to recall her father's high regard for Mr. Thornton to suppress the creeping irritation of fatigue, which threatened to trigger one of the severe headaches she had recently begun to suffer. By the time she finally seated herself to inform her mother that she was no longer Peggy the laundry-maid but Margaret Hale, the lady, she could barely speak. She intended the remark as a jest, and was sufficiently chagrined when her mother took it literally.

"Yes! If anyone had informed me, when I was Miss Beresford and one of the country's belles, that a child of mine would be forced to stand for half a day in a cramped kitchen, laboring like any servant to ensure proper preparation for receiving a tradesman—who happens to be the sole—"

"Oh, Mother!" Margaret exclaimed, rising abruptly. "Do not punish me for such a careless slip of the tongue. I have no objection to ironing or any form of work for you and Father. Through it all, I remain a born and bred lady, even if it involves scrubbing floors or washing dishes. I am merely exhausted for the moment; in half an hour, I shall be ready to repeat the process. As for Mr. Thornton's engagement in trade, well, he cannot alter that now, poor fellow. I doubt his education qualifies him for anything else."

With slow deliberation, Margaret rose and retreated to her own room, unable to endure much more.
