Simultaneously, in Mr. Thornton's residence, a contrasting scene unfolded. A large-boned matron, well past middle age, sat working in a stately, grimly furnished dining room. Her features, mirroring her robust frame, were strong and massive rather than heavy. Her countenance shifted slowly between distinct expressions, each equally resolute. While there was little variation in her demeanor, those who beheld her once invariably returned to look again; even passersby in the street often turned their heads to gaze a moment longer at the firm, severe, and dignified woman who neither yielded in public courtesy nor paused from her direct path toward the clearly defined objective she pursued. She was elegantly attired in sturdy black silk, not a single thread of which appeared worn or discolored. She mended a vast tablecloth of the finest texture, holding it up against the light periodically to identify thin spots requiring her delicate attention. No books cluttered the room save for Matthew Henry's Bible Commentaries, six volumes resting centrally on the massive sideboard, flanked by a tea urn on one side and a lamp on the other. In a distant chamber, the sound of piano practice drifted forth. Someone was rehearsing a *morceau de salon*, executing it with rapidity; on average, every third note was either indistinct or entirely omitted, and the final loud chords were frequently false, yet the performer remained entirely satisfied.

Mrs. Thornton heard a step, matching her own in its decisive rhythm, approach the dining-room door. "John! Is that you?"

Her son entered. "What brings you home so early? I believed you were proceeding to tea with that acquaintance of Mr. Bell's, Mr. Hale?"

"So I am, Mother. I have returned to dress."

"Dress! Humph! When I was a maiden, young men were content to dress once daily. Why must you dress specifically to consume a cup of tea with an elderly parson?"

"Mr. Hale is a gentleman, and his wife and daughter are ladies."

"Wife and daughter! Do they instruct? What are their occupations? You have never mentioned them."

"No, Mother, because I have never encountered Mrs. Hale; I have seen Miss Hale for merely half an hour."

"Beware that you do not fall prey to a penniless girl, John."

"I am not easily ensnared, Mother, as I trust you are aware. Moreover, I must object to Miss Hale being spoken of in that manner, which you know is offensive to me. I have never been aware of any young lady attempting to capture me, nor do I believe anyone has ever bestowed such futile effort upon it."

Mrs. Thornton refused to concede the point to her son, possessing sufficient pride for her sex. "Very well! I merely caution you. Perhaps our Milton maidens possess too much spirit and sensibility to engage in fishing for husbands; yet this Miss Hale hails from the aristocratic counties, where, if rumors are to be believed, wealthy spouses are considered prizes."

Mr. Thornton's brow furrowed, and he advanced a step into the room. "Mother"—accompanied by a brief, scornful laugh—"you will force me to confess. The sole occasion I witnessed Miss Hale, she treated me with a haughty civility steeped in a strong flavor of contempt. She held herself aloof as if she were a queen and I her humble, unwashed vassal. Rest assured, Mother."

"Not at all! Neither am I at ease nor content. What business did she, a renegade clergyman's daughter, have to turn her nose up at you! I would not dress for any of them—a saucy lot! Were I in your place."

As he departed the room, he added, "Mr. Hale is good, gentle, and learned. He is not saucy. Regarding Mrs. Hale, I shall describe her appearance tonight, should you wish to hear." He closed the door and vanished.

Left alone, Mrs. Thornton fumed. "Despise my son! Treat him as her vassal, indeed! Humph! I wonder where she could possibly find another such specimen! Whether boy or man, he is the noblest, stoutest heart I have ever known. It matters not that I am his mother; I can discern truth and remain unblinded. I know what Fanny is, and I know what John is. To despise him! I loathe her!"

"We are the trees whom shaking fastens more." — George Herbert.

Mr. Thornton departed the house without entering the dining-room a second time. Being somewhat late, he hurried briskly toward the Hales' residence at Crampton. He was acutely conscious of the need to honor his new acquaintance; any display of disrespect born of tardiness would be unforgivable. As he stood at the gate, waiting for Dixon's inevitable slowness—a delay compounded by her reluctance to answer the bell—he heard the church clock strike half-past seven.

Dixon eventually opened the door, ushering him into a modest drawing-room where Mr. Hale received him with warmth. His host guided him to his wife, whose pallid countenance and shawl-clad silhouette offered a silent apology for her cool reception. Margaret was in the process of lighting the lamp as Mr. Thornton entered, the twilight already deepening outside. The flame cast a gentle glow across the center of the dim room, a space defined by rural habits that left the starry sky visible through the windows, blending the interior with the vast, outer darkness.

To Mr. Thornton, this room presented a stark contrast to the one he had just left. The latter was handsome and ponderous, devoid of any trace of feminine presence save for his mother seated therein, serving primarily as a hall for consumption rather than comfort. This room, however, was vastly finer—perhaps twenty times so—though lacking in physical comfort. There were no mirrors, not even a fragment of glass to catch and multiply the light, nor any gilding. Instead, it possessed a warm, sober breadth of color, beautifully accented by the faded, cherished chintz curtains and slipcovers of old Helstone. An open davenport sat by the window opposite the door; elsewhere stood a pedestal holding a tall white china vase, from which cascaded wreaths of English ivy, pale green birch, and copper-toned beech leaves. Baskets of needlework dotted the corners, while books lay scattered on a table, seemingly abandoned mid-read. Behind the door, another table was dressed for tea, draped in a crisp white cloth and laden with coconut cakes, a basket overflowing with oranges and ruddy American apples resting atop fresh leaves.

Mr. Thornton perceived that these graceful attentions were the habit of the household, particularly of Margaret. She stood beside the tea table in a light muslin gown touched with pink. Though she appeared oblivious to the conversation, her sole focus was the arrangement of the teacups, her round ivory hands moving with a dainty, noiseless precision. A bracelet adorned one slender arm, threatening to slide down over her rounded wrist. Mr. Thornton found himself watching the removal of this "troublesome" ornament with greater intensity than he paid heed to his host's words. It seemed to captivate him to observe her pushing it up impatiently until it constricted her soft flesh, followed immediately by the loosening and its subsequent fall. He nearly cried out, "There it goes again!"

With the preparations for tea complete, there was little left to occupy him, and he found himself regretting that the necessity of eating and drinking would soon interrupt his observation of Margaret. She served him his tea with the dignified air of an unwilling servant, yet her eyes betrayed her anticipation the moment he signaled readiness for another cup. He almost wished to request that she perform for him the task she was compelled to perform for her father, who currently held her little finger and thumb in his broad hand, forcing them to function as sugar tongs. Mr. Thornton witnessed Margaret lift her beautiful eyes to her father, filled with a mixture of light, laughter, and love, as this silent pantomime unfolded between them, unnoticed by either party.

Though Margaret's head still throbbed, evidenced by her pale complexion and silence, she resolved to bridge any awkward pauses rather than allow her father's guest to feel neglected. However, the conversation flowed uninterrupted. Once the tea service was cleared, Margaret retreated to a corner near her mother with her embroidery, allowing her thoughts to wander freely.

Mr. Thornton and Mr. Hale remained deeply engrossed in a topic begun during their previous meeting. Margaret was abruptly pulled back to the present by a trivial remark from her mother. Looking up from her work, she was struck by the profound divergence in appearance—and thus nature—between her father and Mr. Thornton. Her father was slight of build, appearing taller than he actually was when not juxtaposed with the tall, massive frame of the other man. The lines on his face were soft and wavering, traversed by a constant, tremulous movement that revealed every shifting emotion. His eyelids were large and arched, lending his eyes a languid, almost feminine beauty, while his finely arched brows were raised high by the weight of those dreamy lids.

In contrast, Mr. Thornton's brow was straight and fell low over clear, deep-set, earnest eyes. These eyes, though not sharply cutting, seemed intent on penetrating the very core of his subject. The lines on his face were few but firm, carved as if in marble, concentrated chiefly around lips that were slightly compressed. Beneath them lay teeth of such flawless whiteness that they suggested a sudden burst of sunlight. When a rare, bright smile flashed forth from his eyes, transforming his entire demeanor from severe resolution to keen, honest enjoyment, it was a joyfulness displayed with a fearless immediacy usually reserved for children. Margaret admired this smile; it was the first feature she had found attractive in her father's new friend. The opposition of their characters, evident in these physical details, seemed to illuminate the mutual attraction they clearly shared.

She quietly rearranged her mother's woolwork and sank into her own reverie, effectively forgetting her presence in the room entirely. Mr. Thornton was so thoroughly occupied explaining to Mr. Hale the majestic power yet delicate calibration of the steam-hammer—that it could stretch from earth to sky, filling the horizon, and then instantly compress into a vessel small enough for a child to carry—that he ignored her completely. He recounted how this realization of power emerged from a single man's mind in their town, capable of ascending step by step to ever-greater marvels. He declared his belief that many in their midst could step forward to sustain the struggle compelling all material power to bow to science.

"This imagination of power, this practical realization of a gigantic thought, sprang from one man's brain in our good town," Mr. Thornton asserted. "That very man possesses the capacity to climb, step by step, upon each wonder achieved, reaching higher marvels still. And I wager we have many among us who, were he gone, could spring into the breach and carry on the war that demands all material power yield to science."

"Your boast recalls the old lines: 'I've a hundred captains in England,' he said, 'As good as ever was he.'"

At his father's citation, Margaret looked up with inquisitive wonder. How had they drifted from cogwheels to *Chevy Chase*?

"It is no boast of mine," Mr. Thornton replied. "It is plain matter-of-fact. I will not deny that I am proud to belong to a town—or perhaps better, a district—whose necessities give birth to such grandeur of conception. I would sooner be a man toiling, suffering—even failing and unsuccessful—here, than lead a dull, prosperous life in the old, worn grooves of what you call more aristocratic society down in the South, with its slow days of careless ease. One may become clogged with honey and lose the ability to rise and fly."

"You are mistaken," Margaret interjected, stirred by the slight against her beloved South into a fervent defense that flushed her cheeks and brought angry tears to her eyes. "You know nothing of the South. If there is less adventure or less progress—if I must say less excitement—due to the gambling spirit of trade required to force out these wonders, there is also less suffering. I see men here walking the streets who look crushed by some pinching sorrow or care—not merely sufferers, but haters. In the South, we have our poor, but there is not that terrible expression of sullen injustice on their faces that I see here. You do not know the South, Mr. Thornton," she concluded, collapsing into a determined silence, furious with herself for speaking so freely.

"And may I say you do not know the North?" he asked, his tone possessing an inexpressible gentleness as he sensed he had wounded her.

She remained resolutely silent, yearning passionately for the lovely haunts she had left behind in Hampshire, feeling her voice would tremble if she attempted to speak.

"At any rate, Mr. Thornton," Mrs. Hale intervened, "you will admit that Milton is a much smokier, dirtier town than you will ever encounter in the South."

"I'm afraid I must concede its cleanliness," Mr. Thornton replied, flashing a quick, gleaming smile. "But we are commanded by Parliament to burn our own smoke; so I suppose, like good little children, we shall obey—eventually."

"But I recall you telling me you had altered your chimneys to consume the smoke, did you not?" Mr. Hale asked.

"My alterations were made by my own volition before Parliament interfered," Mr. Thornton explained. "It was an immediate expense, but it repaid itself in coal savings. I am uncertain whether I would have acted had I waited for the Act. At least, I would have waited to be informed against and fined, creating all the legal trouble possible. But all laws relying on informants and fines become inert due to the odiousness of their machinery. I doubt a single chimney in Milton has been reported in the last five years, despite some constantly emitting one-third

"There's iron, they say, in all our blood, And a grain or two perhaps is good; But his, he makes me harshly feel, Has got a little too much of steel." — *Anon.*

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out as he returned from escorting his guest downstairs. "I couldn't help watching your face with genuine anxiety when Mr. Thornton confessed to having once been a shop-boy. I already knew the truth from Mr. Bell, so I was fully prepared for what was coming; yet, I half expected you to rise abruptly and flee the room."

"Oh, Papa! Do you truly think I am so foolish?" Margaret protested, her tone lightening instantly. "In fact, I preferred that admission above anything else he said. Every other detail repelled me due to its sheer hardness. Yet, when he spoke of himself, it was with such simplicity—and so little of the artificiality that often defines the vulgarity of shopkeepers—that I felt less urge to leave than when he boasted of Milton as if no other place existed, or when he casually dismissed people for their careless improvidence without ever considering it his duty to offer them guidance or the training his mother had given him. No! His revelation of his humble origins was, by far, the most endearing part of his story."

"I am surprised at you, Margaret," her mother remarked gently. "You who have always accused others of being 'shoppy' at Helstone! I don't think, Mr. Hale, you did quite right in introducing someone with such a background without warning us. I was genuinely terrified of displaying my shock at certain parts of his speech. What about his father 'dying in miserable circumstances'? It sounds as though he might have ended up in the workhouse."

"I cannot be certain it wasn't worse than the workhouse," her husband replied gravely. "Before we arrived, Mr. Bell shared considerable details about his earlier life. Since he has recounted a portion, allow me to complete the picture. His father engaged in wild speculation, failed catastrophically, and subsequently committed suicide, unable to bear the disgrace. All his former acquaintances recoiled from the revelations regarding his dishonest gambling—desperate, futile struggles using other people's money to recover his modest fortune. No one stepped forward to aid the mother and this boy. There was another child, a girl, too young to earn a living, who naturally had to be supported. Initially, no friends came forward, and Mrs. Thornton, I suspect, is not the type to wait for belated kindness to discover her plight. Consequently, they abandoned Milton. I knew he had entered a shop, and that his wages, combined with a small inheritance secured for his mother, sustained them for a long period. Mr. Bell mentioned they literally subsisted on water-porridge for years—the method remained unknown to him. Long after creditors had despaired of recovering any funds from Old Mr. Thornton's debts—if they ever truly harbored hope following the suicide—young Mr. Thornton returned to Milton. He quietly visited each creditor, paying the first installment owed. There was no clamor, no assembly of debtors; it was executed with profound silence and quietude, yet eventually, everything was settled. This was aided significantly by the circumstance of one particularly crusty creditor, whom Mr. Bell described, accepting Mr. Thornton as a sort of partner."

"That is truly admirable," Margaret observed. "It is a pity such a noble nature should be tarnished by his status as a Milton manufacturer."

"Tarnished?" her father queried.

"Oh, Papa, by judging everything solely through the lens of wealth. When he discussed mechanical powers, he clearly viewed them merely as novel methods to expand trade and generate profit. Furthermore, the impoverished men surrounding him—they were poor because they were vicious, excluded from his sympathies because they lacked his 'iron nature' and the capabilities it bestowed for accumulating riches."

"Not vicious; those were not his words," her father corrected. "He used the terms 'improvident' and 'self-indulgent.'"

Meanwhile, Margaret began gathering her mother's sewing materials, preparing to retire. Just as she was about to depart, she paused. She felt compelled to make an acknowledgment that would please her father, yet one that, to be entirely truthful, must also admit a degree of personal discomfort. Nevertheless, she voiced it.

"Papa, I consider Mr. Thornton a remarkably extraordinary man; however, on a personal level, I do not like him at all."

"And I do!" her father laughed warmly. "Personally, as you phrase it, and all. I do not elevate him to the pedestal of a hero or anything similar. Good night, child. Your mother appears exceedingly fatigued tonight, Margaret."

Margaret had noted her mother's worn-out appearance with growing concern for some time. Her father's comment sent her upstairs with a vague dread settling heavily upon her heart. Life in Milton differed so drastically from what Mrs. Hale had known in Helstone, both within the home and outdoors. In Helstone, they were perpetually immersed in fresh, invigorating air; here, the atmosphere seemed devoid of any revitalizing quality. Domestic anxieties pressed upon every member of the family with such intensity and in such a sordid, unfamiliar guise that there was legitimate cause to fear her mother's health was deteriorating seriously.

Several other indicators suggested something was profoundly wrong. Mrs. Hale and Dixon frequently engaged in secretive consultations in the bedroom, emerging with Dixon crying and irritable—a reaction she displayed whenever her mistress's distress demanded her sympathy. On one occasion, shortly after Dixon departed, Margaret entered the chamber and found her mother kneeling. As she slipped away unnoticed, she overheard fragments of what appeared to be a prayer for strength and patience to endure severe physical suffering.

Margaret yearned to restore the bond of intimate confidence severed by her prolonged absence at Aunt Shaw's. She endeavored to win her mother's trust through gentle touches and soothing words, striving to occupy the warmest corner of her mother's heart. Though she received affection and tender words in abundance—sufficient to have delighted her previously—she sensed a secret being withheld, one she believed pertained critically to her mother's health.

She lay awake for a significant portion of the night, devising plans to mitigate the detrimental influence of their Milton existence on her mother. She considered hiring a servant to assist Dixon permanently, thereby freeing her to dedicate her entire time to seeking relief for her mother, ensuring she received the constant personal attention she had grown accustomed to throughout her life.

For several days, visiting registration offices and encountering a myriad of improbable individuals, with very few appearing remotely suitable, consumed Margaret's time and thoughts. One afternoon, she encountered Bessy Higgins in the street and halted to speak with her.

"Well, Bessy, how are you? I hope you're feeling better now that the wind has shifted."

"Better and not better, if you understand what that implies," Bessy replied.

"Not exactly," Margaret smiled. "I'm better in that I am no longer torn apart by coughing fits at night, but I am weary and exhausted by Milton, longing to escape to the Land of Beulah. Yet, as I realize I drift further and further away, my heart sinks, and I am no better; indeed, I am worse."

Margaret turned to walk beside the girl as she progressed feebly toward home. For a moment or two, neither spoke. Finally, Margaret whispered softly, "Bessy, do you wish to die?"

For she, too, shrank from death, holding onto life with the natural fervor of youth and health. Bessy remained silent for a similar duration. Then she answered, "If you had lived the life I have endured, become as weary of it as I am, and occasionally thought, 'perhaps it will last fifty or sixty years—it does for some,' and felt dizzy and disoriented as each of those sixty years spun around me, mocking me with the length of its hours and minutes, and its endless fragments of time... oh, wench! I tell you, you would be grateful enough when the doctor declared he feared you would never see another winter."

"Why, Bessy, what sort of life has yours been?"

"Nought worse than many others', I reckon. Only I fretted against it, whereas they did not."

"But what was it? You know, I am a stranger here, so perhaps I am not as quick to grasp your meaning as someone who has lived their entire life in Milton."

"If you had come to our house when you promised, I might have explained. But Father says you are just like the rest of them; it is 'out of sight, out of mind' with you."

"I don't know who 'the rest' refers to, and I have been very busy. To tell the truth, I had forgotten my promise—"

"You offered it; we asked for nothing," Bessy interjected.

"I forgot what I said for the time being," Margaret continued calmly. "I suppose I would have remembered sooner if I were less occupied. May I accompany you now?"

Bessy cast a swift glance at Margaret's face to discern whether the expressed desire was genuine. The sharpness in her eyes softened into a wistful longing as she met Margaret's soft, friendly gaze. "I have no one so many to care for me; if you care, you may come."

Thus, they walked on in silence. As they ascended into a small courtyard off a squalid street, Bessy said, "You won't be intimidated if Father is home and speaks somewhat gruffly at first. He has taken a liking to you, you see, and he values your visit

"Well, I suppose we must," Mrs. Thornton sighed, the weight of necessity settling heavily on her shoulders.

The decision to visit the Hales had required a diplomatic campaign on the part of Mr. Thornton. Persuading his mother to abandon her usual protocol of "heavy state" visits—a mode of travel involving hired carriages and a specific, solemn demeanor—had proven difficult. While Mr. Thornton had offered to provide a carriage, Mrs. Thornton remained steadfast in her refusal to accept permanent horses. For her, transportation was a matter of occasion: hired cabs for the rare social engagements, and only the finest equines for the truly grave matters of life and death.

Currently, however, she found herself in a precarious position. She had recently utilized borrowed horses for three days straight, a maneuver that had successfully exhausted the patience and resources of every acquaintance within a twenty-mile radius. Now, with her local network depleted, she faced the prospect of traveling to Crampton, a distance too great for comfortable walking. When she pressed her son regarding the cost of hiring a cab, her tone suggested that the mere idea of paying for such a convenience was absurd.

"Why should I incur this expense?" she had reasoned aloud. "There is no utility in cultivating intimacy with every teacher and master in Milton. Next, he will expect me to call upon Fanny's dancing instructor's wife!"

"And so I would, Mother," Mr. Thornton replied gently, "were Mr. Mason and his wife friendless in this strange city, much as the Hales are."

"Oh! Do not speak so hastily," she countered, her eyes narrowing. "I intend to depart tomorrow. My sole purpose today is to ensure you understand the gravity of the situation."

"If you are leaving tomorrow, I shall arrange for the horses immediately."

"Nonsense, John. One would think you were made of pure gold."

"Not entirely, yet. However, regarding the horses, my resolve is absolute. Recall the last time you ventured out in a cab; you returned home suffering from a severe headache caused by the violent jolting."

"I never complained of it, I assure you," she retorted.

"No," he agreed with a touch of pride. "My mother is not a woman given to complaining. Precisely because she does not complain, I feel compelled to watch over her health with greater vigilance."

He turned his gaze toward his daughter, who sat nearby. "As for Fanny, a measure of hardship would actually benefit her constitution."

"She is not forged from the same material as you, John," Mrs. Thornton stated firmly. "She could not withstand such rigors."

Mrs. Thornton fell silent. Her final remark touched upon a subject that mortified her deeply. She harbored an unconscious disdain for weakness, particularly in those areas where both she and her son possessed strength. Fanny was fragile precisely where her parents were robust. Mrs. Thornton was rarely inclined toward lengthy philosophical debates; her sharp intellect and iron will allowed her to bypass internal deliberation and arrive instantly at conclusions. Instinctively, she knew that nothing could fortify Fanny to endure suffering patiently or confront adversity with courage. Though this realization stung her, it transformed into a peculiar, pitying tenderness in her demeanor—a behavior reminiscent of how mothers treat their frail, sickly offspring.

To an outside observer, unaware of the family dynamics, Mrs. Thornton's conduct might have appeared to indicate a far deeper affection for Fanny than for John. Such an interpretation would have been profoundly mistaken. The boldness with which mother and son exchanged uncomfortable truths revealed a profound trust in the solidity of their respective characters. In contrast, Mrs. Thornton's uneasy gentleness toward her daughter stemmed from a desire to conceal Fanny's poverty amidst the grand virtues she herself possessed and highly valued in others. This shame exposed a lack of security in her maternal affection. She addressed her son solely as "John," reserving endearments like "love" and "dear" exclusively for Fanny. Yet, her heart gave thanks for her son day and night, and she walked proudly among her peers largely due to his existence.

"Fanny dear," Mrs. Thornton announced, breaking the tension, "I shall hire horses for the carriage today to visit the Hales. Would you not like to stop by Nurse's house first? It lies in the same direction, and she delights in seeing you. You could visit her while I attend to Mrs. Hale."

"Oh, Mamma, it is such a long way, and I am so tired," Fanny protested.

"Tired of what?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her brow furrowing slightly.

"The weather, I suppose. It feels so oppressive."

"Could not you ask Nurse to come here? The carriage could fetch her, allowing her to spend the remainder of the day with us, which I am certain she would appreciate."

Mrs. Thornton paused, setting down her embroidery and appearing to deliberate. Finally, she spoke. "It would be a considerable distance for her to walk back at night."

"Oh, I shall send her home in a cab. I simply forgot to consider her needs."

Just then, Mr. Thornton entered the room, preparing to depart for the mill.

"Mother," he said, "need I remind you that if there is any minor comfort you can offer Mrs. Hale, considering her condition, please extend it willingly?"

"If I can ascertain what she requires, certainly. However, having never suffered illness myself, I am somewhat unfamiliar with the particular whims of invalids."

"Well, here stands Fanny," Mr. Thornton pointed out, gesturing to his daughter. "She is perpetually afflicted. Perhaps she can suggest something suitable."

"I do not suffer from ailments constantly," Fanny snapped pettishly. "Moreover, I am not accompanying Mother. I have a headache today and shall remain indoors."

Mr. Thornton's expression darkened with annoyance. His mother's eyes were fixed intently on her needlework, stitching away industriously.

"Fanny," he commanded authoritatively, "I require you to go. It will prove beneficial to you rather than detrimental. Please comply with this request without further discussion."

Having delivered this ultimatum, he exited the room abruptly. Had he lingered even a moment longer, Fanny likely would have burst into tears at his imperious tone, even when couched in polite phrasing. Instead, she merely muttered under her breath.

"John always acts as if I imagine myself to be ill, when I am perfectly certain I am not. Who exactly are these Hales that warrant such excessive fuss?"

"Fanny, do not speak disparagingly of your brother," Mrs. Thornton admonished softly. "He possesses valid reasons, or he would not urge us to make this visit. Hasten and prepare yourself."

However, the brief skirmish between son and daughter did little to improve Mrs. Thornton's disposition toward the Hales. Her possessive heart replayed her daughter's query: *Who are they, that he is so eager for us to bestow our attention upon?* The question hung in the air like a burdensome refrain, lingering long after Fanny had dismissed the matter entirely in the delightful anticipation of admiring her new bonnet in the mirror.

Mrs. Thornton was inherently shy. Only in recent years had she possessed the leisure to engage in societal activities, and even then, she derived satisfaction primarily from hosting dinners or critiquing the culinary arts of others. Venturing out to cultivate acquaintanceship with strangers was a wholly different endeavor, one that left her distinctly ill-at-ease. Consequently, she appeared unusually stern and forbidding as she crossed the threshold into the Hales' modest drawing-room.

Margaret Hale was occupied with embroidering a delicate piece of cambric, intended for a garment for Edith's unborn child. To Mrs. Thornton, this activity represented "flimsy, useless labor." Conversely, she held Mrs. Hale's double knitting in higher regard, deeming it practical and sensible. The room itself was cluttered with various trinkets and ornaments, tasks that demanded significant time to clean—a luxury scarce for families of limited means. These observations formed the backdrop of Mrs. Thornton's stately conversation, punctuated by the standard platitudes that many recite while effectively blinding their senses to reality.

Mrs. Hale, meanwhile, exerted considerable effort in her responses, captivated by a strand of antique lace adorning Mrs. Thornton's attire. As she later confided to Dixon, it was "old English point, not manufactured for seventy years, impossible to purchase. It must be an heirloom, proving she descended from distinguished ancestors." Thus, the possessor of such ancestral treasures deemed herself worthy of more than the languid exertion required merely to be agreeable to a guest.

Soon, Margaret, straining her mental faculties to converse with Fanny, overheard her mother and Mrs. Thornton descending into an interminable discourse on domestic service.

"I presume you are not musical," Fanny remarked, scanning the room. "I observe no piano."

"I hold a fondness for fine music, though I lack the skill to perform it myself. Furthermore, neither my father nor my mother places much value on it; consequently, we sold our old instrument upon arriving here."

"How can you possibly subsist without one? It seems almost essential to life," Fanny mused.

*Fifteen shillings per week, with three shillings saved,* Margaret thought internally. *Yet, she must have been exceedingly young. She likely has forgotten her own youthful experiences, though she ought to recall those times.*

A subtle chill permeated Margaret's demeanor as she responded next. "You host excellent concerts here, I believe."

"Oh, indeed! Delightful! Though they are excessively

"That doubt and trouble, fear and pain,
And anguish, all, are shadows vain,
That death itself shall not remain;
That weary deserts we may tread,
A dreary labyrinth may thread.
Thro' dark ways underground be led;
Yet, if we will one Guide obey,
The dreariest path, the darkest way
Shall issue out in heavenly day;
And we, on divers shores now cast,
Shall meet, our perilous voyage past,
All in our Father's house at last!"
—R. C. Trench.

As soon as their visitors departed, Margaret hastened upstairs, donning her bonnet and shawl with eager haste. Her destination was clear: she intended to inquire after Betsy Higgins and spend as much time as possible with her before dinner.

Stepping out onto the street, Margaret found the narrow thoroughfares teeming with life. To her, the bustling crowd held a newfound significance, a tangible connection forged simply by learning to care for those who called such cramped quarters home. Inside the Higgins household, Mary Higgins—the younger sister, whose appearance was perpetually untidy—had managed to prepare the room for the anticipated arrival. Despite the efforts, the house bore the marks of its industrial surroundings; the floor remained rough-stone in the center, while the flagstones beneath the chairs and tables, as well as those lining the walls, retained their grimy, unwashed darkness.

Though the day was swelteringly hot, a large fire burned brightly within the grate, transforming the entire room into an oven. Margaret failed to recognize the lavish consumption of coal as a gesture of warm hospitality from Mary; instead, she assumed the stifling heat was medically necessary for Bessy's condition.

Bessy lay upon a small sofa positioned beneath the window. She appeared significantly weaker than the previous day, exhausted from repeatedly rising to peer outside, hoping against hope to spot Margaret's approach. Now that her friend had arrived and seated herself beside her, Bessy sank back into silence, her gaze fixed adoringly on Margaret's face. With childlike fascination, she traced the textures of Margaret's clothing, marveling at their refinement.

"I never understood why the folks in the Bible spoke so highly of soft garments," Bessy remarked. "It must be delightful to walk dressed as you do. It feels entirely different from the ordinary. Most fine clothes tire my eyes with their glaring colors, but somehow, yours bring me peace. Where did you acquire this frock?"

"In London," Margaret replied, amused by the question.

"London! Have you actually been there?"

"Yes. I lived there for several years. Though my true home lies elsewhere—in the countryside, amidst the forests."

"Tell me about it," Bessy urged, leaning back and closing her eyes, crossing her hands over her chest as if ready to absorb every word. "I love hearing about the country, the trees, and such things."

Margaret hesitated. Since leaving Helstone, she had rarely spoken of the village save for incidental mentions. Yet, in her dreams, Helstone appeared more vivid than reality itself. As sleep claimed her at night, her memories drifted effortlessly through its cherished landscapes. Seeing the trust in Bessy's eyes, her heart softened.

"Oh, Bessy, I loved the home we left so dearly! I wish you could see it. I cannot describe half its beauty. Great trees stand everywhere, their branches stretching long and level, casting a deep, cooling shade even at midday. Yet, though every leaf seems still, there is a constant, rushing sound of movement all around—not close at hand. Sometimes the turf is as soft and fine as velvet; other times, it is lush with the perpetual moisture of a tiny, hidden brook nearby, tinkling softly. In other areas, vast stretches of billowy fern rise up—some in green shadow, others bathed in long streaks of golden sunlight, resembling the sea."

"I have never seen the sea," Bessy murmured. "But please, go on."

"And here and there lie wide commons, elevated as if above the very tops of the trees—"

"That is wonderful," Bessy interrupted eagerly. "I felt suffocated down below. Whenever I ventured out, I always craved climbing high to see far away, to take a deep, fulfilling breath of fresh air. I become so smothered in Milton that I need to escape. I imagine the eternal rustling you described among the trees would send me dizzy; it was precisely that sound that gave me a headache in the mill. On these commons, however, there must be little noise?"

"No," Margaret assured her. "Nothing but occasionally a lark singing high overhead. Sometimes I'd hear a farmer shouting sharply at his servants, but it was so distant that it merely brought a pleasant reminder that others were hard at work elsewhere, while I sat on the heather doing nothing."

"I once thought," Bessy continued, her voice trembling slightly, "that if I could have a single day of pure idleness, resting in a quiet place like the one you describe, it might restore me. But now, after many days of forced leisure, I am just as weary of them as I was of my labor. Sometimes I am so utterly drained that I fear I cannot enjoy heaven without a period of earthly rest first. I am somewhat afraid of going straight there without a good sleep in the grave to strengthen me."

"Do not be afraid, Bessy," Margaret said gently, placing her hand over the girl's. "God can grant you a perfection of rest that neither earthly idleness nor the dead sleep of the grave can provide."

Bessy shifted uncomfortably. "I wish Father would not speak to me as he does. He means well, as I told you yesterday, and I tell you again. But you see, though I do not believe him during the day, by night—when I am burning with fever, half-asleep and half-awake—it returns to haunt me. Oh, so terribly! And I think, if this is truly the end of everything, and if all I was born for was to wear out my heart and life, to sicken in this dreadful place with those mill-noises ringing in my ears forever until I scream to be silenced, and to choke on the dust until I thirst for one long, deep breath of the clear air you speak of... and with my mother gone, unable to tell her again how much I loved her, and with all my troubles unresolved..."

Her voice broke, and she sat up abruptly, seizing Margaret's hand with violent, almost fierce intensity.

"You wench, you!" she cried. "If this life is the end, and there is no God to wipe away tears from all eyes—you, I could go mad, and kill you, I could!"

She collapsed back onto the sofa, completely spent by the surge of her passion.

Immediately, Margaret knelt beside her. "Bessy, we have a Father in Heaven."

"I know it! I know it!" Bessy moaned, turning her head restlessly from side to side. "I am very wicked. I have spoken very wickedly. Oh, do not be frightened of me, and never come again. I would not harm a hair of your head. And," she opened her eyes, staring earnestly at Margaret, "I believe, perhaps more than you do, in what is to come. I have memorized the Book of Revelation, and whenever I am awake and in my right mind, I never doubt the glory awaiting me."

"Let us not discuss the fancies that enter your fevered head," Margaret advised kindly. "I would prefer to hear about what you used to do when you were well."

"I think I was well when Mother died," Bessy admitted. "But I have never been truly strong since sometime around that time. I began working in a carding-room shortly thereafter, and the fluff entered my lungs and poisoned me."

"Fluff?" Margaret asked, puzzled.

"Fluff," Bessy repeated. "Tiny bits that fly off the cotton during carding, filling the air until it looks like fine white dust. They say it coils around the lungs and tightens them. Many who work in carding-rooms end up wasting away, coughing and spitting blood, simply poisoned by the fluff."

"But can it not be prevented?" Margaret questioned.

"I do not know," Bessy sighed. "Some factories have a great wheel at one end to create a draft and carry the dust away. But that wheel costs a fortune—perhaps five or six hundred pounds—and yields no profit. Only a few masters dare install them. Moreover, I've heard workers complain that they dislike working where such a wheel exists. After being accustomed to swallowing the dust, they claim the clean air makes them hungry, insisting their wages should be raised to compensate for the hardship of breathing freely. Thus, between the masters and the men, the wheels never get installed. I know I wished ours had one."

"Did your father not know about it?"

"He did," Bessy confirmed. "He was sorry. But our factory was generally a good one, staffed by steady, reliable people. My father feared sending me to a strange place. Even now, you might not believe it, but many considered me a respectable young woman. I did not wish to be labeled 'nesh' and soft. Furthermore, Mother insisted on keeping Mary's education, and Father always enjoyed buying books and attending various lectures—all expenses that required money. So, I simply continued working until I shall never remove the whirring from my ears or the fl

"I was used to sleep at nights as sweetly as a child,—Now if the wind blew rough, it made me start, And think of my poor boy tossing about Upon the roaring seas. And then I seemed To feel that it was hard to take him from me For such a little fault." —SOUTHEY.

It was a profound relief to Margaret, in these latter days, to discover that her mother drew nearer to her with a tenderness and intimacy unknown since the days of her girlhood. Her mother embraced her as a confidante, filling the role Margaret had always secretly coveted and envied in Dixon. Margaret responded eagerly to every appeal for sympathy, however trivial the cause might seem to her own judgment—an elephant lifting a tiny pin at its foot only because commanded by its keeper. Unconsciously, Margaret was drawing close to a revelation she had long sought.

One evening, with Mr. Hale away, her mother began to speak of her brother Frederick, the very topic Margaret had craved to discuss but whose mention usually silenced her natural boldness. The stronger her desire to know, the more reticent she became.

"Oh, Margaret, the wind last night was dreadful! It howled down the chimney into our room. I could not sleep; I never can when the wind is so fierce. I developed a waking habit when poor Frederick was at sea, and now, even if I do not wake suddenly, I dream of him amidst some tempestuous sea, surrounded by towering walls of glass-green water, rising higher than his ship's masts, curling over her with that cruel, terrible white foam, like a gigantic crested serpent. It is an ancient dream, yet it returns on every windy night until I am grateful to wake, sitting upright and rigid in bed, seized by terror. Poor Frederick! He is on land now, so the wind can harm him no longer. Though I feared it might topple some of those tall chimneys."

"Where is Frederick now, Mamma?" Margaret asked gently. "Our letters are addressed to the care of Messrs. Barbour in Cadiz. I know that, but where is he personally?"

"I cannot recall the specific town, but he does not answer to the name Hale; you must remember that, Margaret. Observe the initials 'F.D.' stamped in every corner of the envelopes. He has assumed the surname Dickinson. I wished him to retain the name Beresford, to which he had a certain claim, but your father deemed it wiser otherwise. He might be recognized if he bore my name."

"Mamma," Margaret ventured, "I was at Aunt Shaw's when everything occurred, and I suppose I was too young to be told plainly about it. Yet, I should like to know now, if I may—if speaking of it causes you no excessive pain."

"Pain!" Mrs. Hale replied, her cheek flushing warmly. "Not pain, surely. Yet, it hurts to think I may never see my darling boy again. Or, perhaps, he acted rightly. People may say what they please, but I possess his own letters as proof, and I shall believe him, though he is my son, sooner than any earthly court-martial."

She motioned toward a small Japanese cabinet. "Go to my little cabinet, dear, and in the second drawer on the left, you will find a packet of letters."

Margaret obeyed. There lay the yellow, sea-stained missives, carrying the distinct, briny fragrance of ocean mail. Returning to her mother, she watched as the older woman untied the silken cord with trembling fingers. Examining the dates, Mrs. Hale handed the bundle to Margaret, offering hurried, anxious commentary on their contents before her daughter could fully grasp them.

"You see, Margaret, from the very beginning, he detested Captain Reid. He was a second lieutenant aboard the *Orion* when Frederick sailed for the first time. How well he looked in his midshipman's uniform, holding his dirk as if it were a penknife, slicing open newspapers! But this Mr. Reid, then merely a lieutenant, seemed to harbor a dislike for Frederick from the outset. Then—wait! These are letters written aboard the *Russell*. When he was assigned to her and discovered his old adversary, Captain Reid, in command, he intended to endure his tyranny with patience. Read this, Margaret. Where does he say—"

She paused abruptly. "'My father may rely upon me that I will bear with all proper patience everything that one officer and gentleman can take from another. But from my former knowledge of my present captain, I confess I look forward with apprehension to a long course of tyranny on board the *Russell*.'"

"He promises patience, and I am certain he kept it," Mrs. Hale murmured, her voice softening. "He was the sweetest-tempered boy, when not provoked, that could possibly exist. Is this the letter regarding Captain Reid's impatience with the men for failing to execute ship maneuvers as swiftly as the *Avenger*? He notes that the *Russell* had many new hands, whereas the *Avenger* had spent nearly three years on the station, keeping slavers at bay and working her crew until they scrambled the rigging like rats or monkeys."

Margaret read the faded, partially illegible letter slowly. It described Captain Reid's imperiousness over trifles, likely exaggerated by the writer while the memory was fresh and hot. A sailor aloft in the main-topsail rigging had been ordered to descend rapidly, threatened with the cat-o'-nine-tails if he lagged behind. The man furthest out on the spar, realizing he could not pass his companions yet terrified of the disgrace of flogging, threw himself desperately downward to grab a lower rope. He missed, fell senseless onto the deck, and died within hours. The crew's indignation was at a boiling point when young Hale penned this account.

"But we received this letter long after hearing of the mutiny," Mrs. Hale whispered, her voice thick with emotion. "Poor Fred! Writing it must have comforted him, even if he knew not how to send it. Soon after, the newspapers reported an atrocious mutiny on the *Russell*, claiming the rebels retained possession and fled to become pirates, leaving Captain Reid adrift in a boat with a handful of officers and men whose names were listed—they were rescued by a West Indian steamer. Oh, Margaret! How your father and I grew ill reading that list when Frederick Hale's name was missing. We suspected a mistake; poor Fred was such a fine fellow, perhaps only slightly too passionate. We hoped 'Carr,' appearing on the list, was a printer's error for 'Hale'; newspapers are so careless."

She recounted how, the following evening, Mr. Hale walked to Southampton to fetch the papers. Unable to stay at home, Mrs. Hale met him under the hedge near the Oldham beech tree. He arrived late, his limbs limp, his head bowed, walking with the heaviness of a man bearing a crushing burden.

"Margaret, I see him now," she said, her eyes glistening.

"Don't go on, Mamma. I understand it all," Margaret interjected, leaning affectionately against her mother's side and kissing her hand.

"No, you cannot, Margaret. No one can unless they witnessed it. I could scarcely raise myself to greet him; the world seemed to reel around me. When I reached him, he neither spoke nor showed surprise at finding me three miles from home. Instead, he slipped my arm into his, stroking my hand as if soothing me under a great blow. When I trembled so violently I could not utter a word, he gathered me in his arms, bent his head to mine, and began to shake and sob in a strange, muffled, groaning voice. So frightened was I that I stood utterly still, begging him to tell me what he had heard. Then, with a jerky movement—as if another hand guided it—he thrust a vile newspaper into my hands, labeling our Frederick a 'traitor of the blackest dye,' 'a base, ungrateful disgrace to his profession.' Oh! I cannot recount the foul epithets they employed."

Mrs. Hale's voice broke. "I took the paper immediately. I tore it into tiny fragments—I tore it—oh, I believe, Margaret, I tore it with my teeth. I did not cry. I could not. My cheeks burned like fire, and my eyes felt as though they were burning inside my skull. I saw your father looking grimly at me. I declared it a lie, and indeed it was. Months later, this letter arrived, revealing the provocation Frederick endured. He did not rebel for himself or his own grievances; he refused to remain silent against Captain Reid's cruelty, and thus things deteriorated from bad to worse. Most of the sailors stood by Frederick."

After a long silence, her voice weak and trembling, she added, "I think, Margaret, I am glad of it. I am prouder of Frederick standing up against injustice than if he had simply been a compliant officer."

"I am sure I am," Margaret affirmed firmly. "Loyalty and obedience to wisdom and justice are noble, but it is even nobler to defy arbitrary power, unjustly and cruelly wielded—not for oneself, but for others who are more helpless."

"For all that," Mrs. Hale sighed wistfully, "I wish I could see Frederick once more—just once. He was my first baby, Margaret." She spoke with a longing that bordered on apology, as if fearing such a wish diminished her love for her surviving child. Yet, such a thought never crossed Margaret's mind; instead, she pondered

"Thought fights with thought; out springs a spark of truth / From the collision of the sword and shield." — W. S. Landor.

The following morning, Mr. Hale approached Margaret with a proposal. "We must return Mrs. Thornton's call," he said gently. "Your mother is unwell and feels unable to travel so far today, but you and I shall go this afternoon."

As they walked together, Mr. Hale broached the subject of Mrs. Hale's health with a quiet, underlying anxiety that finally pierced through Margaret's earlier attempts to dismiss it.

"Have you consulted a doctor, Margaret? Have you sent for one?" he asked.

"No, Papa," she replied, surprised by his persistence. "You mentioned he might come to see me when I was well. But if only I knew of a competent physician, I would ask him to come this very afternoon. I am certain Mother is seriously indisposed."

She laid her fears bare and forcefully, sensing that her father had previously blocked the possibility of serious illness. His response, however, was heavy with despair.

"Do you truly believe she harbors a hidden ailment? Is she gravely ill? Has Dixon said anything? Oh, Margaret! I am tormented by the fear that our arrival in Milton has ruined her health. My poor Maria!"

"Oh, Papa! Do not imagine such horrors," Margaret exclaimed, shocked. "She is merely unwell, that is all. Many endure temporary ailments; with proper care, they recover stronger than before."

"But has Dixon offered any insight?"

"No! You know Dixon delights in turning trifles into mysteries. Her vagueness regarding Mother's condition has indeed alarmed me, though perhaps without cause. As you once noted, I was becoming fanciful."

"I hope and pray you retain that capacity for imagination," Mr. Hale countered warmly. "But disregard my earlier words. I cherish your concern for your mother. Tell me your worries freely; I may have sounded annoyed before, but I value your vigilance. Let us inquire of Mrs. Thornton if she knows of a skilled doctor. We shall not waste our resources on anyone less than the finest."

Turning the corner onto Marlborough Street, the scene shifted dramatically. The street held no grand residences worthy of Mrs. Thornton's stature. While Mr. Hale's presence usually masked the nature of his home, Margaret had instinctively pictured the formidable Mrs. Thornton residing in a mansion matching her own elegance. Instead, Marlborough Street revealed long rows of modest cottages and blank walls.

"He assured me he lived here," Mr. Hale murmured, looking perplexed.

"Perhaps it is one of his frugal measures to reside in a humble dwelling," Margaret suggested. "But there are many people about; let me ask."

She queried a passerby, who confirmed that Mr. Thornton resided near the mill, pointing toward the factory lodge at the end of a long, dead-end wall.

The lodge itself resembled a standard garden gate, flanked by massive iron doors for carts and wagons. Beyond lay a vast rectangular yard containing business offices on one side and the immense, multi-windowed mill on the other. From the factory issued a constant, deafening clatter of machinery and the groaning roar of the steam engine. Across the yard stood a handsome, stone-capped house. Though blackened by industrial smoke, its paint, windows, and steps were scrubbed with meticulous care. Built perhaps half a century prior, its architecture—stone facings, narrow windows, multiple flights of stairs with railings—betrayed its age. Margaret wondered why those who could afford such a substantial home did not opt for a smaller cottage in the countryside or suburbs, escaping the relentless noise and chaos of the factory district.

Standing on the steps, Margaret barely heard her father's voice. The yard, bounded by the silent wall, offered a dismal view compared to the interior she soon encountered. After climbing the old-fashioned staircase, they were ushered into a drawing-room where three windows overlooked the front door and the adjacent room. No one was present. It appeared untouched since the furniture had been carefully bagged, as if preparing for a volcanic eruption to bury the house a millennium later.

The room was a spectacle of sterile perfection. Walls painted pink and gold faced a carpet patterned with floral bunches, largely obscured by a colorless linen runner. Lace curtains framed the windows; every chair and sofa was draped in protective nets or knitted covers. Alabaster statues sat on every available surface beneath glass shades. In the center, beneath a bagged chandelier, stood a large circular table adorned with neatly bound books arranged like colorful spokes of a wheel. Every surface reflected light; nothing absorbed it. The result was a painfully spangled, speckled atmosphere that unsettled Margaret. She struggled to comprehend the effort required to maintain such icy, snowy purity in such an environment. Everywhere she looked, there was evidence of labor, but not labor aimed at comfort or tranquility; it was labor dedicated solely to ornamentation and the preservation of that ornament from dust or damage.

They waited in silence before Mrs. Thornton arrived. She entered rustling in elegant black silk, her muslins and laces competing with, yet failing to surpass, the pristine whiteness of the room's decor. Margaret explained her mother's inability to join them, but driven by anxiety to avoid reigniting her father's fears, she delivered a clumsy account. She implied Mrs. Hale's condition was a temporary, perhaps fanciful, ailment of a "fine lady," easily dismissed if motivation existed, or deferrable due to severity. Recalling the horses hired for her own visit and the instruction given to Fanny to attend to the Hales with utmost respect, Mrs. Thornton bristled slightly. She offered no sympathy and granted little credence to the report of Mrs. Hale's illness.

"How fares Mr. Thornton?" Mr. Hale inquired. "I feared he was unwell based on his hurried note yesterday."

"My son is rarely ill," Mrs. Thornton replied smoothly. "When he is, he neither complains nor uses it as an excuse for inaction. He told me he lacked leisure to read with you last night, sir. He regrets it deeply; he values those hours spent in your company."

"I am certain they are equally delightful to me," Mr. Hale responded. "Seeing his joy and appreciation for the finer aspects of classical literature makes me feel youthful again."

"I have no doubt classics are desirable for those with leisure," Mrs. Thornton conceded, though her tone remained sharp. "Yet, I confess, renewing his studies was contrary to my judgment. The time and place in which he lives demand all his energy and attention. Classics suit men who loaf away their days in the country or colleges. But men of Milton must absorb their thoughts and powers into the work of today. That is my firm belief." She added this final clause with a pride that mimicked humility.

"But surely," Margaret interjected, "if the mind focuses too long on a single object, it becomes stiff and rigid, incapable of embracing diverse interests."

"I fail to grasp what you mean by a mind growing stiff," Mrs. Thornton retorted. "Nor do I admire those 'whirligig' characters who abandon one passion today only to forget it entirely tomorrow. Possessing myriad interests does not suit the life of a Milton manufacturer. It suffices—and ought to suffice—for him to possess one great desire and dedicate all life's purposes to fulfilling it."

"And that desire is—" Mr. Hale prompted.

Mrs. Thornton's sallow cheeks flushed, her eyes lighting up. "To hold and maintain a high, honorable position among the merchants of his country—the men of his town. Such a standing my son has earned for himself. Go where you will—I need not limit myself to England—but across Europe, the name of John Thornton of Milton is known and respected by all men of business. Naturally, it remains unknown in fashionable circles," she continued with scorn. "Idle gentlemen and ladies are unlikely to know much of a Milton manufacturer unless he enters Parliament or marries a lord's daughter."

Both Mr. Hale and Margaret felt an awkward, almost comical realization that they had never heard of this celebrated figure until Mr. Bell wrote recommending him as a valuable acquaintance in Milton. Mrs. Thornton's world was alien to theirs: neither the Harley Street gentility nor the circle of country clergy and Hampshire squires. Despite her efforts to remain neutral, Margaret's face betrayed her unease.

"You presume I have never heard of this wonderful son of mine, Miss Hale? You think I am an old woman obsessed with Milton, whose own crow is the whitest ever seen?"

"No," Margaret replied with spirit. "It may be true that I had scarcely heard Mr. Thornton's name before arriving in Milton. But since my arrival, I have heard enough to respect and admire him, and to recognize the justice and truth in your praise."

"Who spoke to you of him?" Mrs. Thornton asked, slightly softened yet wary that others might not have done him full justice.

Margaret hesitated, disliking the authoritative tone. Mr. Hale stepped in to her aid. "It was Mr. Thornton himself who revealed the kind of man he is, was it not, Margaret?"

Mrs. Thornton straightened her posture. "My son is not one to recount his own deeds. May I ask, Miss Hale, from whose account you formed your favorable opinion? A mother is naturally curious and eager for commendation of her offspring."

"It was largely what Mr. Thornton *withheld*," Margaret answered diplomatically. "More than what he said, it was the omission of details regarding his past life, as shared by Mr. Bell, that led us to feel the depth of your justified

"Trust in that veiled hand, which leads
None by the path that he would go;
And always be for change prepared,
For the world's law is ebb and flow."

The following afternoon, Dr. Donaldson arrived to pay his first visit to Mrs. Hale. The delicate mystery that Margaret had hoped their recent intimacy might have dissolved was immediately reinstated. She was barred from the room, while Dixon was ushered inside. Margaret was not naturally prone to jealousy, yet where her affection burned, it consumed her with a fierce, possessive intensity. Retreating to her mother's bedroom, situated directly behind the drawing-room, she paced the floor, waiting for the physician to emerge. Periodically, she halted, straining her ears. She imagined she heard a moan—a faint, ragged sound. Her fingers curled tightly, her breath hitched in her throat. She was certain she had heard it. Then, silence reigned for several moments. Finally, the rustle of chairs, the murmur of raised voices, and the subtle noises of departure filled the air. As the door opened, Margaret slipped out swiftly.

"My father is away, Doctor," she announced, her tone crisp and authoritative. "He must attend to a pupil at this hour. Would you kindly follow me to his study downstairs?"

She observed Dixon's startled reaction and felt a surge of triumph. She had successfully navigated every obstacle the old servant erected; adopting the demeanor of the eldest daughter, she silenced Dixon's officiousness with effortless authority. This sudden assumption of dignity amused her momentarily, cutting through the sharp edge of her anxiety. She watched Dixon's bewildered face and allowed herself a brief respite from the crushing weight of the situation. But the reality crashed back instantly, stealing the air from her lungs.

It took a moment for her voice to return, but when it did, it carried an air of absolute command. "What is the matter with my mother? You will oblige me by stating the plain truth."

Seeing a flicker of hesitation in the doctor's eyes, she pressed on. "I am her only child—at least, here in this house. I fear my father is not sufficiently alarmed. Therefore, if there is grave cause for concern, it must be delivered to him gently. I can handle this. I can care for my mother. Please, speak, sir. To see your face unreadable is a terror worse than whatever words you might deliver."

"My dear young lady," Dr. Donaldson replied, "your mother appears to have a most attentive and efficient servant, someone who acts more like a friend—"

"I am her daughter, sir."

"But when I tell you she explicitly requested that you remain unaware—"

"I am neither virtuous nor patient enough to submit to such a prohibition. Furthermore, I am convinced you are too wise, too experienced, to have sworn to secrecy."

"Well," he said, offering a half-smile tinged with sadness, "in that, you are correct. I made no promise. Indeed, I fear the truth will surface soon enough without my revealing it."

He paused. Margaret turned pale, pressing her lips together tighter. Not a muscle elsewhere betrayed her emotion. Possessing the intuitive grasp of character essential to a physician of his stature, Dr. Donaldson realized she would demand the complete truth; she would detect even the slightest omission, and the torment of suspicion would be far sharper than the knowledge itself. Speaking in a low voice, he watched her intently until her pupils dilated into pools of black horror and her pallor deepened to a livid shade. He stopped. He waited for the shock to pass, for her gasping breath to steady.

Then she spoke. "I thank you most sincerely, sir, for your confidence. That dread has haunted me for weeks. It is a genuine, excruciating agony. My poor, poor mother!"

Her lips trembled, and he permitted her the release of tears, confident in her capacity for self-command to restrain them. A few tears flowed, and then she remembered the flood of questions burning within her. "Will there be much suffering?"

He shook his head. "We cannot predict that. It depends on constitution, on countless variables. However, recent advancements in medical science have granted us considerable power to alleviate pain."

"My father!" Margaret whispered, trembling violently. "I do not know Mr. Hale. I mean... it is difficult to offer counsel. Yet, I would say: endure, armed with the knowledge I have forced upon you so abruptly, until the fact I could not conceal becomes somewhat familiar to you. Only then will you be able, without excessive strain, to offer comfort to your father. Until then—my visits, which I shall undoubtedly repeat, though I fear I can do little but ease symptoms—a thousand minor incidents will arise to heighten his alarm, preparing him gradually. Nay, my dear young lady—nay, my dear—I saw Mr. Thornton, and I honor your father for the sacrifice he makes, however mistaken I may deem his judgment. Very well, this once, if it pleases you, my dear. Remember, when I return, I come as a friend. You must learn to view me thus, for meeting in such critical times is worth years of polite morning calls."

Margaret could not speak; instead, she squeezed his hand tightly at parting.

"That is what I call a fine girl," Dr. Donaldson mused as he settled into his carriage, examining the ringed hand that had suffered from her grip. "Who would have thought such a small hand possessed such strength? The bones are well-set, granting immense power. What a queen she is! First, throwing her head back to compel me to speak the truth, then leaning forward so eagerly to listen. Poor creature! I must ensure she does not overexert herself. It is astonishing what these thoroughbred souls can endure. That girl is spine-backed. Another, who had drained to that color, would have collapsed into fainting or hysteria. But she refused both—not she! The sheer force of her will brought her back. A girl like that would win my heart if I were thirty years younger. Too late now. Ah, here we are at the Archers'."

Leaping from the vehicle, he descended with thought, wisdom, experience, and sympathy, ready to serve the family as if they were the sole occupants of the world.

Meanwhile, Margaret retreated briefly to her father's study to regain her composure before ascending to her mother's chamber. "Oh, my God, my God! This is terrible. How shall I endure it? Such a fatal illness! No hope! Oh, Mamma, Mamma, I wish I had never gone to Aunt Shaw's, never spent those precious years away from you! Poor Mamma! How much she must have suffered! Oh, pray, my God, that her pain may not be too acute, too dreadful. How can I bear to witness it? How can I withstand Papa's agony? He must not be told yet—not all at once. It would kill him. But I cannot waste another second of my beloved, precious mother's time."

She ran upstairs. Dixon was absent. Mrs. Hale reclined in an easy chair, draped in a soft white shawl, wearing a modest cap, anticipating the doctor's arrival. Her face held a faint flush, and the exhaustion following the examination lent her features a serene quality. Margaret was taken aback by her mother's apparent calm.

"Why, Margaret, how strangely you look! What is the matter?" Mrs. Hale asked. Then, as the realization of the true state of affairs crept into her mind, she added, with a touch of displeasure, "You haven't seen Dr. Donaldson, have you? Haven't you asked him any questions, child?"

Margaret remained silent, gazing wistfully toward her. Mrs. Hale grew more annoyed. "He would surely not break his word to me, and—"

"Oh, yes, Mamma, he did. I compelled him. It was I—blame me."

Kneeling beside her mother, Margaret seized her hand, refusing to let go despite Mrs. Hale's attempts to withdraw. She kissed it fervently, her hot tears soaking the fabric.

"Margaret, it was very wrong of you. You knew I did not wish you to know." Yet, worn down by the struggle, she left her hand in Margaret's grasp, eventually returning the pressure feebly. This encouragement prompted Margaret to speak.

"Oh, Mamma! Let me be your nurse. I will learn whatever Dixon can teach me. But you know I am your child, and I believe I have a right to do everything for you."

"You do not realize what you are asking," Mrs. Hale shuddered.

"Yes, I do. I understand far more than you suspect. Let me be your nurse. Let me try, at any rate. No one has ever, nor shall ever, strive harder than I will. It will be such a comfort, Mamma."

"My poor child! Well, you shall try. Did you know, Margaret, that Dixon and I feared you would recoil in horror upon learning the truth?"

"Dixon thought!" Margaret retorted, her lip curling. "Dixon could not credit me with enough true love—not even as much as she possesses herself! She likely assumed I was one of those frail women content to lounge on rose leaves and be fanned all day. Do not let Dixon's fantasies intervene between us, Mamma. Do not, I beg you!"

"Do not be angry with Dixon," Mrs. Hale pleaded anxiously.

Margaret composed herself. "No! I will not. I will strive to

"There are briars besetting every path,
Which call for patient care;
There is a cross in every lot,
And an earnest need for prayer."

Margaret stepped out of the house with a heaviness in her heart that seemed to drag at her feet. Yet, as she walked the length of Milton Street, the familiar air of the city began to lift her spirits. By the time she reached her first turn, her step had lightened, and color returned to her cheeks. For the first time since arriving, she allowed her attention to drift outward from her internal turmoil.

The streets teemed with an unusual energy. Men with hands deep in their pockets sauntered with a casual swagger, while groups of girls laughed loudly, their voices ringing with a boisterous independence that startled her. The rougher elements of the population congregated on the steps of the beer-houses and gin-palaces, smoking and offering sharp commentary on every passerby. Margaret shuddered at the prospect of walking further through these crowded thoroughfares toward the fields she intended to visit. Instead, she decided to seek out Bessy Higgins. It would lack the serene refreshment of a country stroll, but it offered a kind of human connection she desperately needed.

She found Nicholas Higgins seated by the fire, smoking his pipe, while Bessy rocked gently beside him. Nicholas removed his pipe from his lips and stood, pushing his chair aside to make room for Margaret. Leaning casually against the chimney-piece, he greeted her warmly.

"How is Bessy?" Margaret asked.

"Hoo's rather down in the mouth regarding spirits," Nicholas replied, "but hoo's better in health. Hoo doesn't like this strike. Hoo's set on peace and quietness at any price."

"This is the third strike I've seen," Bessy sighed, her voice heavy.

"Well, the third time pays for all," Nicholas declared confidently. "See if we don't damn the masters this time. See if they won't come begging us to return at our own terms. We missed our chance before, I grant you; but this time, we've laid our plans deep."

"Why do you strike?" Margaret asked, genuinely puzzled. "Striking means stopping work until you secure your desired wage, isn't it? I shouldn't wonder at my ignorance; in my homeland, I've never heard of such a thing."

"I wish I were there," Bessy murmured wearily. "But it's not for me to grow weary of strikes. This is the last one I'll witness. Before it ends, I shall be in the Great City—the Holy Jerusalem."

"She's so filled with thoughts of the life to come that she cannot focus on the present," Nicholas explained. "Now, I must do the best I can here. I believe a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush. Thus, we differ in our views on the strike."

"But," Margaret pressed, "if the people in my village struck, as most of them are field laborers, the seeds wouldn't be sown, the hay wouldn't be gathered, and the corn wouldn't be reaped."

"And what then?" Nicholas challenged, resuming his pipe and framing his 'well' as a question.

"Then," Margaret continued, "what becomes of the farmers?"

Nicholas puffed thoughtfully. "I reckon they'd either have to abandon their farms or agree to a fair wage."

"Suppose they couldn't, or wouldn't?" Margaret argued. "They couldn't give up their farms instantly, no matter how much they wished to. Yet, they would have no hay or corn to sell that year. Where would the money come from to pay the laborers' wages the following season?"

Still puffing, Nicholas finally spoke. "I know nothing of your ways down South. I've heard they are a race of spiritless, downtrodden men, utterly crushed by poverty, so dazed by their misery that they don't even realize when they are being exploited. Here, it is not so. We know when we are put upon, and we have too much blood in us to tolerate it. We simply take our hands from our looms and declare, 'You may crush us, but you shall not exploit us, my masters!' And be damned to them, they shall not succeed this time!"

"I wished I lived down South," Bessy interjected.

"There is much to endure there," Margaret admitted. "There are sorrows everywhere. There is grueling physical labor performed with scant nourishment to sustain the body."

"But it is outdoors," Bessy countered. "Away from the endless, suffocating noise and the sickening heat of the mills."

"It rains heavily, and it freezes bitterly," Margaret insisted. "A young person can withstand it, but an old man suffers terribly from rheumatism, becoming bent and withered prematurely. Yet, he must continue working, or he faces the workhouse."

"I thought you were so enamored with the customs of the South," Nicholas teased.

"So I am," Margaret smiled slightly, realizing she had been caught. "I only meant, Bessy, that there is both good and evil in everything. Since you feel the hardship here, I thought it fair that you learn of the hardships down there."

"Do you say they never strike down there?" Nicholas asked abruptly.

"No," Margaret replied. "I believe they possess too much sense."

"And I think," Nicholas retorted, blowing ash onto the floor with such force that his pipe shattered, "that it is not that they have too much sense, but that they lack spirit."

"Oh, Father!" Bessy cried out. "What have you gained by striking? Consider the first strike when Mother died. How we all had to stop work—you suffered the most of all. Yet, many continued working weekly at the same wage until everyone was employed, and some remained beggars for the rest of their lives."

"Aye," Nicholas conceded. "That strike was poorly managed. Fools and untrustworthy men took charge. But you will see; this time will be different."

"But you haven't told me what you are striking for," Margaret persisted.

"You see," Nicholas explained, "there are five or six masters who have maintained their current wages for the past two years, prospering and growing richer. Now, they approach us demanding lower pay. We refuse. We intend to crush them first and see who works for them afterward. They will have killed the goose that laid the golden eggs, I reckon."

"And so you plan to die in revenge against them?" Margaret asked.

"No," he corrected firmly. "I merely anticipate the possibility of dying at my post sooner than yielding. That is what people call noble in a soldier, and why not in a poor weaver?"

"But," Margaret pointed out, "a soldier dies for the Nation—for the sake of others."

He laughed grimly. "My lass, you are but a young woman, but do you not think I can support three people—Bessy, Mary, and myself—on sixteen shillings a week? Do you think I strike for myself? It is just as much for the sake of others as that soldier, only perhaps his cause involves strangers he has never met, whereas I champion the cause of John Boucher, who lives next door with a sickly wife and eight children, none of whom are of factory age. I do not champion only his case, though he is a poor soul who struggles to manage two looms at once. I champion the cause of justice. Why should we receive less now than we did two years ago?"

"Do not ask me," Margaret said, feeling her ignorance keenly. "Ask your masters. Surely they will provide a reason. It cannot be merely an arbitrary decision arrived at without cause."

"You are just a foreigner," Nicholas dismissed her contemptuously. "Know nothing about it. Ask the masters! They would tell us to mind our own business, and they would mind theirs. Our duty is to accept the reduced wage and be grateful; their duty is to drive us down to starvation levels to swell their profits. That is the reality."

"But," Margaret insisted, refusing to yield despite sensing she was irritating him, "perhaps the state of trade prevents them from offering the same remuneration."

"State of trade!" Nicholas exploded. "That is mere masterly humbug. I was speaking of the *rate* of wages. The masters control the state of trade; they wield it like a scarecrow to frighten naughty children into submission. Let me tell you, it is their role—their cue—to beat us down and enrich themselves. It is our duty to stand up and fight—not solely for ourselves, but for those around us, for justice and fairness. We contribute to their profits, and therefore, we deserve to share in spending them. It is not that we crave their coin as much as before; we have saved money. We are resolved to stand or fall together. Not a single man among us will accept less than the Union deems our due. So, I shout 'Hooray for the strike,' and let Thornton, Slickson, Hamper, and their ilk look out for themselves!"

"Thornton!" Margaret exclaimed. "Mr. Thornton of Marlborough Street?"

"Aye! Thornton of Marlborough Mill," he confirmed.

"He is one of the masters you oppose, is he not? What manner of man is he?"

"Have you ever seen a bulldog?" Nicholas asked. "Set a bulldog on its hind legs, dress it in a coat and breeches, and you have John Thornton."

"Nay," Margaret laughed

"My heart revolts within me, and two voices Make themselves audible within my bosom." — Wallenstein.

Upon returning home, Margaret discovered two letters waiting on the table. One was a brief note for her mother; the other, bearing fresh foreign postmarks, was clearly from Aunt Shaw. It was thin, silvery paper that rustled softly as she lifted it. She began to examine it when her father entered abruptly.

"So your mother is already tired and retired!" Mr. Hale exclaimed, his voice tinged with concern. "I feared such a thunderous day was hardly suitable for the doctor's visit. What did he say? Dixon mentioned he spoke to you regarding her condition."

Margaret hesitated. Her father's expression grew increasingly grave and anxious. "Does he not consider her seriously ill?"
