"Not at present," Margaret replied cautiously. "He believes she requires careful nursing. He was very kind and promised to return later to assess how the prescribed remedies are taking effect."

"Only care?" Mr. Hale pressed, his tone sharpening. "Did he not suggest a change of air? Did he not imply that this smoky town is harming her health, Margaret?"

"No," she answered solemnly. "He said nothing of the sort."

"He seemed anxious, I thought," her father noted.

"Doctors often possess that anxious demeanor; it is merely professional," he dismissed lightly, though his eyes betrayed him. Margaret recognized the signs in her father's nervous agitation. Despite his attempts to minimize the situation, the initial shadow of dread had taken root in his mind. He could not dismiss the topic, unable to transition to other subjects. Throughout the evening, he returned to it repeatedly, refusing even the faintest suggestion of unfavorable news, leaving Margaret deeply sorrowful.

"This letter is from Aunt Shaw, Papa," Margaret interrupted gently. "She has arrived in Naples but found it too hot, so she has secured lodgings in Sorrento. However, I doubt she enjoys Italy."

"Did he mention anything regarding her diet?" her father asked immediately.

"It should be nourishing and easy to digest," Margaret explained. "Mamma's appetite seems reasonably good, I believe."

"Yes! And that makes it all the stranger that he considered diet at all," her father mused.

"I asked him, Papa."

Another pause stretched between them. Then Margaret continued, "Aunt Shaw mentions sending me some coral ornaments, Papa. But," she added with a tentative smile, "she fears the Milton dissenters may not appreciate them. Has she formed all her impressions of Dissenters solely from the Quakers?"

"If your mother expresses a wish for anything, ensure you inform me immediately. I am terrified she does not always tell me what she desires. Please, also attend to that young woman Mrs. Thornton mentioned. Had we a competent housekeeper, Dixon could accompany her constantly, and I guarantee we would quickly integrate her into our family if rest alone can heal her. She has been exhausted lately due to the heat and the scarcity of servants. A period of repose will surely restore her completely—will it not, Margaret?"

"I hope so," Margaret replied, but her sadness was palpable. Her father noticed instantly. Pinching her cheek affectionately, he said, "Come, if you appear so pale, I must apply a touch of rouge. Take care of yourself, child, or you'll require the doctor next."

Yet, he remained unsettled throughout the evening. He moved restlessly back and forth on the tips of his toes, peering into the bedroom to confirm whether his wife was still asleep. Margaret's heart ached witnessing his turmoil—his desperate attempt to suppress and suffocate the terrifying fear rising from the depths of his soul. Finally, he returned, slightly reassured.

"She is awake now, Margaret. She smiled warmly upon seeing me stand by her side. It was her old, familiar smile. She stated she feels refreshed and eager for tea. Where is the note for her? She wishes to see it. I shall read it aloud while you prepare the tea."

The note contained a formal invitation from Mrs. Thornton, extending an invitation to Mr., Mrs., and Miss Hale to dine on the twenty-first. Margaret was surprised to find an acceptance being considered, especially after learning of the grim possibilities earlier that day. Nevertheless, the prospect of her husband and daughter attending captivated Mrs. Hale's imagination, even before Margaret revealed the contents of the letter. It represented a welcome diversion from the monotony of her invalid confinement. Clinging to the idea with determined insistence, Mrs. Hale resisted any objections Margaret raised.

"Nonsense, Margaret," Mr. Hale insisted anxiously as he prepared to draft the acceptance the following morning. "If she desires it, I am certain we shall both go willingly. She would never express such a wish unless she genuinely believed herself stronger, truly better than we suspected, would she not, Margaret?"

"Eh, Margaret?" he queried, making a nervous gesture with his hands.

It seemed cruel to deny him the solace he craved. Moreover, his passionate denial of fear nearly inspired hope within Margaret herself.

"I do believe she is improving since last night," she said softly. "Her eyes appear brighter, and her complexion clearer."

"God bless you," her father said earnestly. "But is it true? Yesterday was so stiflingly hot that everyone felt unwell. It was a particularly unfortunate day for Mr. Donaldson to visit."

With that, he departed to his daily duties, which had recently expanded to include preparing lectures for a neighboring Lyceum. He had selected Ecclesiastical Architecture as his topic, aligning more closely with his personal tastes and expertise than with the specific interests of his audience or the nature of the venue. Given that the institution was heavily indebted, they were only too grateful to secure a free series of lectures from an educated and accomplished man like Mr. Hale, regardless of the subject matter.

"Well, Mother," Mr. Thornton asked that evening, "who have accepted your invitations for the twenty-first?"

"Fanny, where are the notes?" Mrs. Thornton called out. "The Slicksons accept, Collingbrooks accept, the Stephenses accept, and the Browns decline. The Hales—both father and daughter—are coming, though Mother is too ill to travel. The Macphersons accept, along with Mr. Horsfall and Mr. Young. I was considering inviting the Porters, as the Browns cannot come."

"Very well," Mr. Thornton replied. "Do you realize, Fanny, that I am genuinely concerned Mrs. Hale is far from well, based on what Dr. Donaldson reported?"

"It is peculiar of them to accept a dinner invitation if she is so severely ill," Fanny remarked.

"I did not say 'severely ill,'" her brother corrected sharply. "I merely said 'far from well.' Perhaps they are unaware of the extent of her condition."

Then, he suddenly recalled that, according to Dr. Donaldson's account, Margaret must certainly be cognizant of the precise state of affairs.

"Very likely they are fully aware of what you said yesterday, John—of the significant advantage it would offer them, specifically Mr. Hale, to be introduced to individuals such as the Stephenses and Collingbrooks."

"I am certain that motive would not influence them," Fanny declared confidently. "No! I believe I understand the situation now."

"John!" Fanny laughed in her characteristic weak, nervous manner. "How you claim to comprehend these Hales, and how you steadfastly refuse to acknowledge that we can understand anything about them. Are they truly so distinct from the majority of people one encounters?"

She did not intend to provoke him, yet if she had, she could scarcely have succeeded more effectively. He bristled in silence, refusing to respond to her query.

"They do not seem extraordinary to me," Mrs. Thornton observed. "He appears to be a decent, worthy man, albeit perhaps too simplistic for commerce—which explains why becoming a clergyman and subsequently a teacher suited him. She is somewhat of a fine lady, bolstered by her illness. As for the girl... she is the only one who perplexes me when I reflect upon her, which I rarely do. She seems possessed of a grandiose notion of elevating herself, and I cannot fathom why. I almost suspect she considers herself superior to her current companions at times. Yet, they are not wealthy; from everything I gather, they have never been affluent."

"And she lacks accomplishments, Mother," Fanny added. "She cannot play music."

"Proceed, Fanny. What further criteria do you demand to elevate her to your standards?"

"Nay, John," his mother interjected, "Fanny's remark caused no offense. I myself heard Miss Hale admit she could not play. If you would leave us in peace, we might eventually learn to appreciate her and recognize her virtues."

"I am certain I never could!" Fanny murmured, shielded by her mother.

Mr. Thornton heard her but chose not to reply. He paced the length of the dining room, wishing fervently that his mother would command the candles be lit so he could engage in reading or writing, thereby halting the conversation. However, he never considered interfering with the minor domestic regulations Mrs. Thornton meticulously maintained, born of her lifelong habits of economy.

"Mother," he stopped abruptly, speaking the truth with brave sincerity, "I wish you would like Miss Hale."

"Why?" she asked, startled by his earnest yet gentle tone. "Are you not contemplating marriage to her? A girl without a penny."

"She would never have me," he replied with a brief laugh.

"No, I do not believe she would," his mother agreed.

"As angels in some brighter dreams Call to the soul when man doth sleep, So some strange thoughts transcend our wonted themes, And into glory peep." — Henry Vaughan.

Mrs. Hale watched the preparations for the Thornton dinner party with a mixture of amusement and genuine interest. She approached the event with the simple, anticipatory curiosity of a child eager to have every detail of a promised joy described beforehand. Yet, the monotonous existence of an invalid often shrinks the world until it resembles a child's perception; lacking a sense of proportion, one believes the walls and curtains enclosing their daily routine must necessarily dwarf everything hidden beyond them. Furthermore, Mrs. Hale carried within her the dormant vanities of her youth. Having felt the sharp sting of humiliation when she became a poor clergyman's wife, those youthful aspirations had been smothered and suppressed, though never extinguished. Now, she delighted in imagining Margaret dressed for a gala, discussing attire with an unsettled anxiety that amused Margaret, who had known more of society during her single year in Harley Street than her mother had in twenty-five years of Helstone.

"Then you intend to wear your white silk?" Mrs. Hale asked. "Are you certain it will fit? It has been nearly a year since Edith was married!"

"Oh, yes, Mamma," Margaret replied cheerfully. "Mrs. Murray made it, and it is sure to be correct. It might be a straw's breadth shorter or longer-waisted depending on whether I have gained or lost weight, but I do not think I have altered in the least."

"Would it not be safer to let Dixon inspect it? Fabric may have gone yellow from lying idle."

"If you prefer, Mamma. But if the worst comes to the worst, I have a lovely pink gauze Aunt Shaw gave me only two or three months prior to Edith's marriage. That surely hasn't yellowed."

"No! But it may have faded."

"Well, then, I have a green silk. I feel as though I am drowning in an embarrassment of riches."

"I wish I knew exactly what you ought to wear," Mrs. Hale said, her voice betraying nervousness.

Margaret's demeanor shifted instantly. "Shall I try them on one after another, Mamma, so you can decide which you prefer?"

"But—yes! Perhaps that is the best plan."

Off went Margaret. Though dressed in finery at such an unusual hour, she felt a sudden inclination toward mischief—to make her rich white silk balloon absurdly, or to retreat backward from her mother as if she were royalty. However, finding that these whims were viewed merely as interruptions to serious business and annoying to her mother, she quickly settled into a grave and sedate composure. She could not comprehend why the world should fuss so over her dress. Later that afternoon, however, when she mentioned her engagement to Bessy Higgins regarding the servant Mrs. Thornton intended to inquire about, Bessy's spirits lifted dramatically.

"Dear! Are you going to dine at Thornton's in Marlborough Mills?"

"Yes, Bessy. Why are you so surprised?"

"Oh, I dunno. But they associate with all the finest people in Milton."

"And you do not consider us quite the finest folk in Milton, do you, Bessy?"

Bessy's cheeks flushed slightly as her thought was so easily read.

"Well," she said hesitantly, "you see, people here value money greatly. And I reckon you haven't acquired much."

"No," Margaret admitted calmly. "That is very true. But we are educated people, raised among the educated. Is there anything remarkable about being invited to dinner by a man who acknowledges himself inferior to my father, coming to him for instruction? I do not mean to fault Mr. Thornton. Few former drapers' assistants could have achieved what he has."

"But can you entertain a dinner party in your small house? Thornton's home is three times as large."

"Well, I believe we could manage to host Mr. Thornton, as you call it. Perhaps not in such a vast room, nor with so many guests. But I suppose we have not considered it in that light."

"I never thought you'd dine with the Thorntons," Bessy repeated. "Why, the Mayor himself eats there, along with Members of Parliament and everyone."

"I think I could uphold the honor of meeting the Mayor of Milton."

"But those ladies dress so grand!" Bessy remarked, casting an anxious glance at Margaret's printed gown, which her Milton-born eyes valued at sevenpence a yard.

Margaret's face brightened into a merry laugh. "Thank you, Bessy, for thinking so kindly of my appearance among such smart company. I possess plenty of grand gowns—a week ago, I would have said they were far too extravagant for anything I would ever need here. But since I am to dine at Mr. Thornton's and perhaps meet the Mayor, I shall certainly wear my very best gown."

"What will you wear?" Bessy asked, feeling somewhat relieved.

"White silk," Margaret answered. "It was a gown I wore to a cousin's wedding a year ago."

"That'll do!" Bessy declared, sinking back into her chair. "I would be loath to have you looked down upon."

"Oh! I shall be fine enough, if that saves me from being looked down upon in Milton."

"I wish I could see you dressed up," Bessy sighed. "I reckon you aren't what people would call pretty; you lack the red and white for that. But do you know, I have dreamed of you long before I ever saw you."

"Nonsense, Bessy!"

"Ay, but I did. Your very face—looking with your clear, steadfast eyes out of the darkness, your hair blown back from your brow, radiating outward like rays around your forehead, which was just as smooth and straight as it is now. And you always came to give me strength, which I seemed to gather from your deep, comforting eyes. You were dressed in shining raiment—exactly as you are dressing today. So, you see, it was you!"

"Nay, Bessy," Margaret said gently, "it was but a dream."

"And why might I not dream a dream in my affliction as well as others? Did not many in the Bible? Ay, and see visions too! Even my father thinks deeply of dreams. I tell you again, I saw you as plainly, coming swiftly toward me, your hair flying back with the speed of motion, just as it grows naturally, standing off slightly; and the white shining dress you have obtained to wear. Let me come and see you in it. I want to see you and touch you as if you were truly present in my dream."

"My dear Bessy, it is quite a fancy of yours."

"Fancy or no fancy—you have come, as I knew you would, when I perceived your movement in my dream. And when you are near me, I reckon I feel calmer in my mind and comforted, just as a fire warms one on a dreary day. You said it is on the twenty-first. Please God, I shall come to see you."

"Oh, Bessy! You may come and welcome; but do not talk so—it really makes me sad. It does indeed."

"Then I shall keep it to myself, if I have to bite my tongue out. Not that it is untrue for all that."

Margaret fell silent. Finally, she said, "Let us discuss it sometime, if you believe it to be true. But not now. Tell me, has your father turned out?"

"Ay!" Bessy replied heavily, her tone shifting drastically from moments before. "He and many others—all of Hamper's men, and countless besides. The women are as savage as the men this time. Food prices are high, and they must have food for their children, I reckon. Suppose Thorntons sent them their meals out—the same money spent on potatoes and meal could quiet many a crying baby and soothe a mother's heart for a while!"

"Don't speak so!" Margaret exclaimed. "You will make me feel wicked and guilty for attending this dinner."

"No!" Bessy insisted. "Some are predestined for sumptuous feasts and purple linens—you may be one of them. Others toil and moil their entire lives, and even the dogs are not pitied in our days as they were in the days of Lazarus. But if you ask me to cool your tongue with the tip of my finger, I will cross the great gulf to you simply for the thought of what you have been to me here."

"Bessy, you are very feverish! I can tell it by the touch of your hand as well as by your words. It will not suffice in that dreadful day that some of us have been beggars here while others have been rich—we shall not be judged by that poor accident, but by our faithful following of Christ."

Margaret rose, fetched some water, soaked her pocket-handkerchief, and laid the cool dampness on Bessy's forehead, beginning to rub her stone-cold feet. Bessy closed her eyes and allowed herself to be soothed. Eventually, she murmured, "You would have been deafened out of your five wits, as well as me, if you had one body after another coming in to ask for Father, staying to tell me each their tale. Some spoke of deadly hatred, making my blood run cold with the terrible things they said of the masters—but more, being women, kept complaining, complaining (with tears streaming down their cheeks, never wiped away

"Old and young, boy, let 'em all eat, I have it; Let 'em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not." — *Rollo, Duke of Normandy.*

Margaret returned home so weighed down by the sights and sounds of the day that she struggled vainly to summon the energy required for the duties awaiting her. Her mother, confined to the house, relied upon Margaret's brief excursions as the sole source of news, expecting a constant stream of cheerful chatter.

"And can your factory friend come on Thursday to see you dressed?"

"She was so ill I never thought of asking her," Margaret replied, her voice dripping with sorrow.

"Dear! Everyone seems ill nowadays, I think," remarked Mrs. Hale, a flicker of envy crossing her features, common among those bedridden and dependent on others' health. "But it must be terribly sad to be sick in one of those narrow back alleys."

(Kindness overriding her own discomfort, and the old customs of Helstone resurfacing in her mind.)

"It is hard enough here. What could you possibly send her, Margaret? Mr. Thornton has sent me some of his vintage port since you left. Do you think a bottle would do her any good?"

"No, Mother! I doubt they are truly destitute—they certainly didn't sound like it. Besides, Bessy's ailment is consumption; she wouldn't benefit from wine. Perhaps I might bring her a jar of preserves made from our beloved Helstone fruits. No! There is another family I wish to assist—"

"Oh, Mother, Mother!" Margaret suddenly broke through the self-imposed restraint she had maintained upon entering, spilling the raw details of her encounter at Higgins's cottage. "How am I to appear in my finest attire and attend these fashionable gatherings after witnessing such misery today?"

The revelation distressed Mrs. Hale profoundly. Irritated and restless until compelled to act, she ordered Margaret immediately to pack a basket in the drawing-room to be dispatched to the family at once. She was nearly incensed when Margaret suggested it mattered little if the gift arrived until morning, noting that Higgins had already secured their immediate needs and that she had left funds with Bessy. Accusing her daughter of callousness, Mrs. Hale refused to pause until the basket was delivered.

Only then did she sigh, reflecting, "After all, we may have erred. Last time Mr. Thornton visited, he remarked that true friends do not aid in prolonging struggles by supporting the riots. Wasn't that Boucher involved in a riot?"

When Mr. Hale ascended the stairs later, fresh from a lesson with Mr. Thornton that had devolved into their usual discourse, his wife posed the question. In her current agitation, Margaret cared little whether their charity inadvertently extended the conflict; her thoughts were too clouded. Mr. Hale listened, striving for judicial calm, recalling arguments that had seemed undeniable moments prior. He reached an uneasy compromise. While acknowledging his wife and daughter's correctness in this specific instance, he conceded Mr. Thornton's broader point: if the strike dragged on, masters would eventually recruit labor from afar, or machines would render human hands obsolete. Thus, generally speaking, refusing assistance that propped up foolish stubbornness was the kindest course. Yet, regarding Boucher specifically, he resolved to visit him first thing the following morning to ascertain what aid was feasible.

True to his word, Mr. Hale departed the next day. Finding Boucher absent, he held a lengthy conversation with his wife, promising to secure an infirmary order for her. Observing the abundance Mrs. Hale had provided—which the children, acting as masters in their father's absence, had spent lavishly—he returned with a report far more reassuring than Margaret had dared hope. Indeed, her previous night's grim description had primed her father's imagination to exaggerate the severity of conditions; consequently, he painted a picture of recovery that was rosier than reality warranted.

"I shall return tomorrow to see the man himself," Mr. Hale declared. "I find it difficult to reconcile these dwellings with the cottages of Helstone. Here, furniture exists that our laborers could scarcely dream of purchasing, and foods considered luxuries elsewhere are staples. Yet, with wages halted, these families seem to have no recourse but the pawnbroker. One must adopt a new standard of living here in Milton."

Bessy showed slight improvement that day, though her weakness was such that she appeared to have forgotten her earlier fervent desire to witness Margaret's transformation—unless that impulse stemmed from a feverish delirium. Margaret found herself contrasting this peculiar preparation for a social event she dreaded against the joyful, girlish trousseaus she and Edith had orchestrated barely a year prior. Her only solace in adorning herself now was the knowledge that her mother would delight in viewing her. She flushed when Dixon opened the drawing-room door to solicit approval.

"Miss Hale looks radiant, ma'am," Dixon observed. "Mrs. Shaw's coral couldn't have chosen a finer spot. It provides the perfect splash of color, ma'am. Without it, Miss Margaret would have been far too pale."

Margaret's thick black hair, unsuitable for braiding, was instead coiled tightly around her head in massive silken rings resembling a crown, culminating in a large spiral knot at the nape. Two substantial coral pins, shaped like slender arrows, anchored the weight. Her white silk sleeves were bound with cords of matching material, and heavy coral beads rested beneath the curve of her milky throat.

"Oh, Margaret! How I long to accompany you to one of the old Barrington assemblies," her mother gushed, envisioning herself as Lady Beresford escorting her daughter. "Taking you as she used to take me."

Margaret kissed her mother, suppressing a smile despite her low spirits. "I would prefer staying at home with you, Mother. Much preferred."

"Nonsense, darling! Ensure you observe the dinner closely. I shall love hearing how they manage affairs in Milton. Especially the second course, dear. Notice what they serve instead of game."

Had Mrs. Hale witnessed the opulence of the table and its settings, she would have been less interested and more astounded. For Margaret, possessing a London-refined palate, the array of delicacies felt oppressive; half the provision would have sufficed for a tighter, more elegant presentation. Yet, adhering to Mrs. Thornton's rigid code of hospitality, every guest was entitled to partake of each distinct dish if inclined. Though careless in her own daily habits, setting a feast before her guests was a point of immense pride for Mrs. Thornton. Her son shared this sentiment. Having known nothing but societies revolving around magnificent repasts, he denied himself the trivial expense of a sixpence and regretted sending the invitations, yet he welcomed the chance to witness the old grandeur of preparation.

Margaret and her father arrived first. Mr. Hale was punctually anxious. The drawing-room was empty save for Mrs. Thornton and Fanny. Covers were removed, revealing walls draped in yellow silk damask and carpets blooming with brilliant flowers. Ornamentation filled every corner, creating a visual fatigue that stood in stark contrast to the bare, ugly view of the mill yard outside, where wide gates stood open for carriages. The mill towered to the left, casting a premature shadow over the summer evening.

"My son was detained by business until the last moment. He arrives shortly, Mr. Hale. Please, take a seat."

Mr. Hale stood by a window. Turning away, he queried, "Do you not find such proximity to the mill somewhat disagreeable at times?"

Mrs. Thornton straightened her posture. "Never. I have not grown so refined as to wish to forget the source of my son's wealth and power. Moreover, there is no other factory in Milton. A single room spans two hundred and twenty square yards."

"I meant the smoke and noise—the constant comings and goings of workers might prove irritating!"

"I concur with you, Mr. Hale!" interjected Fanny. "There is a perpetual scent of steam and oil, and the noise is utterly deafening."

"I have heard noises termed 'music' far more deafening," Mrs. Thornton retorted. "The engine-room sits at the street end; we hear it only in summer when windows are open. As for the constant hum of the workforce, it disturbs me no more than the buzzing of a bee-hive. If I ponder it, I associate it with my son, feeling how everything belongs to him, guided by his head. Currently, silence reigns; the hands have proven ungrateful enough to strike, as you likely heard. However, the very business I mentioned concerns the steps he intends to take to teach them their place."

Dark anger settled upon her stern face as she spoke, failing to lift even when Mr. Thornton entered. She instantly perceived the burden of care weighing upon him, though his greeting to the guests remained cheerfully cordial. He shook Margaret's hand—a first meeting between them, though she remained unaware. He inquired after Mrs. Hale, receiving Mr. Hale's optimistic report, and glanced at Margaret to gauge her agreement. Seeing no dissent, he was struck anew by her beauty. Never had he seen her so attired, yet this elegance suited her noble figure and serene countenance perfectly; she seemed destined to wear such splendor always.

He watched his sister, Fanny, fidgeting restlessly with her gown, her eyes wandering aimlessly, contrasting sharply with Margaret's steady gaze fixed on a single point, radiating a gentle repose. He noted the soft curve of Margaret's red lips, slightly parted in attentive listening, and the graceful sweep of her head from the light-catch

"On earth is known to none / The smile that is not sister to a tear." — Elliott.

Margaret and her father walked home together. The night was fine, the streets immaculate, and clad in her pretty white silk dress, kilted up to her knee like the gown of Leezie Lindsay in the ballad, she moved forward with her father, eager to dance along with the crisp, refreshing air.

"I rather think Thornton is not entirely at ease regarding this strike," Margaret observed. "He seemed very anxious tonight."

"I should not be surprised," Mr. Hale replied. "Yet he spoke with his customary composure to the others when they proposed various solutions just before we departed."

"So he did after dinner as well. It takes a great deal to disturb his cool demeanor; however, his face betrays anxiety."

"I would be anxious in his place," Mr. Hale admitted. "He must perceive the growing fury and barely suppressed hatred within his workforce, who view him as the 'hard man' described in Scripture—not necessarily unjust, but unfeeling. He stands firm on his rights with a clarity that seems unnatural given our own petty claims in the sight of the Almighty. I am glad you noticed his worry. Recalling Boucher's half-mad words and actions makes me unable to conceive how calmly Mr. Thornton could have spoken."

"In the first instance, I am not as convinced as you are of Mr. Boucher's total despair. In that moment, he was undoubtedly distressed. However, there is often a mysterious reservoir of funds within these Unions. Furthermore, based on what you said, it appears Mr. Boucher is of a passionate, demonstrative nature, giving free rein to every emotion he feels."

"Oh, Papa!"

"Well! My only wish is that you render justice to Mr. Thornton, whom I suspect possesses a nature diametrically opposed to Mr. Boucher's—a man far too proud to display his feelings. That is precisely the character I expected you to admire, Margaret."

"So I do—I should; yet I do not feel entirely certain of the existence of those hidden emotions. He is a man of immense strength of character and unusual intellect, considering the limited advantages he has enjoyed."

"Not so few," Mr. Hale corrected gently. "He has lived a practical life from a very young age, constantly called upon to exercise judgment and self-control. Such experiences develop a specific facet of the intellect. Granted, he lacks some historical knowledge that provides the truest foundation for predicting the future; but he recognizes this deficiency, and that awareness is significant. You are becoming somewhat prejudiced against Mr. Thornton, Margaret."

"He is the first manufacturer—the first person engaged in trade—I have ever had the opportunity to study closely, Papa. He is my first olive; let me make a face while I swallow it. I know he is good-hearted, and eventually, I shall like the kind. I think I am already beginning to do so. I was deeply interested by the conversation among the gentlemen earlier, though I understood only fragments of it. I felt quite uncomfortable when Miss Thornton came to escort me to the other end of the room, assuring me I would be uneasy being the sole lady among so many men. I had never considered it until then, so engrossed was I in listening; besides, the ladies were so dull, Papa—oh, so dull! Yet, I suppose it was clever. It reminded me of our old game where each of us had to incorporate a set number of nouns into a sentence."

"What do you mean, child?" Mr. Hale asked.

"Why, they selected nouns signifying objects indicative of wealth—housekeepers, under-gardeners, the extent of glass, valuable lace, diamonds, and similar items—and each fashioned her speech to include them in the most pleasing, seemingly accidental manner possible."

"You will be just as proud of your single servant once you acquire her, if everything Mrs. Thornton says about her proves true."

"To be sure, I shall. I felt like a profound hypocrite tonight, sitting there in my white silk gown with idle hands, remembering all the diligent, thorough housework performed today. They treated me as a fine lady, I'm sure."

"Even I was foolish enough to think you looked like a lady, my dear," Mr. Hale said, smiling quietly.

However, smiles vanished instantly, replaced by pale, trembling expressions when they beheld Dixon's face as she opened the door.

"Oh, Master!—Oh, Miss Margaret! Thank God you are here! Dr. Donaldson is here. The servant next door fetched him because the charwoman has gone home. She is better now; but, oh, Sir! I thought she would have died an hour ago!"

Mr. Hale seized Margaret's arm to steady himself from collapsing. He gazed at her face, noting an expression of surprise and extreme sorrow, yet lacking the paralyzing agony of terror that constricted his own unprepared heart. She knew more than he did, yet she listened with a hopeless look of awed apprehension.

"Oh! I should not have left her—wicked daughter that I am!" Margaret moaned, supporting her trembling father's hurried steps upstairs.

Dr. Donaldson awaited them on the landing. "She is improving now," he whispered. "The opiate has taken effect. The spasms were severe; no wonder they frightened your maid, but she will rally this time."

"This time!" Mr. Hale cried. "Let me go to her!"

Half an hour prior, Mr. Hale had been a middle-aged man; now his vision blurred, his senses wavered, and his walk tottered as if he were seventy years old. Dr. Donaldson took his arm and guided him into the bedroom. Margaret followed closely.

There lay her mother, bearing an unmistakable look upon her face. Perhaps she was better now; she slept, but Death had claimed her signature, and it was evident he would soon return to take full possession. Mr. Hale stared at her for some time in silence. Then he began to tremble violently. Turning away from Dr. Donaldson's anxious ministrations, he groped for the door, unable to see it despite the several candles burning brightly in the sudden panic. He staggered into the drawing-room and searched for a chair. Dr. Donaldson wheeled one toward him and seated him.

He felt his pulse. "Speak to him, Miss Hale. We must rouse him."

"Papa!" Margaret cried, her voice wild with pain. "Papa! Speak to me!"

Speculation returned to his eyes, and he made a tremendous effort. "Margaret, did you know of this? Oh, it was cruel of you!"

"No, Sir, it was not cruel!" Dr. Donaldson replied with swift decisiveness. "Miss Hale acted under my instructions. There may have been a mistake, but it was not cruel. Your wife will be a different creature tomorrow, I trust. She suffered spasms, as I anticipated, though I did not inform Miss Hale of my fears. She has taken the opiate I brought; she will have a long, restorative sleep, and tomorrow that look which has alarmed you so greatly will have passed."

"But not the disease?"

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. Her bowed head and face raised in silent plea revealed that this keen observer of human nature believed the whole truth should be spoken.

"Not the disease. With all our vaunted skill, we cannot touch the disease. We can only delay its progress and alleviate the pain it causes. Be a man, Sir—a Christian. Have faith in the immortality of the soul, which no pain, no mortal disease, can assail or touch!"

But the only response he received was the choked words, "You have never been married, Dr. Donaldson; you don't know what it is," accompanied by deep, manly sobs that echoed through the stillness of the night like heavy pulses of agony.

Margaret knelt beside him, caressing him with tearful tenderness. No one, not even Dr. Donaldson, knew how the time passed. Mr. Hale was the first to dare address the necessities of the present moment.

"What must we do?" he asked. "Tell us both. Margaret is my staff—my right hand."

Dr. Donaldson offered his clear, sensible directions. There was no cause for fear tonight—nay, perhaps even peace for tomorrow and many days thereafter. Yet, there was no enduring hope of recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to retire to bed, leaving only one person to watch the slumber, which he hoped would remain undisturbed. He promised to return early in the morning. With a warm, kindly handshake, he departed.

They exchanged few words; exhaustion from their terror rendered them incapable of more than deciding on immediate action. Mr. Hale was resolved to stay awake through the night, and all Margaret could achieve was persuading him to rest on the drawing-room sofa. Dixon stoutly and bluntly refused to go to bed. As for Margaret, it was simply impossible for her to leave her mother, regardless of what doctors worldwide might say about "husbanding resources" or requiring "only one watcher."

Thus, Dixon sat, staring, blinking, drooping, and jerking herself upright again, finally surrendering the battle and falling into a fair snore. Margaret had removed her gown, tossing it aside with impatient disgust, and donned her dressing-gown. She felt as though she could never sleep again; her entire senses were acutely vital, endowed with double keenness solely for the purpose of vigilance. Every sight, sound—even every thought—touched some nerve to the very quick.

For over two hours, she heard her

"Work grew scarce, while bread grew dear, And wages lessened, too; For Irish hordes were bidders here, Our half-paid work to do." — *Corn Law Rhymes*

Margaret was ushered into the drawing-room. The room had reverted to its usual state of disarray, draped in heavy coverings. The windows stood half-open against the stifling heat, the Venetian blinds casting a gray, grim light that reflected from the pavement below. This distorted illumination, mixed with the greenish hue filtering from above, rendered even Margaret's own reflection in the mirror ghastly and wan. She sat and waited, but no one appeared. Occasionally, the wind seemed to carry the distant, multitudinous sounds closer, only to die away into a profound, unnerving stillness.

Finally, Fanny entered.

"Mamma will be with you directly, Miss Hale," Fanny began, her voice tight. "She wanted me to apologize for the delay. You likely know my brother has imported laborers from Ireland, which has enraged the Milton locals terribly—as if he lacked the right to hire workers wherever he could. These foolish people here simply refuse to work for him. Consequently, they've terrified these poor Irish starvelings with threats, so we dare not let them out. You'll find them huddled in the top room of the mill; they're sleeping there to protect them from the brutes who won't let them work. Mamma is arranging their food, and John is talking to them, as some of the women are crying to return. Ah! Here comes Mamma!"

Mrs. Thornton entered, her face etched with a black, stern severity that made Margaret feel she had arrived at a disastrous moment. Yet, she had come solely to fulfill her mother's expressed wishes regarding necessities during her illness. Mrs. Thornton's brow furrowed and her mouth set firmly as Margaret spoke gently of her mother's restlessness and Dr. Donaldson's recommendation for a water-bed. Silence stretched between them until Mrs. Thornton abruptly sprang up.

"They're at the gates! Call John, Fanny—bring him in from the mill! They're at the gates! They'll batter them down! Call John, I say!"

Simultaneously, the gathering tramp she had been ignoring in favor of Margaret's words reached the walls just outside. An increasing din of angry voices erupted behind the wooden barrier, shaking it as if the unseen, maddened crowd used their bodies as battering rams. They retreated slightly only to surge forward again with greater unity and steady impetus, their rhythmic pounding making the strong gates quiver like reeds in a gale.

Women gathered round the windows, transfixed by the terrifying scene. Mrs. Thornton, the female servants, and Margaret were all present. Fanny had fled upstairs screaming as if pursued, collapsing onto the sofa in hysterical sobs. Mrs. Thornton scanned the room for her son, who remained in the mill. He emerged, looked up at the pale cluster of faces, and offered a smile of brave encouragement before locking the factory door. He then signaled a servant to unlock his own door, which Fanny had hastily barred in her panic. Mrs. Thornton stepped forward herself.

To the infuriated multitude outside, the sound of his familiar, commanding voice tasted like blood. Until now, they had been voiceless, needing every breath for their strenuous efforts to breach the gates. Now, hearing him speak within, they unleashed a fierce, unearthly groan so intense that even Mrs. Thornton turned white with fear as she led the way into the room. Mr. Thornton entered, slightly flushed, his eyes gleaming with the fire of danger. With a proud look of defiance, he appeared a noble figure, though not necessarily handsome. Margaret, who had always feared her courage might fail her in an emergency, proving her a coward, found herself forgotten in the face of this real, pressing terror. Instead, she felt an intense, painful sympathy for the moment.

Mr. Thornton stepped forward frankly.

"I'm sorry, Miss Hale, you've visited us at this unfortunate hour, when you may be involved in whatever risk we face. Mother! Hadn't you better retire to the back rooms? I'm not certain they haven't breached the path from Pinner's Lane into the stable-yard, but if not, you'd be safer there. Go, Jane!" he commanded the upper servant.

She departed, followed by the others.

"I stay here!" his mother declared. "Where you are, I remain." Indeed, retreat to the back rooms was futile; the crowd had surrounded the outbuildings at the rear, emitting an awful, threatening roar. The servants scrambled into the garrets amidst cries and shrieks. Mr. Thornton smiled scornfully at their panic. He glanced at Margaret, who stood alone by the window nearest the factory. Her eyes glittered, her cheeks and lips deepened in color. Sensing his gaze, she turned to him, voicing a question that had long troubled her mind:

"Where are the poor imported workpeople? Are they in the factory?"

"Yes," he replied. "I left them cowering in a small room at the head of a back staircase, telling them to flee down there if they sensed an attack on the mill doors. But it isn't them they want—it is me."

"When can the soldiers arrive?" his mother asked, her voice low but unsteady.

He consulted his watch with the same steady composure he applied to everything. After a brief calculation, he said, "If Williams left immediately as instructed and didn't have to navigate through the crowd, it will be twenty minutes yet."

"Twenty minutes!" his mother exclaimed, her terror finally breaking through in her tone.

"Shut down the windows instantly, Mother! The gates won't withstand another shock. Close that window, Miss Hale."

Margaret obeyed, then assisted her mother's trembling fingers. For several minutes, there was a tense pause in the unseen street. Mrs. Thornton watched her son's countenance with wild anxiety, seeking an interpretation of the sudden silence. His face was carved into rigid lines of contemptuous defiance; neither hope nor fear could be read there.

Fanny raised herself weakly. "Are they gone?" she whispered.

"Gone!" he replied. "Listen!"

They all heard the single, great strain of breath, the creak of wood slowly giving way, the wrench of iron, and the mighty crash of the ponderous gates falling. Fanny stood up, tottering, took a step toward her mother, and collapsed into her arms in a fainting fit. Mrs. Thornton lifted her with a strength born of sheer will and carried her away.

"Thank God!" Mr. Thornton said, watching her exit. "Would you not prefer to go upstairs, Miss Hale?"

Margaret's lips formed a "No," but he could not hear her, for the tramp of innumerable footsteps pounded right beneath the house wall, accompanied by a fierce growl of low, angry voices possessing a ferocious murmur of satisfaction more dreadful than their baffled cries moments before.

"Never mind!" he said, attempting to encourage her. "I regret that you were trapped in this alarm, but it cannot last long. A few more minutes, and the soldiers will be here."

"Oh, God!" Margaret cried suddenly. "There is Boucher! I recognize his face, though he is livid with rage—he is fighting to reach the front! Look! Look!"

"Who is Boucher?" Mr. Thornton asked coolly, moving close to the window to identify the man causing such concern. Upon seeing him, the crowd set up a yell that defied description—not human, but akin to the demonic hunger of a wild beast denied its prey. Even he drew back momentarily, dismayed by the intensity of hatred he had provoked.

"Let them yell!" he said. "In five minutes more... I only hope my poor Irishmen aren't terrified out of their wits by such fiendish noise. Keep your courage for five minutes, Miss Hale."

"Don't be afraid for me," she said hastily. "But what happens in five minutes? Can you do nothing to soothe these poor creatures? It is awful to witness."

"The soldiers will be here directly, and that will restore their reason."

"To reason!" Margaret snapped. "What kind of reason?"

"The only reason that works on men who turn themselves into wild beasts. By heaven! They've turned to the mill-door!"

"Mr. Thornton," Margaret said, shaking with passion, "go down this instant, if you are not a coward. Go down and face them like a man. Save these poor strangers whom you have decoyed here. Speak to your workmen as if they were human beings. Speak kindly. Do not let the soldiers come in and slaughter poor creatures driven mad. I see one there who is already mad. If you possess any courage or nobility, go out and speak to them, man to man!"

He turned to look at her as she spoke. A dark cloud settled over his face as he listened. He clenched his teeth at her words.

"I will go. Perhaps I may ask you to accompany me downstairs and bar the door behind me; my mother and sister will need that protection."

"Oh! Mr. Thornton! I do not know—I may be wrong—only—"

But he was gone. He was downstairs in the hall, having unbolted the front door. All she could do was follow him quickly, fasten it behind him, and climb the stairs again with a sick heart and a dizzy head. Once more, she took her place by the farthest window. He was on the steps below,

Margaret had scarcely been gone five minutes when Mr. Thornton burst into the dining-room, his face flushed with a heat that seemed to glow from within. "I couldn't come sooner," he panted, his eyes darting around the room. "The superintendent would have delayed me. Where is she?"

He scanned the empty chairs, then turned his gaze almost fiercely upon his mother, who was calmly rearranging the displaced furniture. She offered no immediate answer.

"Where is Miss Hale?" he demanded again.

"Gone home," Mrs. Thornton replied, her tone somewhat curt.

"Gone home!" he repeated, pacing restlessly. "Yes, she was much better. I don't believe it was anything serious; some people simply faint at the slightest shock."

"I'm sorry she's gone," he muttered, unable to settle. "She wasn't fit to travel."

"She insisted she was," his mother countered. "Mr. Lowe confirmed it. I fetched him myself."

"Thank you, Mother." He paused, extending his hand in a gesture of gratitude. She ignored it entirely.

"What have you done with the Irishmen?" she asked abruptly.

"Sent them to the Dragon for a meal, poor wretches. Fortunately, I managed to catch Father Grady and persuaded him to stay and talk to them, preventing a mass departure. But how did Miss Hale manage to get home? I'm certain she couldn't have walked."

"She had a cab. Everything was handled properly, right down to the fare. Let's discuss something else; she's already caused enough commotion."

"I wouldn't have known where to turn but for her," Mr. Thornton insisted.

"Have you become so helpless that you require a girl to defend you?" Mrs. Thornton retorted with disdain.

He blushed deeply. "Few girls would have taken the blows intended for me—with such genuine goodwill, too."

"A girl in love will do wonders," she replied dryly.

"Mother!" he cried, stepping forward, then halting as he struggled to contain a surge of passion. The intensity of his emotion startled her. She sensed the storm brewing within him but could not decipher its nature. Was it rage? His eyes burned, his frame expanded, and his breathing grew ragged. It was a chaotic blend of joy, fury, pride, relief, and desperate uncertainty. Yet, she remained baffled. The mere presence of such raw, unshared feeling unsettled her profoundly.

Turning her back to him, she moved to the sideboard, retrieved a duster from a drawer, and pretended to polish a spot of eau de Cologne on the sofa arm. She lingered longer than necessary, keeping her back turned. When she finally spoke, her voice sounded strained and artificial.

"You've taken steps regarding the rioters, I assume? Do you anticipate further violence? Where were the police? Always absent when needed!"

"On the contrary," he answered, "I saw several of them struggling ineffectually near the gate, and more arrived just as the yard cleared. I could have arrested some of them then, had I been quicker-witted. But there will be no trouble; plenty of witnesses can identify them."

"But won't they return tonight?"

"I intend to arrange a sufficient guard. I'll meet Captain Hanbury at the station in half an hour."

"You ought to have some tea first."

"Tea! Yes, I suppose so. It's half-past six, and I may be gone for a while. Don't wait up for me, Mother."

"Do you expect me to retire before ensuring your safety?"

"Well, perhaps not." He hesitated. "But if I have time, I'll stop by Crampton after settling matters with the police and speaking to Hamper and Clarkson."

Their eyes locked, staring intently at one another for a full minute.

"Why are you going via Crampton?" she asked softly.

"To inquire after Miss Hale."

"I will send someone. Williams can deliver the water-bed she requested and check on her condition."

"I must go myself."

"Not merely to ask how she is?"

"No, not just that. I wish to thank her for standing between me and the mob."

"What brought you down there at all? You were throwing your head into the lion's mouth!"

He glanced sharply at her, noting her ignorance of the private exchange he'd shared with Margaret in the drawing-room, and deflected with another query: "Will you be frightened to remain alone until I summon the police? Or should we send Williams now so they arrive before we finish tea? Time is critical. I must depart in fifteen minutes."

Mrs. Thornton withdrew from the room. Her servants noted the confusion in her usual sharp commands; she appeared uncertain and distracted. Mr. Thornton remained in the dining-room, ostensibly planning his visit to the police office, though his thoughts were entirely consumed by Margaret. The memory of her arms around his neck, the soft clinging that sent waves of color through his cheeks, seemed to blur everything else into vagueness.

The tea service was stiflingly quiet, broken only by Fanny's incessant narration of her own terror—how she had been alarmed, then relieved, then seized by sickness and trembling limbs.

"There, that's enough," Mr. Thornton interrupted, rising. "The reality was sufficient for me."

He prepared to leave when his mother placed a hand on his arm.

"You will return here before going to the Hales'," she whispered anxiously.

Fanny murmured to herself, "I know what I know. Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?"

"John, come back to me for this one evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale. But that isn't why. Tomorrow, you will... Come back tonight, John!"

She rarely pleaded with her son; her pride forbade it. Yet, she had never failed.

"I will return immediately after finishing my business. You will surely inquire after them? After her?"

Mrs. Thornton was neither a conversationalist nor a patient listener while her son was away. However, upon his return, her attention sharpened keenly to every detail of his precautions against future outrages. He saw his objective clearly: punishment and suffering were the natural consequences for the rioters. All that was required to protect property and assert the proprietor's will was a clean, sharp execution of justice.

"Mother!" he exclaimed suddenly, breaking a silence in which she had momentarily forgotten Margaret. "You know what I have to say to Miss Hale tomorrow?"

"Yes!" she replied, looking up. "You can hardly avoid it."

"Do otherwise? I don't understand."

"I mean that, having allowed her feelings to overwhelm her, you are bound by honor—"

"Honor?" he scoffed. "I'm afraid honor has nothing to do with it. 'Her feelings overwhelmed her!' What feelings do you mean?"

"Nay, John, no need to be angry. Didn't she rush down and cling to you to save you from danger?"

"She did!" he admitted.

"But, Mother," he continued, stopping abruptly before her. "I dare not hope. I have never been faint-hearted before, yet I cannot believe such a creature cares for me."

"Don't be foolish, John. 'Such a creature'! Why, she might be a duchess's daughter in your estimation. What greater proof could you desire of her affection? I suspect she has struggled against her aristocratic upbringing, but I admire her for seeing clearly at last. It is a significant concession on her part," Mrs. Thornton said, smiling slowly as tears welled in her eyes. "For after tonight, I stand second. It was to have you to myself, exclusively, for a few more hours, that I begged you not to leave until tomorrow."

"Dearest Mother!"

Yet, love remains inherently selfish. In an instant, he retreated into his own hopes and fears, casting a cold shadow across his mother's heart.

"But I know she doesn't care for me. I shall place myself at her feet—I must. If it is but one chance in a thousand, or a million, I will seize it."

"Don't fear!" his mother urged, suppressing her own stinging mortification at his lack of acknowledgment of her fleeting maternal joy, and the pang of jealousy that revealed the depth of her neglected love. "Don't be afraid," she said coolly. "As far as love goes, she may be worthy of you. It must have taken considerable strength to overcome her pride. Don't worry, John," she added, kissing him as she bid him goodnight.

Then she departed slowly and majestically. Once inside her own room, however, she locked the door and sat down to shed tears she had rarely allowed herself to shed.

***

Margaret entered the sitting room, where her parents were engaged in hushed conversation. She looked exceedingly pale and wan. Approaching them cautiously, she found her voice difficult to summon.

"Mrs. Thornton will send the water-bed, Mama."

"My dear, how exhausted you look! Is it terribly hot?"

"Very hot, and the streets are rough due to the strike."

Margaret's complexion flared brightly, only to drain away instantly.

"There has been a message from Bessy Higgins asking you to visit her," Mrs. Hale said gently. "But I'm sure you look too tired."

"Yes," Margaret whispered. "I am tired. I cannot go."

She sat silently and trembled while preparing the tea. She was grateful to see her father so absorbed in attending to her mother that he overlooked her distress. Even after her mother retired, he refused to leave her side,

"Your beauty was the first that won the place
And scal'd the walls of my undaunted heart,
Which, captive now, pines in a captive case,
Unkindly met with rigour for desert:—
Yet not the less your servant shall abide,
In spite of rude repulse or silent pride."

The following morning, Margaret dragged herself upright, grateful that the night had finally ended. Though she felt unrefreshed, she possessed a strange, lingering restfulness. Everything within the house had proceeded smoothly; her mother had awakened only once during the sleepless hours. A gentle breeze stirred the stagnant heat outside. Although there were no trees nearby to display the playful tossing of leaves, Margaret knew that somewhere—a roadside, a copse, or a dense green wood—a pleasant, murmuring dance of wind must exist. The mere thought of that rushing, falling noise echoed a distinct gladness in her own heart.

She sat working at Mrs. Hale's desk. Once this brief forenoon slumber passed, she intended to assist her mother with dressing. After dinner, she planned to visit Bessy Higgins. She resolved to banish all memory of the Thornton family entirely; there was no need to summon them until they physically stood before her in flesh and blood. Yet, naturally, the very effort to suppress the thought only drove it closer to the surface. From time to time, a hot flush swept across her pale cheeks, coloring them as swiftly as a sunbeam breaks through watery clouds to strike the sea.

Dixon entered the room with extreme softness, approaching on tiptoe. "Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret. He is in the drawing-room."

Margaret dropped her embroidery instantly. "Did he ask for me? Has Papa returned?"

"He requested you, miss; Master is still out."

"Very well, I will come," Margaret said quietly. Yet she lingered strangely near the shaded window.

Mr. Thornton stood beside one of the panes, his back ostensibly turned toward the door as if absorbed in watching the street. In truth, however, he was terrified of himself. His heart hammered violently at the prospect of her arrival. He could not erase the sensation of her arms around his neck, which he had felt impatiently the previous day; now, the recollection of her clinging defense thrilled him through and through, melting every resolution and stripping away all power of self-control as if it were wax before a flame. He dreaded advancing to meet her with arms outstretched in mute entreaty—that she would come and nestle against him as she had the day before, unheeded then, but never again to be ignored. His heart throbbed loudly and quickly. Despite his strength as a man, he trembled at the anticipation of what he needed to say and how it might be received.

Perhaps she would droop, flush, and flutter into his arms as to her natural home and resting place. One moment he burned with impatience at the thought that she might do exactly this; the next, he feared a passionate rejection, an idea so devastating it withered his future with a deadly blight, causing him to refuse even to entertain it.

He was startled by the sudden awareness of another presence in the room. Turning, he found she had entered so silently that he had not heard her footsteps; the street noises had been far more distinct to his distracted ear than her slow movements in her soft muslin gown.

She stood by the table, refusing to sit. Her eyelids were lowered half over her eyes; her teeth were closed, not compressed; her lips were slightly parted, revealing the white line between their curves. Her slow, deep breaths expanded her chin and her noble nostrils—it was the only motion visible on her countenance. The fine-grained skin, the oval cheek, the rich outline of her mouth with its deep-set corners and dimples—all appeared wan and pale today. The loss of their usual healthy color was accentuated by the heavy shadow of her dark hair, drawn down over her temples to hide all trace of the blow she had suffered. Her head, despite her drooping eyes, was thrown back slightly in her old proud posture. Her long arms hung motionless at her sides. Altogether, she resembled a prisoner falsely accused of a crime she loathed and despised, one from which she was too indignant to defend herself.

Mr. Thornton made a hasty step forward, then recovered himself, walking with quiet firmness to the door—which she had left ajar—and shutting it. He returned and stood opposite her for a moment, absorbing the general impression of her beautiful presence before daring to disturb it, perhaps even repelling it, with whatever words lay in store.

"Miss Hale, I was very ungrateful yesterday—"

"You had nothing to be grateful for," she interrupted, raising her eyes to look at him fully and steadily.

"You mean, I suppose, that you believe I ought to thank you for what I did."

In spite of herself, in defiance of her anger, thick blushes spread across her face, burning into her very eyes, yet she kept her gaze grave and unwavering.

"It was only a natural instinct; any woman would have acted precisely the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege when we witness danger. I ought rather," she added hastily, "to apologize to you for those thoughtless words which sent you down into the peril."

"It was not your words; it was the truth they conveyed, expressed as pungently as it was. But you shall not drive me off on that ground alone, thereby escaping the expression of my deep gratitude, my—" He paused, standing on the verge of speech. He would not speak in the haste of his hot passion; he would weigh every syllable. His will proved triumphant, and he stopped mid-sentence.

"I do not attempt to escape from anything," she declared. "I simply state that you owe me no gratitude; and I may add that any expression of it would be painful to me, for I do not feel I deserve it. Nevertheless, if it will relieve you from even a fancied obligation, please proceed."

"I do not wish to be relieved from any obligation," he retorted, goaded by her calm demeanor. "Fancied or real, I question neither. I choose to believe that I owe my very life to you—ay, smile if you deem it an exaggeration. I believe it, because it adds value to that life to think—oh, Miss Hale!" He lowered his voice to a tenderness of passion so intense she shivered and trembled beneath it. "To think that circumstances were so wrought that whenever I exult in existence henceforward, I can tell myself, 'All this gladness in life, all honest pride in doing my work in the world, all this keen sense of being, I owe to her!' It doubles the gladness, it makes the pride glow, it sharpens the sense of existence until I hardly know whether it is pain or pleasure to realize that I owe it to one—nay, you must, you shall hear—to one whom I love as I do not believe any man has ever loved a woman before."

He seized her hand tightly, panting as he awaited her response. With indignation, he flung the hand away upon hearing her icy tone; for cold it was, though the words stumbled forth as if she searched desperately for them.

"Your manner of speaking shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot help it if that is my first reaction. It might not be so, I dare say, if I understood the nature of the feeling you describe. I do not wish to vex you; moreover, we must speak gently, for Mother is asleep. However, your entire manner offends me—"

"How!" he exclaimed. "Offends you! I am indeed most unfortunate."

"Yes!" she replied, regaining her dignity. "I do feel offended; and I think, justly. You seem to imagine that my conduct yesterday"—once more the deep carnation blush rose, but this time accompanied by eyes kindling with indignation rather than shame—"was a personal act between you and me; and that you may come to thank me for it, instead of perceiving, as a gentleman would—yes! a gentleman," she repeated, alluding to their earlier debate regarding that specific title, "that any woman, worthy of the name of woman, would step forward to shield, with her revered helplessness, a man in danger from the violence of a multitude."

"And the gentleman thus rescued is forbidden the relief of thanks!" he interjected contemptuously. "I am a man. I claim the right of expressing my feelings."

"And I yielded to that right; simply stating that you gave me pain by insisting upon it," she answered proudly. "But you seem to have imagined that I was not merely guided by womanly instinct, but"—here the passionate tears, held back for so long, struggled vehemently to the surface and choked her voice—"but that I was prompted by some particular feeling for *you*—you! Why, there was not a man—not a single poor, desperate soul in that entire crowd—for whom I did not possess more sympathy; for whom I would not have done what little I could more heartily."

"You may continue, Miss Hale. I am aware of these misplaced sympathies of yours. I now believe it was only your innate sense of oppression—(yes; I, though a master, may also be oppressed)—that compelled you to act so nobly. I know you despise me; permit me to say, it is because you do not understand me."

"I do not care to understand," she

"Revenge may have her own; Roused discipline aloud proclaims their cause, And injured navies urge their broken laws." — Byron.

Margaret began to wonder if all proposals arrived with such startling suddenness, proving equally distressing the moment they occurred as the two she had recently endured. An involuntary comparison between Mr. Lennox and Mr. Thornton arose in her mind. She had regretted that an expression of anything other than friendship had been drawn from Henry Lennox by circumstance. That regret had dominated her feelings during his first approach. Yet, she had not felt so stunned, so utterly overwhelmed as she did now, while the echoes of Mr. Thornton's voice still lingered in the room. With Lennox, it seemed he had momentarily slipped across the boundary from friendship to love, only to retreat from it almost immediately, regretting the step nearly as much as she did, albeit for different reasons. With Mr. Thornton, however, there appeared to be no such transitional phase of friendship. Their interaction had been a continuous series of opposition. Their opinions clashed constantly, and indeed, she had never sensed that he valued her opinions as belonging to her as an individual. Wherever they challenged his rock-like character or his strength of passion, he seemed to cast them aside with contempt, until she grew weary of the futile exertion of making protests. And now, in this strange, wild, passionate manner, he had come to declare his love!

For, although it had initially struck her that his offer was forced from him by sharp compassion for the exposure she had made of herself—a sentiment he, like others, might misinterpret—yet even before he left the room, and certainly within five minutes thereafter, a clear conviction dawned upon her, shining brightly: that he loved her; that he had loved her; and that he would love her. She shrank and shuddered as if under the fascination of some great power repugnant to her entire previous life. She crept away, hiding from his idea. But it was in vain. Parodying a line from Fairfax's *Tasso*: "His strong idea wandered through her thought." She disliked him the more for having mastered her inner will. How dared he claim he would love her still, even if she shook him off with contempt? She wished she had spoken more strongly. Sharp, decisive words crowded into her mind now that it was too late to utter them. The deep impression of the interview was like a horror in a dream that refuses to leave the room once we awaken, rub our eyes, and force a stiff, rigid smile upon our lips. It remains there—cowering and gibbering with fixed, ghastly eyes in some corner of the chamber, waiting to hear if we dare acknowledge its presence to anyone. We dare not; poor cowards that we are! Thus, she shuddered away from the threat of his enduring love. What did he mean? Did she not possess the power to daunt him? She intended to see. It was more daring than became a man to threaten her so. Was it grounded upon the miserable events of yesterday? If need be, she would do the same tomorrow—not by a crippled beggar, willingly and gladly, but by him, just as bravely, in spite of his deductions and the cold slime of woman's impertinence. She acted because it was right, simple, and true to save where she could save; even to try to save. *"Fais ce que dois, advienne que pourra."*

Hitherto, she had not moved from where he had left her; no external circumstance had roused her from the trance of thought into which his final words and the look of his deep, intent, passionate eyes had plunged her, causing her own defenses to fall before his gaze. She went to the window and threw it open to dispel the oppression hanging around her. Then she opened the door with an impetuous wish to shake off the recollection of the past hour amidst the company of others or through active exertion. But the house was profoundly hushed in the noonday stillness of a household where an invalid catches the unrefreshing sleep denied to the night-hours. Margaret refused to be alone. What should she do?

*"Go and see Bessy Higgins, of course,"* she thought, as the memory of the message sent the night before flashed into her mind. Away she went.

When she arrived, she found Bessy lying on the settle, moved close to the fire despite the sultry, oppressive heat. She was laid down quite flat, as if resting languidly after some paroxysm of pain. Margaret felt certain Bessy needed the greater freedom of breathing afforded by a more upright posture. Without a word, she raised her and arranged the pillows so that Bessy was more at ease, though still very languid.

"I thought I should na' ha' seen yo' again," Bessy said finally, looking wistfully into Margaret's face.

"I'm afraid you're much worse. But I could not have come yesterday; my mother was so ill—for many reasons," Margaret replied, coloring slightly.

"You'd m'appen think I went beyond my place in sending Mary for yo'. But the wraglin' and the loud voices had just torn me to pieces. I thought when father left, oh! if I could just hear her voice reading me some words of peace and promise, I could die away into the silence and rest of God, just as a baby is hushed to sleep by its mother's lullaby."

"Shall I read you a chapter now?"

"Ay, do! M'appen I shan't listen to the sense at first; it will seem far away—but when ye come to words I like, to the comforting texts, it'll seem close in my ear, going through me as it were."

Margaret began. Bessy tossed to and fro. If, by an effort, she attended for one moment, it seemed as though she were convulsed into double restlessness the next. Finally, she burst out:

"Don't go on reading. It's no use. I'm blaspheming all the time in my mind, thinking angrily on what canna be helped. Ye'd hear of the riot, m'appen, yesterday at Marlborough Mills? Thornton's factory, ye know."

"Your father was not there, was he?" Margaret asked, coloring deeply.

"Not he. He'd ha' given his right hand if it had never come to pass. It's that that's fretting me. He's fairly knocked down in his mind by it. It's no use telling him; fools will always break out of bounds. Ye never saw a man so downhearted as he is."

"But why?" Margaret asked, confused.

"Why, ye see, he's a committee-man on this special strike. The Union appointed him because, though I say it as shouldn't say it, he's reckoned a deep chap and true to the backbone. He and the other committee-men laid their plans. They were to hold together through thick and thin; whatever the majority thought, the others were to think, whether they would or no. Above all, there was to be no going against the law of the land. Folk would follow them if they saw them striving and starving with dumb patience; but if there was once any noise of fighting and struggling—even with knobsticks—all was up, as they know by the experience of many times before. They would try to get speech of the knobsticks, coax 'em, reason with 'em, and maybe warn 'em off; but whatever happened, the Committee charged all members of the Union to lie down and die, if need be, without striking a blow. Then they reckoned they were sure of carrying the public with them. Besides all that, the Committee knew they were right in their demands and didn't want to have right mixed up with wrong, till folks couldn't separate it, no more than I can separate the physic-powder from the jelly ye gave me to mix. The jelly is much the bigger, but the powder tastes it all through.

"Well, I've told ye at length about this, but I am tired out. Ye just think for yorsel what it must be for father to have all his work undone by such a fool as Boucher, who must needs go right against the orders of the Committee and ruin the strike, just as bad as if he meant to be a Judas. Eh! but father gave it him last night! He went so far as to say he'd go and tell the police where they might find the ringleader of the riot; he'd give him up to the mill-owners to do what they would with him. He'd show the world that the real leaders of the strike were not such as Boucher, but steady, thoughtful men; good hands and good citizens, friendly to law and judgment, who would uphold order; who only wanted their rightful wage and wouldn't work, even though they starved, till they got it; but who would ne'er injure property or life. For," dropping her voice, "they do say that Boucher threw a stone at Thornton's sister that well-nigh killed her."

"That's not true," said Margaret. "It was not Boucher that threw the stone"—she went first red, then white.

"Ye'd be there then, were ye?" Bessie asked languidly, for indeed she had spoken with many pauses, as if speech was unusually difficult.

"Yes. Never mind. Go on. Only it was not Boucher that threw the stone. But what did he answer to your father?"

"He did na' speak words. He were all in such a tremble with spent passion I could na

"I have found that holy place of rest / Still changeless." —Mrs. Hemans.

When Mr. Thornton left the house that morning, he was nearly blinded by a baffled fury. He felt as dizzy as if Margaret, rather than appearing as a tender, graceful woman, had been a rough fishwife who had struck him across the face with clenched fists. He suffered actual physical pain—a splitting headache and a pulse that throbbed intermittently. The noise of the streets, the glaring sunlight, and the constant rumble of traffic were unbearable to him. He chided himself for being so weak, yet he could not immediately recall the specific cause of his anguish, nor judge if the trigger warranted the severity of his reaction.

It would have been a relief to sit on a doorstep and weep openly, perhaps crying alongside a small child who was raging against some perceived injustice. "I hate Margaret," he told himself, but as soon as the words formed, a wild, sharp surge of love tore through his dull, thunderous anger like lightning. His greatest solace was in embracing his torment, clinging to the certainty he had voiced to her: that even if she despised him, scorned him, or treated him with proud indifference, nothing would change within him. She could not alter his nature. He loved her, and he would love her; he would defy her and this miserable physical pain. He paused for a moment to cement this resolve.

An omnibus passed, heading toward the countryside. The conductor assumed he wanted a seat and pulled up near the curb. Apologizing seemed too much trouble, so he boarded and was carried away—past endless rows of houses, then past detached villas with neat gardens, until they reached true country hedgerows and eventually a small rural town. Everyone alighted, and Mr. Thornton followed. Walking briskly into the open fields, he sought the sharp motion to soothe his agitated mind.

Now, the details of the morning flooded back. He remembered the pathetic figure he must have cut, the absurdity of doing precisely what he had repeatedly convinced himself was the most foolish act imaginable, only to meet the exact consequences he had always predicted. Had he been bewitched by those beautiful eyes, that soft, half-open, sighing mouth pressed against his shoulder just yesterday? He could not shake the memory of her presence, of her arms around him—even if never again. He only caught fleeting glimpses of her; he did not fully comprehend her. One moment she was brave, the next timid; now tender, then haughtily regal. He replayed every encounter in an attempt to forget her, seeing her in every dress and mood, unable to decide which version was best. This morning, she had looked magnificent, her eyes flashing with the notion that sharing his danger yesterday proved her affection for him!

If Mr. Thornton considered himself a fool in the morning, he certainly did not become wiser by afternoon. The sixpence fare of the omnibus bought him only a sharper conviction: that no one in the world was like Margaret; that she did not love him and never would; yet that neither she nor the entire universe should prevent him from loving her.

He returned to the market square and rode back to Milton. Late in the afternoon, he was set down near his warehouse. The familiar surroundings triggered his habitual routines. He knew his workload had increased due to the previous day's commotion. He needed to meet his fellow magistrates, finalize arrangements for the safety of his newly imported Irish workers, and ensure they remained isolated from the discontented locals of Milton. Finally, he had to return home and face his mother.

Mrs. Thornton had spent the entire day in the dining room, waiting for news of her son's acceptance by Miss Hale. She had braced herself against every sudden noise, snatching up half-finished embroidery to stitch diligently despite her dimmed spectacles and trembling hands. Time after time, the door opened for trivial errands, causing her rigid face to soften from its frozen expression into a look of rare despondency. She wrested herself away from contemplating the dreary changes her son's marriage would bring, forcing her thoughts back into the safe grooves of household management.

She counted out stacks of linen, distinguishing between items marked *G.H.T.* (George and Hannah Thornton) and those purchased by her son with his own money. Some of the older Dutch damasks marked with her son's initials were exquisite treasures, unmatched today. Standing before them, Mrs. Thornton recalled her own pride in them during her youth. She knitted her brow, pinched her lips tight, and carefully unpicked the initial 'G' to replace it with her son's letter. She searched for the red thread needed for the new markings but found it exhausted; she lacked the heart to order more just yet.

Staring into the empty space, a series of visions played before her, all centered on her son—her pride, her property. He still had not arrived. Undoubtedly, he was with Miss Hale. The new love was already displacing her position as the first in his heart. A terrible, physical pang of jealous despair shot through her, forcing her to sit down. Moments later, she rose again, as upright as ever, wearing a grim smile for the first time that day, ready to greet the triumphant visitor who would never know the deep regret his mother felt regarding his union.

In all this, Mrs. Thornton gave little thought to the future daughter-in-law as an individual. She was merely "John's wife." Taking Mrs. Thornton's place as mistress of the house was just one of the glorious rewards accompanying the supreme honor. Household abundance, fine linens, honor, love, obedience, and loyal friends would flow naturally like jewels onto a king's robe, valued only for their collective splendor. Being chosen by John elevated a kitchen maid above the rest of the world. As for Miss Hale, she wasn't entirely bad. If she were a native of Milton, Mrs. Thornton would have liked her instantly. She possessed pungency, taste, spirit, and flavor. True, she was prejudiced and ignorant, but that was expected of someone raised in the South.

A strange, mortifying comparison between Fanny and Miss Hale churned in Mrs. Thornton's mind. For once, she spoke harshly to her daughter, abusing her roundly, then performed a penance by picking up Henry's *Commentaries* to force her attention away from the linen.

Then, he appeared! She heard his footsteps even as she believed she was finishing a sentence. While her eye scanned the page and her memory could mechanically repeat the words, her heightened senses interpreted every sound: now he was at the hat stand, now approaching the room door. Why did he hesitate? Did he want to tell her the truth? Keeping her head bowed over the book, she waited.

He approached the table and stood still, waiting for her to finish the paragraph that seemed to absorb her completely. With an effort, she looked up.

"Well, John?"

He understood the weight of those few words. Though bitterness surged within him, longing to answer with a jest, he held his tongue; his mother deserved better.
