He moved behind her so she could not see his expression, bent forward to lift her gray, stony face, and kissed it, murmuring, "No one loves me, no one cares for me, but you, mother."

Turning away, he leaned his head against the mantelpiece, tears pricking his manly eyes. She stood up, then tottered. For the first time in her life, the strong woman faltered. Placing her hands on his shoulders, she looked deeply into his face, compelling him to look at her.

"Mother's love is given by God, John. It holds fast forever and ever. A girl's love is like a puff of smoke—it changes with every wind. And she would not have you, my own lad, would she?" She gritted her teeth, showing them like a dog along the full length of her mouth.

He shook his head. "I am not fit for her, mother. I knew I was not."

She ground out words between her closed teeth. He could not hear them, but the look in her eyes conveyed a curse—not necessarily coarse in wording, but cruel in intent as anything ever spoken. Yet, her heart leaped lightly to know he was hers again.

"Mother!" he said hurriedly. "I cannot hear a word against her. Spare me—I am very weak in my sore heart. I love her yet; I love her more than ever."

"And I hate her," Mrs. Thornton replied in a low, fierce voice. "I tried not to hate her when she stood between you and me. I told myself she would make you happy, and I would give my heart's blood to ensure that. But now, I hate her for your misery's sake. Yes, John, there is no use hiding your aching heart from me. I am the mother who bore you, and your sorrow is my agony. If you do not hate her, I do."

"Then, mother, you make me love her more. She is unjustly treated by you, and I must restore the balance. But why discuss love or hatred? She does not care for me, and that is enough, too much. Let us never mention the subject again. That is the only thing you can do for me. Let us never name her."

"With all my heart. I only wish that she, and everything connected to her, were swept back to where they came from."

He stood still, gazing into the fire for a minute or two longer. Dry, dim eyes filled with unexpected tears as she watched him, yet she remained as grim and quiet as usual

"For never any thing can be amiss / When simpleness and duty tender it." — *Midsummer Night's Dream*.

Mr. Thornton approached the affairs of the following day with absolute clarity. There was a modest demand for finished goods within his sector of the trade, and seizing the opportunity, he negotiated aggressively. He arrived at the magistrate's meeting precisely on time, offering his peers the benefit of his keen judgment and his ability to foresee consequences instantly, thereby driving decisions forward with remarkable speed. While older, wealthier gentlemen—men whose fortunes were tied up in landed estates—sought his counsel, Mr. Thornton possessed only floating capital invested entirely in commerce. Yet, they looked to him for prompt wisdom. He was the one appointed to liaise with the police and lead necessary proceedings. However, he regarded their unconscious deference with the same indifference as the soft west wind, which barely disturbed the straight, vertical plume of smoke rising from the great factory chimneys. He remained unaware of the silent respect accorded to him; had he perceived it, he might have viewed it as an impediment to his objectives. As it was, his sole focus was the swift execution of his duties.

News of his high regard filtered through the town via the gossipy ears of the magistrates' wives and the wealthy ladies. They whispered that without Mr. Thornton, matters would have deteriorated significantly. Consequently, he swept aside his business engagements with efficiency. It appeared as though the deep mortification of the previous day, followed by those stunned, aimless hours, had finally burned away the mist from his intellect. He felt his power surging within him, reveling in it, and nearly defying his own heart. Had he been aware of the truth, he could have sung the song of the miller living by the River Dee: "I care for nobody—Nobody cares for me."

The evidence against Boucher and other ringleaders of the riot was presented before him; the charges against the remaining three for conspiracy failed. Nevertheless, he sternly ordered the police to remain vigilant, insisting that the swift arm of the law stand ready to strike the moment further proof emerged. Then, he exited the hot, reeking courtroom of the borough hall and stepped out onto the street. It was fresher, yet still sultry. Suddenly, he seemed to collapse under a wave of exhaustion; his thoughts lost their grip and drifted inevitably toward her. They recalled not the scene of his rejection the day before, but the looks and actions of the day prior to that. He walked mechanically through the crowded thoroughfares, weaving in and out of the throng without truly seeing anyone, consumed by a sickness of longing for that single half-hour, that brief interval when she had clung to him and her heart had beaten against his. He desired to return to that moment once more.

"Why, Mr. Thornton! You're treating me very coldly, I must say. And how is Mrs. Thornton? Fine weather this! We doctors aren't fond of it, I can assure you!"

"I beg your pardon, Dr. Donaldson. I genuinely didn't see you. My mother is quite well, thank you. It is a splendid day, ideal for the harvest, I trust. If the wheat gets in properly, we shall enjoy a brisk trade next year, regardless of what you doctors endure."

"Ay, ay. Every man for himself. Your bad weather and lean times are my profitable ones. When trade falters, there is far more undermining of health and preparation for death among you Miltonians than you realize."

"Not with me, Doctor. I am forged of iron. News of the worst debt I've ever incurred never varied my pulse. This strike, which affects me more profoundly than any other individual in Milton—even more than Mr. Hamper—does not diminish my appetite. You must seek another patient, Doctor."

"By the way, you recommended a good case to me, a poor lady! To avoid sounding heartless, I must confess I believe Mrs. Hale—that lady in Crampton, you know—has perhaps only a few weeks left. I never held much hope for a cure, as I mentioned before, but I visited her today, and my prognosis remains grim."

Mr. Thornton fell silent. His boasted steady pulse faltered for an instant. "Is there anything I can do?" he asked, his voice altered. "You know... since funds are scarce, are there any comforts or delicacies she requires?"

"No," replied the Doctor, shaking his head. "She craves fruit; she suffers from a constant fever. But Jargonelle pears will suffice, and there are plenty available in the market."

"You will inform me immediately if there is anything else I can provide, I promise," Mr. Thornton said. "I rely upon your honesty."

"Oh! Never fear! I shall not spare your purse—I know it is deep enough. I wish you'd grant me a carte-blanche for all my patients and their needs."

However, Mr. Thornton lacked general benevolence; few would attribute strong affections to him. Yet, he proceeded directly to the first fruit shop in Milton. He selected a cluster of purple grapes bearing the most delicate bloom, the richest-hued peaches, and vines with the freshest leaves. These were packed carefully into a basket. The shopkeeper waited for his instruction regarding delivery.

"To Marlborough Mills, I presume, sir?"

"No!" Mr. Thornton declared. "Give the basket to me—I will carry it."

He gripped the basket with both hands, forcing his way through the busiest section of the town, navigating the crowds typically reserved for female shoppers. Numerous young ladies he knew turned to stare, finding it peculiar to see him carrying a load like a porter or an errand-boy. Inside his mind, he reasoned: *"I shall not be deterred from doing as I choose by the fear of her opinion. I wish to deliver this fruit to the poor mother, and it is simply right that I should. She shall never shame me out of performing a kindness. It would be a cruel jest indeed if, terrified of a haughty girl, I failed to act on a whim I hold dear. I do this for Mr. Hale; I do it in open defiance of her."*

He moved at an unusually brisk pace until he reached Crampton. Ascending the stairs two steps at a time, he entered the drawing room before Dixon could announce him. His face was flushed, his eyes shining with a kind, earnest intensity. Mrs. Hale lay on the sofa, burning with fever. Mr. Hale was reading aloud, while Margaret sat on a low stool beside her mother, her work forgotten. Her heart fluttered violently, though whether Mr. Hale shared her agitation was unclear.

Yet, Mr. Thornton paid no attention to either of them—not even to Mr. Hale. He marched straight to Mrs. Hale with his basket, speaking in a subdued, gentle tone that was particularly touching when employed by a robust man in prime health addressing a frail invalid.

"I encountered Dr. Donaldson, ma'am, and as he noted that fruit would benefit you, I took the liberty—the great liberty—of procuring some specimens that seemed exceptionally fine to me."

Mrs. Hale was overcome with delight, trembling with eager anticipation. Mr. Hale, using fewer words, expressed a depth of gratitude. "Fetch a plate, Margaret—a basket—anything."

Margaret rose by the table, half-fearing movement or noise might alert Mr. Thornton to her presence. She imagined it would be awkward for both parties to acknowledge her existence simultaneously. She assumed that, having started seated on a low stool and now standing behind her father, he had inadvertently overlooked her in his haste. In reality, he seemed oblivious to her presence entirely, despite his gaze never resting upon her.

"I must depart," he announced. "I cannot stay longer. If you will forgive this intrusion—and my rough manners, which seem too abrupt—I fear I shall be gentler next time. Please permit me the pleasure of bringing you more fruit whenever I encounter something tempting. Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Goodbye, ma'am."

And he was gone. Not a single word addressed to Margaret. Not a single glance exchanged. She convinced herself he had not seen her. She retrieved a plate in silence and lifted the fruit out tenderly, using the tips of her slender fingers. It was generous of him to bring it, especially considering the events of the previous day.

"Oh! It is so delicious!" Mrs. Hale murmured in her weak voice. "How kind of him to think of me! Margaret, my love, please taste these grapes! Was it not thoughtful of him?"

"Yes!" Margaret replied quietly.

"Margaret!" Mrs. Hale said, with a hint of querulousness, "you dislike everything Mr. Thornton does. I have never seen anyone so prejudiced."

Mr. Hale had been peeling a peach for his wife and cut a small slice for himself. "If I harbored any prejudices, the gift of such exquisite fruit would surely dissolve them. I have not tasted fruit like this—not even in Hampshire—since I was a boy. For boys, I suspect, all fruit is delightful. Do you recall the tangled currant bushes at the corner of the west wall in our garden at home?"

Did she not? Did she not remember every weather stain on the ancient stone wall, the gray and yellow lichens mapping its surface, and the tiny crane's-bill growing in the cracks? Shaken by the tumultuous past forty-eight hours, her entire life currently straining her fortitude, these casual reminiscences from her father triggered a sudden reaction. Dropping her sewing on the floor, she hurried out of the room and into her private chamber.

"Through cross to crown!—And though thy spirit's life / Trials untold assail with giant strength, / Good cheer! good cheer! Soon ends the bitter strife, / And thou shalt reign in peace with Christ at length." —KOSEGARTEN.

"Ay sooth, we feel too strong in weal, to need / Thee on that road; But woe being come, the soul is dumb, that crieth not on 'God.'" —MRS. BROWNING.

That afternoon, Margaret walked swiftly toward the Higgins' home. Mary was waiting for her, her face etched with half-distrust. Margaret smiled warmly into her eyes, offering reassurance. Together, they moved quickly through the house, up the stairs, and into the quiet presence of the dead. Immediately, Margaret felt glad she had come. The face, so often weary with pain and restless with troubling thoughts, now bore the faint, soft smile of eternal rest. Slow tears gathered in Margaret's eyes, yet a profound calm settled within her soul. This was death. It appeared more peaceful than life itself. Sacred scriptures flooded her mind: *"They rest from their labours." "The weary are at rest." "He giveth his beloved sleep."*

Slowly, Margaret turned away from the bed. Behind her, Mary humbly sobbed. They descended the stairs without exchanging a word.

Nicholas Higgins stood in the center of the floor, his hand resting on the house-table. His great eyes, startled wide by the news he had heard from many busy tongues as he approached the courtyard, remained dry and fierce. He studied the reality of her passing, forcing himself to accept that her place would know her no more. She had been so sickly, dying for so long, that he had convinced himself she would not actually die; that she would "pull through."

Margaret felt as though she had no business to be there, familiarizing herself with the surroundings of death, which the father had only just begun to learn. There had been a brief pause on the steep, crooked stair when she first saw him, but now she tried to slip past his abstracted gaze, leaving him to the solemn circle of his household misery.

Mary sat down on the nearest chair, threw her apron over her head, and began to weep loudly.

The noise seemed to rouse Nicholas. He seized Margaret's arm suddenly, holding her until he could find words to speak. His throat felt dry; the words came up thick, choking, and hoarse.

"Were yo' with her? Did yo' see her die?"

"No!" Margaret replied, standing still with the utmost patience, now that she realized she was being observed. It took some time before he spoke again, though he kept his grip on her arm.

"All men must die," he said finally, with a strange gravity that first suggested to Margaret he had been drinking—not enough to intoxicate himself, but enough to bewilder his thoughts. "But she were younger than me." Still, he pondered the event, not looking at Margaret, though he held her tightly. Suddenly, he looked up at her with a wild, searching inquiry in his glance. "Yo're sure and certain she's dead—not in a dream, a faint? she's been so before, often."

"She is dead," Margaret answered. She felt no fear in speaking to him, despite the pain his grip caused her arm, as wild gleams crossed the stupidity of his eyes.

"She is dead!" she repeated.

He continued to stare at her with that searching look, which seemed to fade from his eyes as he gazed. Then, abruptly, he released his hold on Margaret. Throwing his body half across the table, he shook it violently, rattling every piece of furniture in the room with his sobs. Mary trembled forward toward him.

"Get thee gone!—get thee gone!" he cried, striking wildly and blindly at her. "What do I care for thee?" Margaret took her hand and held it softly in hers. He tore at his hair, beat his head against the hard wood, and then lay exhausted and stupefied. Neither his daughter nor Margaret moved. Mary trembled from head to foot.

Finally—it might have been a quarter of an hour, perhaps an hour—he lifted himself up. His eyes were swollen and bloodshot, and he seemed to have forgotten anyone was present; he scowled at the watchers when he noticed them. He shook himself heavily, gave them one final sullen look, said not a word, and made for the door.

"Oh, father, father!" Mary cried, throwing herself onto his arm—"not tonight! Any night but tonight. Oh, help me! he's going out to drink again! Father, I'll not leave you. You may strike, but I'll not leave you. She told me last of all to keep you from drink!"

But Margaret stood silently in the doorway, commanding nonetheless. He looked up at her defiantly.

"It's my own house. Stand out of the way, wench, or I'll make ye!" He had shaken Mary off with violence; he looked ready to strike Margaret. Yet she never moved a muscle, never taking her deep, serious eyes off him. He stared back at her with gloomy fierceness. Had she stirred a finger or a toe, he would have thrust her aside with even more violence than he had used against his own daughter, whose face was bleeding from her fall against a chair.

"What are yo' looking at me in that way for?" he asked eventually, daunted and awed by her severe calm. "If yo' think for to keep me from going what gait I choose, because she loved ye—and in my own house, too, where I never asked ye to come, ye're mistaken. It's very hard upon a man that he can't go to the only comfort left."

Margaret sensed he acknowledged her power. What could she do next? He had seated himself on a chair close to the door; half-conquered, half-resentful; intending to leave as soon as she vacated her position, but unwilling to use the violence he had threatened mere moments before. Margaret laid her hand on his arm.

"Come with me," she said. "Come and see her!"

Her voice was very low and solemn, yet there was no fear or doubt expressed in it, regarding either him or his compliance. He sullenly rose. He stood uncertain, with a dogged irresolution on his face. She waited for him there; quietly and patiently waiting for his moment to move. He derived a strange pleasure in making her wait, but eventually, he moved toward the stairs.

They stood beside the corpse.

"Her last words to Mary were, 'Keep my father from drink.'"

"It canna hurt her now," he muttered. "Nought can hurt her now." Then, raising his voice to a wailing cry, he continued: "We may quarrel and fall out—we may make peace and be friends—we may come to skin and bone—and nought of all our griefs will ever touch her more. She's had her portion on 'em. What with hard work first, and sickness at last, she led the life of a dog. And to die without knowing one good piece of rejoicing in all her days! Nay, wench, whatever she said, she can know nought about it now, and I mun ha' a sup of drink just to steady me again sorrow."

"No," said Margaret, softening with his softened manner. "You shall not. If her life has been what you say, at any rate she did not fear death as some do. Oh, you should have heard her speak of the life to come—the life hidden with God, that she is now gone to."

He shook his head, glancing sideways up at Margaret as he did so. His pale, haggard face struck her painfully.

"You are sorely tried. Where have you been all day—not at work?"

"Not at work, sure enough," he said, with a short, grim laugh. "Not at what you call work. I were at the Committee, till I were sickened out with trying to make fools hear reason. I were fetched to Boucher's wife before seven this morning. She's bed-fast, but she were raving and raging to know where her dunner-headed brute of a chap was, as if I'd to keep him—as if he were fit to be ruled by me. The d--d fool, who has put his foot in all our plans! And I've walked my feet sore with going about to see men who wouldn't be seen, now the law is raised against us. And I were sore-hearted, too, which is worse than sore-footed; and if I did see a friend who offered to treat me, I never knew who lay dying here. Bess, lass, thou'd believe me, thou wouldst—wouldstn't thou?" turning to the poor, dumb form with a wild appeal.

"I am sure," said Margaret, "I am sure you did not know; it was quite sudden. But, now, you see, it would be different; you do know; you do see her lying there; you hear what she said with her last breath. You will not go?"

There was no answer. Indeed, where was he to look for comfort?

"Come home with me," she said at last, with a bold venture, half-trembling at her own proposal as she made it. "At least you shall have some comfortable food, which I'm sure you need."

"Yo'r father's a parson?" he asked, with

"Some wishes crossed my mind and dimly cheered it, And one or two poor melancholy pleasures, Each in the pale unwarming light of hope, Silvering its flimsy wing, flew silent by— Moths in the moonbeam!" —Coleridge.

The following morning brought Margaret a letter from Edith. It was affectionate and inconsequential, much like its writer. Yet, the affection charmed Margaret's own generous nature, and having grown up alongside Edith's scattered logic, she perceived no fault in it. The note ran thus:

"Oh, Margaret, it is worth a journey from England to see my boy! He is a superb little fellow, especially in his caps, and most especially in the one you sent him, you good, dainty-fingered, persevering little lady! Having made all the mothers here envious, I want to show him to somebody new, and hear a fresh set of admiring expressions; perhaps that's all the reason; perhaps it isn't—nay, possibly, there is just a little cousinly love mixed with it; but I do want you so much to come here, Margaret! I'm sure it would be the very best thing for Aunt Hale's health; everybody here is young and well, and our skies are always blue, and our sun always shines, and the band plays deliciously from morning till night; and, to come back to the burden of my ditty, my baby always smiles. I am constantly wanting you to draw him from me, Margaret. It doesn't signify what he is doing; that very thing is prettiest, gracefulst, best. I think I love him a great deal better than my husband, who is getting stout, and grumpy—what he calls 'busy.' No! he isn't. He has just come in with news of such a charming picnic, given by the officers of the *Hazard*, at anchor in the bay below. Because he has brought in such a pleasant piece of news, I retract all I said just now. Did not somebody burn his hand for having said or done something he was sorry for? Well, I can't burn mine, because it would hurt me, and the scar would be ugly; but I'll retract all I said as fast as I can. Cosmo is quite as great a darling as baby, and not a bit stout, and as ungrumpy as ever husband was; only, sometimes he is very, very busy. I may say that without love—wifely duty—where was I?—I had something very particular to say, I know, once. Oh, it is this—Dearest Margaret!—you must come and see me; it would do Aunt Hale good, as I said before. Get the doctor to order it for her. Tell him that it's the smoke of Milton that does her harm. I have no doubt it is that, really. Three months (you must not come for less) of this delicious climate—all sunshine, and grapes as common as blackberries, would quite cure her. I don't ask my uncle"—(here the letter became more constrained, and better written. Mr. Hale was in the corner, like a naughty child, for having given up his living.)—"because, I dare say, he disapproves of war, soldiers, and bands of music; at least, I know that many Dissenters are members of the Peace Society, and I am afraid he would not like to come; but, if he would, dear, pray say that Cosmo and I will do our best to make him happy; and I'll hide up Cosmo's red coat and sword, and make the band play all sorts of grave, solemn things; or, if they do play pomps and vanities, it shall be in double slow time. Dear Margaret, if he would like to accompany you and Aunt Hale, we will try and make it pleasant, though I'm rather afraid of any one who has done anything for conscience' sake. You never did, I hope. Tell Aunt Hale not to bring any warm clothes, though I'm afraid it will be late in the year before you can come. But you have no idea of the heat here! I tried to wear my great beauty Indian shawl at a picnic. I kept myself up with proverbs as long as I could; 'Pride must abide,'—and such wholesome pieces of pith; but it was no use. I was like mamma's little dog Tiny with an elephant's trappings on; smothered, hidden, killed with my finery; so I made it into a capital carpet for us all to sit down upon. Here's this boy of mine, Margaret—if you don't pack up your things as soon as you get this letter, and come straight off to see him, I shall think you're descended from King Herod!"

Margaret longed for a single day of Edith's life—her freedom from care, her cheerful home, her sunny skies. If a wish could have transported her, she would have gone off; just for one day. She yearned for the strength which such a change would give—even for a few hours to be in the midst of that bright life, and to feel young again. Not yet twenty! and she had had to bear up against such hard pressure that she felt quite old. That was her first feeling after reading Edith's letter. Then she read it again, and, forgetting herself, was amused at its likeness to Edith's self, and was laughing merrily over it when Mrs. Hale came into the drawing-room, leaning on Dixon's arm. Margaret flew to adjust the pillows. Her mother seemed more than usually feeble.

"What were you laughing at, Margaret?" asked she, as soon as she had recovered from the exertion of settling herself on the sofa.

"A letter I have had this morning from Edith. Shall I read it to you, mamma?"

She read it aloud, and for a time it seemed to interest her mother, who kept wondering what name Edith had given to her boy, and suggesting all probable names, and all possible reasons why each and all of these names should be given. Into the very midst of these wonders Mr. Thornton came, bringing another offering of fruit for Mrs. Hale. He could not—say rather, he would not—deny himself the chance of the pleasure of seeing Margaret. He had no end in this but the present gratification. It was the sturdy wilfulness of a man usually most reasonable and self-controlled. He entered the room, taking in at a glance the fact of Margaret's presence; but after the first cold, distant bow, he never seemed to let his eyes fall on her again. He only stayed to present his peaches—to speak some gentle, kindly words—and then his cold, offended eyes met Margaret's with a grave farewell, as he left the room.

She sat down silent and pale. "Do you know, Margaret, I really begin to like Mr. Thornton."

No answer at first. Then Margaret forced out an icy "Do you?"

"Yes! I think he is really getting quite polished in his manners."

Margaret's voice was more in order now. She replied, "He is very kind and attentive—there is no doubt of that."

"I wonder Mrs. Thornton never calls. She must know I am ill, because of the water-bed."

"I dare say, she hears how you are from her son."

"Still, I should like to see her. You have so few friends here, Margaret."

Margaret felt what was in her mother's thoughts—a tender craving to bespeak the kindness of some woman towards the daughter that might soon be left motherless. But she could not speak.

"Do you think," said Mrs. Hale, after a pause, "that you could go and ask Mrs. Thornton to come and see me? Only once—I don't want to be troublesome."

"I will do anything, if you wish it, mamma—but if—but when Frederick comes——"

"Ah, to be sure! we must keep our doors shut—we must let no one in. I hardly know whether I dare wish him to come or not. Sometimes I think I would rather not. Sometimes I have such frightful dreams about him."

"Oh, mamma! we'll take good care. I will put my arm in the bolt sooner than he should come to the slightest harm. Trust the care of him to me, mamma. I will watch over him like a lioness over her young."

"When can we hear from him?"

"Not for a week yet, certainly—perhaps more."

"We must send Martha away in good time. It would never do to have her here when he comes, and then send her off in a hurry."

"Dixon is sure to remind us of that. I was thinking that, if we wanted any help in the house while he is here, we could perhaps get Mary Higgins. She is very slack of work, and is a good girl, and would take pains to do her best, I am sure, and would sleep at home, and need never come upstairs, so as to know who is in the house."

"As you please. As Dixon pleases. But, Margaret, don't get to use these horrid Milton words. 'Slack of work:' it is a provincialism. What will your aunt Shaw say, if she hears you use it on her return?"

"Oh, mamma! don't try and make a bugbear of aunt Shaw," said Margaret, laughing. "Edith picked up all sorts of military slang from Captain Lennox, and aunt Shaw never took any notice of it."

"But yours is factory slang."

"And if I live in a factory town, I must speak factory language when I want it. Why, mamma

"The saddest birds a season find to sing." — Southwell.

"Never to fold the robe o'er secret pain... Thou art gone home!" — Mrs. Hemans.

The following morning, Mrs. Thornton visited Mrs. Hale. The invalid's condition had deteriorated sharply overnight; a sudden, terrifying step toward death had been taken in the space of twelve hours, leaving her family stunned by the gray, sunken hollows that had appeared in her features. Mrs. Thornton, who had not seen her for weeks, felt a sudden softening within her own hardened heart. Though she had arrived because her son requested it as a personal favor, her pride and bitterness remained firmly entrenched against the Hale family, of whom Margaret was a member. She questioned the reality of Mrs. Hale's illness, suspecting it was merely a fleeting whim designed to excuse the lady from her usual duties.

"I wish we had never come near this place," she declared to her son, "that you had never gotten acquainted with them. There have never been such useless languages as Latin and Greek invented."

Her son endured this tirade in silence. Only when she finished her invective against the dead tongues did he calmly reiterate his wish for her to visit Mrs. Hale at the appointed hour, noting it was the most convenient time for the invalid. Mrs. Thornton submitted with the worst grace imaginable, yet secretly admired her son for his persistence in maintaining contact with the Hales.

To her, his goodness bordered on weakness, while her own contempt for Mr. and Mrs. Hale and her outright dislike of Margaret filled her thoughts until she was overwhelmed by the dark shadow of the Angel of Death. There lay Mrs. Hale—a mother, like herself, yet much younger—stiff and still on a bed offering no sign of hope. The room was devoid of light and shade; there was no power of action, only faint whispers alternating with studied silence. Yet, this monotony seemed almost too much to bear.

When Mrs. Thornton entered, strong and vibrant with life, Mrs. Hale lay motionless, though clearly conscious of her visitor's presence. She did not open her eyes for a moment or two. Heavy tears glistened on her lashes before she finally looked up. Reaching feebly beneath the covers, she sought the touch of Mrs. Thornton's large, firm fingers and whispered, barely audible:

"Margaret—you have a daughter—my sister is in Italy. My child will be without a mother; in a strange place—if I die—will you..."

Her filmy, wandering eyes fixed themselves on Mrs. Thornton's face with an intensity of longing. For a minute, Mrs. Thornton's features remained rigid, stern, and unmoved. Had the sick woman's vision not been dimming with gathering tears, she might have seen a dark cloud cross the cold features of the visitor. It was not a thought of her son or her living daughter, Fanny, that finally stirred Mrs. Thornton's heart, but a sudden recollection triggered by the room's arrangement: of a little daughter, dead in infancy long ago. Like a sudden sunbeam, this memory melted the icy crust behind which lay a real, tender woman.

"You wish me to be a friend to Miss Hale?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her voice measured and clear, refusing to soften despite her heart's turmoil.

Mrs. Hale pressed the hand lying below hers on the coverlet, unable to speak further. Mrs. Thornton sighed. "I will be a true friend, if circumstances require it. Not a tender friend. That I cannot be." She paused, tempted to add "to her," but relented at the sight of the poor, anxious face. "It is not my nature to show affection even where I feel it, nor do I volunteer advice in general. Still, at your request—and if it will be any comfort to you—I will promise."

A long pause followed. Conscientious to a fault, Mrs. Thornton refused to promise what she did not intend to perform. Performing acts of kindness regarding Margaret, whom she currently despised, was nearly impossible.

"I promise," she said with grave severity, a tone that somehow inspired the dying woman with faith more stable than the flickering, wavering life around them. "I promise that in any difficulty in which Miss Hale—"

"Call her Margaret!" gasped Mrs. Hale.

"In which she comes to me for help, I will assist her with every power I possess, as if she were my own daughter. I also promise that if ever I see her doing what I believe to be wrong—such wrong not touching me or mine, where I might be suspected of having an interested motive—I will tell her of it faithfully and plainly, as I should wish my own daughter to be told."

Another long silence ensued. Mrs. Hale sensed the promise excluded everything, yet it was significant. Its reservations were unclear to her weakened mind, but she was too dizzy and exhausted to dwell on them. Meanwhile, Mrs. Thornton reviewed the probable scenarios in which she had bound herself. She derived a fierce pleasure from the prospect of delivering unwelcome truths to Margaret in the name of duty.

"I thank you," Mrs. Hale began, her voice fading. "I pray God to bless you. I shall never see you again in this world. But my last words are, I thank you for your promise of kindness to my child."

"Not kindness!" Mrs. Thornton corrected, ungraciously truthful to the end. Having soothed her conscience with the words, she was glad they went unheard. She pressed Mrs. Hale's soft, languid hand, then rose and departed from the house unseen by anyone.

While this somber interview unfolded, Margaret and Dixon were huddled together, plotting how to keep Frederick's impending arrival a profound secret from everyone outside the house. A letter from him could arrive any day, followed swiftly by his physical presence. Martha was to be sent away on holiday immediately; Dixon would maintain a stern guard at the front door, admitting only essential visitors to Mr. Hale's room downstairs—an excuse easily made by Mrs. Hale's critical illness. If Mary Higgins needed assistance in the kitchen, she was to hear and see as little of Frederick as possible, referring to him only as Mr. Dickinson if necessary. Her sluggish, indifferent nature served as the greatest safeguard of all.

They resolved that Martha would leave that very afternoon for her mother's house. Margaret regretted not sending her earlier, fearing it might seem odd to grant a servant a holiday when the household required constant attention.

Poor Margaret spent the entire afternoon playing the role of a Roman daughter, draining her own scanty reserves of strength to support her father. Mr. Hale oscillated between hope and despair amidst his wife's attacks, buoying himself up during brief respites from her pain, convinced that recovery was imminent. Consequently, when the paroxysms returned, each more severe than the last, they brought fresh agony and crushing disappointment.

That afternoon, he sat in the drawing-room, unable to bear the solitude of his study or engage in any activity. He buried his head in his arms, folded on the table. Margaret's heart ached witnessing his state, yet, since he offered no speech, she hesitated to offer unsolicited comfort. Martha was gone; Dixon sat with Mrs. Hale while she slept. The house was profoundly still and quiet, and darkness fell without anyone moving to fetch candles. Margaret sat by the window, staring out at the streetlamps and the road, seeing nothing but alive to her father's heavy sighs. She dreaded going down for lights, fearing that her withdrawal might trigger a violent emotional collapse in her father without her being nearby to soothe him. Just as she considered attending to the kitchen fire, which was solely her responsibility, the muffled doorbell rang with such violent force that the wires jingled throughout the house, though the actual sound was muted.

She jumped up, passed her father—who had not stirred at the dull, veiled noise—and returned to kiss him tenderly. He remained immovable, taking no notice of her embrace. She descended softly into the dark hallway. Dixon would have engaged the chain before opening the door, but Margaret, her mind preoccupied, felt no fear. A tall male figure stood between her and the luminous street. He was looking away, but turned quickly at the sound of the latch.

"Is this Mr. Hale's?" he asked in a clear, full, delicate voice.

Margaret trembled from head to toe. Initially, she did not answer. Then, in a moment, she sighed out, "Frederick!" stretching out both hands to catch his and draw him inside.

"Oh, Margaret!" he exclaimed, holding her gently by the shoulders after their kiss, as if even in the darkness he could see her face and read a quicker answer to his question than words could provide. "My mother! Is she alive?"

"Yes, she is alive, dear, dear brother! She—as ill as she can be—is; but alive! She is alive!"

"Thank God!" he breathed.

"Papa is utterly prostrate with this great grief."

"You expect me, don't you?"

"No, we received no letter."

"Then I have come before it. But my mother knows I am coming."

"Oh! We all knew you would come. But wait a little! Step in here. Give me your hand. What is this? Oh! Your carpet-bag. Dixon has shut the shutters; but this is Papa's study, and I can take you to a chair to rest for a few minutes while I go and tell him."

She groped her way to the taper and matchbox. Suddenly, she felt shy as the feeble light revealed them. All she could discern was that her brother's

The chill, shivering October morning arrived, bearing none of the gentle, silvery mist typical of the countryside that clears before the sun to reveal vibrant colors. Instead, it was the grim October of Milton, where heavy fogs clung to the streets, allowing the sun only fleeting glimpses of long, dusky thoroughfares. Margaret moved through the house with a languid step, aiding Dixon in preparing the home for its final days. Tears constantly blurred her vision, yet she found no time to weep openly. Her father and brother were consumed by grief, but she remained the anchor, forced to work, plan, and deliberate. Even the intricate arrangements for the funeral fell to her shoulders.

When the fire crackled brightly and the tea-kettle sang its familiar song, signaling readiness for breakfast, Margaret cast one last glance around the room. She desired everything to appear cheerful, a stark contrast to the desolation in her own heart. As the room bloomed with domestic warmth, the disparity between the setting and her thoughts triggered a sudden, violent sobbing. Kneeling beside the sofa, she buried her face in the cushions, desperate to hide her cries from others. Then, a hand touched her shoulder.

"Come, Miss Hale—come, my dear!" Dixon's voice cut through her despair. "You must not give way, or where shall we all be? There is no one else in this house fit to direct matters. Who manages the funeral? Who decides the guests? Who chooses the venue? Everything must be settled. Master Frederick is beside himself with weeping, and Master Hale has never been strong for such tasks; he wanders about as if lost. It is hard, I know, but death claims us all. You are fortunate never to have lost a friend until now."

Perhaps she was. Yet this specific loss felt unique, incomparable to any other tragedy in the world. Dixon's words offered no solace, but the unusual tenderness of the prim old servant's manner struck a chord deep within Margaret. Driven by a need to express her gratitude, she forced herself upright, offering a fragile smile to Dixon's anxious gaze before heading to summon Mr. Hale and Frederick.

Mr. Hale entered as if in a trance, moving with the unconscious drift of a sleepwalker, his eyes and mind occupied by worlds unseen. Frederick followed, brisk and attempting forced cheerfulness. He seized Margaret's hand, searched her eyes, and then broke down in tears. Throughout breakfast, Margaret struggled to fill the silence with trivialities, terrified that the reality of their shared last meal would overwhelm them with memories of the strained vigil they had kept by the sickbed.

After eating, Margaret approached her father regarding the funeral plans. He nodded vacantly, agreeing to suggestions that often contradicted one another, yielding no real decision. As she prepared to retreat to confer with Dixon, Mr. Hale gently signaled her to return.

"Ask Mr. Bell," he rasped, his voice hollow.

"Mr. Bell?" Margaret asked, startled. "Mr. Bell of Oxford?"

"Yes. He was my groom's-man."

The connection clicked instantly in Margaret's mind. "I will write today," she promised. Mr. Hale slumped back into his listlessness.

All morning, Margaret labored in a whirlwind of melancholy business, longing for rest that refused to come. Toward evening, Dixon returned with news that unsettled her deeply.

"I've done it, miss," Dixon reported. "I feared for Master Frederick; he sat so still I thought he'd suffer a stroke. He spent the entire day with poor Mrs. Hale, talking to her as if she were breathing. When I entered, he would freeze, confused and lost. I decided he needed rousing. I told him it wasn't safe for Master Frederick to remain here. Besides, I met a Southampton man earlier—the first I've seen since arriving in Milton. It was young Leonards, the draper's son, a scamp who plagued his father to death before running off to sea. I never liked him. He served aboard the *Orion* with Frederick, though I'm unsure if he was present during the mutiny."

"Did he recognize you?" Margaret asked, her interest sharpening.

"That's the problem," Dixon replied. "I didn't believe he'd know me unless I called out his name. Being a stranger in a strange land, I foolishly addressed him as 'cousin,' calling him a nasty, good-for-nothing fellow. He responded politely, asking who would have thought to see me here. I insisted he address me as an unmarried lady, noting I might have married well had I not been so particular. He was charming enough, but I wasn't to be trifled with. So, I turned the tables and asked after his father, whom I knew had disowned him, pretending we were close friends. Naturally, to spite me, he began questioning Master Frederick, mentioning the scandal, the hanging threat, and the hundred-pound reward. He claimed to be a disgrace to his family, all to wound me because I once helped old Mr. Leonards secure a good rating for George in Southampton. I retorted that other families had far more cause to blush. He laughed, claiming a 'confidential situation' and offering his patronage to anyone seeking to reform a vicious youth. He was waiting for a bus, but turned back to taunt me, suggesting we partner in trapping Lieutenant Hale for the reward. He left with a wicked grin, having won the upper hand."

Margaret felt a wave of discomfort. "Have you told Frederick?"

"No," Dixon admitted. "I was uneasy knowing Leonards was in town, but with so much else to worry about, I didn't press it. Seeing Master Frederick so rigid and glazed-eyed, I thought reminding him of his brother's danger might snap him out of his stupor."

"Oh, I'm not afraid of Mr. Bell," Margaret said, her voice tight. "But I dread Leonards. I must tell Frederick. What did he look like?"

"A dreadful sight," Dixon assured her. "Red whiskers I'd be ashamed to wear. Dressed in fustian like a laborer despite his claims of high status."

It became clear Frederick must leave immediately. He had already carved out a vital role in the family, promising to support his father and sister, caring for the living mother and mourning the dead. Now, he faced exile.

Just as Margaret contemplated this, sitting by the drawing-room fire while her father paced anxiously, Frederick entered. The extreme violence of his grief had subsided, replaced by a dimmed brightness. He approached Margaret, kissing her forehead.

"How pale you look, Margaret!" he whispered softly. "Everyone thinks of everyone else; no one thinks of you. Lie on the sofa; there is nothing left for you to do."

"That is the worst," Margaret murmured sadly, but she obeyed, lying down as her brother draped a shawl over her feet and sat on the floor beside her.

She recounted Dixon's encounter with Leonards. Frederick let out a long whistle of dismay. "I want to confront that young man. A worse sailor never sailed, nor a worse human being. Do you know the full story, Margaret?"

"Mamma told me," she replied.

"Well, when the decent sailors were indignant at our captain, this fellow sought favor! Pah! To think he is here! If he knew I was within twenty miles, he'd dig me up to settle old scores. I'd rather anyone had the hundred pounds they claim I'm worth than that rascal. What a pity Dixon couldn't be convinced to leave and secure her future!"

"Hush, Frederick!" Margaret pleaded.

Mr. Hale stepped forward, trembling and eager. Having overheard their conversation, he took Frederick's hands. "My boy, you must go. It is terrible, but unavoidable. You have done all you could; you have been a comfort to her."

"Papa, must he go?" Margaret begged, fighting her own sense of duty. "I feel I could face it, stand my trial. If only I could gather my evidence! I cannot bear being in the power of such a blackguard. In other times, I might have enjoyed this stolen visit; it holds all the charm of forbidden pleasures."

"One of my earliest memories," Margaret interjected gently, "is of you in great disgrace for stealing apples. We had trees laden with fruit, yet someone told you stolen fruit tastes sweetest, and you took it literally. You haven't changed much since then."

"Yes—you must go," Mr. Hale repeated, ignoring the detour to focus on the necessity. His mind was fixed on the departure, struggling to keep pace with his children's erratic thoughts.

Margaret and Frederick exchanged a look. That moment of shared understanding, conveyed silently through their eyes, would vanish if he left. They held onto the thought until it dissolved in sadness. Frederick broke the silence first.

"Do you know, Margaret, I nearly frightened Dixon and myself this afternoon? I was in my bedroom, hearing a ring at the front door. Assuming the caller had departed, I opened my door to greet Dixon, only to find her frowning and kicking me back into hiding. I stayed behind the door and heard a message delivered to Father's study. Could it have been shopmen?"

"Likely," Margaret said indifferently. "A small man came for orders around two o'clock."

"But this was not a small man—a large, powerful figure—and it was past four."

"It was Mr. Thornton," Mr. Hale stated flatly.

Relief washed over them at involving him in the discussion.

"Mr. Thornton!" Margaret exclaimed, surprised

"What! Remain to be denounced—dragged, it may be, in chains!" Werner had cried.

All the next day, the three sat together. Mr. Hale spoke scarcely at all, answering only when his children pressed him with questions, forcing him, as it were, into the present moment. Frederick's grief had vanished from sight and sound; the initial paroxysm had passed, leaving behind a man ashamed of having been so utterly battered down by emotion. Yet, his sorrow for the loss of his mother remained a deep, real feeling that would endure his entire life, though it was never to be spoken of again. Margaret, though less passionate at first, suffered more acutely now. At times she wept profusely, and even when discussing other matters, her manner possessed a mournful tenderness that deepened whenever her gaze fell upon Frederick and she contemplated his rapidly approaching departure. She was glad he was leaving, primarily for her father's sake, despite her personal anguish over the separation.

The anxious terror in which Mr. Hale lived, fearing his son might be detected and captured, far outweighed any pleasure he derived from Frederick's presence. His nervousness had intensified since Mrs. Hale's death, likely because he dwelt upon it more exclusively. He started at every unusual sound and found comfort only when Frederick sat out of the immediate view of anyone entering the room.

Towards evening, Mr. Hale said, "Will you go with Frederick to the station, Margaret? I shall want to know he is safely off. Will you bring me word that he is clear of Milton, at any rate?"

"Certainly," said Margaret. "I shall like it, if you won't be lonely without me, papa."

"No, no! I should always be fancying someone had known him and that he had been stopped, unless you could tell me you had seen him off. Go to the Outwood station. It is quite as near, and there are fewer people about. Take a cab there; there is less risk of his being seen. What time is your train, Fred?"

"Ten minutes past six; very nearly dark. So, what will you do, Margaret?"

"Oh, I can manage. I am getting very brave and very hard. It is a well-lighted road all the way home, even if it should be dark. Besides, I was out last week much later."

Margaret felt thankful when the parting was finally over—the farewell to both the dead mother and the living father. She hurried Frederick into the cab to shorten a scene she saw was bitterly painful to her father, who intended to accompany his son for one last look at his mother. Partly due to this haste, and partly owing to a common mistake in the "Railway Guide" regarding arrival times at smaller stations, they found themselves with nearly twenty minutes to spare upon reaching Outwood. The booking office was closed, preventing them from purchasing tickets immediately. Consequently, they descended the flight of steps leading to the ground level beside the railway. There lay a broad cinder path cutting diagonally across a field adjacent to the carriage road, and they walked back and forth along it for the few remaining minutes.

Margaret's hand rested in Frederick's arm. He grasped it affectionately.

"Margaret! I am going to consult Mr. Lennox regarding the chance of exculpating myself, so that I may return to England whenever I choose. This is more for your sake than for anyone else's. I cannot bear to think of your lonely position if anything happens to my father. He looks sadly changed—terribly shaken. I wish you could persuade him to consider the Cadiz plan; there are many reasons why. What could you do if he were taken away? You have no friends nearby. We are curiously bereft of relations."

Margaret could hardly restrain her tears at the tender anxiety with which Frederick brought this probable disaster before her, given how severely the cares of the last few months had affected Mr. Hale. Yet, she tried to rally herself.

"There have been such strange, unexpected changes in my life during these last two years that I feel more than ever it is not worth while to calculate too closely what I should do if some future event takes place. I try to think only upon the present."

She paused. They stood still for a moment, close to the stile leading into the road, bathed in the light of the setting sun. Frederick held her hand, looking into her face with wistful anxiety, reading there more care and trouble than she betrayed in her words.

"We shall write often to one another," she continued. "And I will promise—for I see it will set your mind at ease—to tell you every worry I have. Papa is—" She started slightly, a movement hardly visible, but Frederick felt the sudden jerk of the hand he held and turned his full face toward the road. Along the path, a horseman rode slowly, just passing the very stile where they stood.

Margaret bowed stiffly. Her bow was met with a rigid return.

"Who is that?" Frederick asked, almost before the rider was out of earshot.

"Mr. Thornton; you saw him before, you know," Margaret replied, appearing a little drooping and flushed.

"Only his back. He is an unprepossessing-looking fellow. What a scowl he has!"

"Something has happened to vex him," Margaret said apologetically. "You would not have thought him unprepossessing if you had seen him with mamma."

"I fancy it must be time to go and take my ticket. If I had known how dark it would be, we wouldn't have sent the cab back, Margaret."

"Oh, don't fidget about that. I can take a cab here if I like, or go back by the railway when I should have shops and people and lamps all the way from the Milton station-house. Don't think of me; take care of yourself. I am sick with the thought that Leonards may be in the same train with you. Look well into the carriage before you get in."

They returned to the station. Margaret insisted on going into the bright glare of the flaring gas inside to purchase her ticket. Several idle-looking young men were lounging about with the station master. Margaret believed she had seen the face of one of them before and returned him a proud look of offended dignity for his somewhat impertinent stare of undisguised admiration. She hastened to her brother, who stood outside, taking hold of his arm.

"Have you got your bag? Let us walk about here on the platform," she said, a little flurried at the prospect of being left alone so soon, her bravado leaking out faster than she cared to admit even to herself.

She heard a step following them along the flagstones; it halted when they halted, as they looked out along the line and listened to the whistle of the approaching train. Neither spoke; their hearts were too full. Another moment, and the train would be here; a minute more, and he would be gone. Margaret almost repented the urgency with which she had urged him to go to London; it seemed to throw additional chances of detection in his way. If he had sailed for Spain via Liverpool, he might have been away within two or three hours.

Frederick turned around, facing directly the lamp, where the gas darted upward in vivid anticipation of the arriving train. Suddenly, a man dressed as a railway porter started forward—a bad-looking individual who appeared to have drunk himself into a state of brutality, though his senses seemed perfectly sharp.

"By your leave, miss!" he said, roughly shoving Margaret aside and seizing Frederick by the collar. "Your name is Hale, I believe?"

In an instant, Margaret did not see how it happened; everything seemed to dance before her eyes. But through some quick act of wrestling, Frederick had tripped him up, sending the man crashing from the height of three or four feet—the elevation of the platform above the soft ground beside the tracks. He lay there.

"Run, run!" gasped Margaret. "The train is here. Was it Leonards? Oh, run! I will carry your bag."

Taking him by the arm, she pushed him forward with all her feeble strength. A door opened in a carriage; he jumped in. As he leaned out to cry, "God bless you, Margaret!" the train rushed past her, leaving her standing alone.

She was so terribly sick and faint that she was grateful to turn into the ladies' waiting-room and sit down for a brief respite. Initially, she could do nothing but gasp for breath. It had been such a hurry, such a sickening alarm, such a narrow escape. Had the train not arrived at that precise moment, the man would have risen again and called for assistance to arrest him. She wondered if the man had gotten up; she tried to recall if she had seen him move; she feared he might have been seriously hurt.

Venturing out, she found the platform brightly lit yet completely deserted. She went to the end and looked over, somewhat fearfully. Seeing no one, she was relieved she had inspected the area, for otherwise, terrible thoughts would haunt her dreams. Even as it was, she was so trembling and affrighted that she felt unable to walk home along the road, which indeed seemed lonely and dark as she gazed down upon it from the station's blaze. She decided to wait until the down train passed and take her seat in it. But what if Leonards recognized her as Frederick's companion? She peered about before venturing into the booking office to buy her ticket. Only a few railway officials were standing about, talking loudly among themselves.

"So Leonards has been

"Sleep on, my love, in thy cold bed, / Never to be disquieted! / My last Good Night—thou wilt not wake / Till I thy fate shall overtake." —Dr. King

The silence within the Hale household felt unnatural, a heavy stillness born of recent terror and chaotic noise. Mr. Hale had overseen the preparations for his daughter's return, ensuring every detail was attended to, only to retreat once more to his customary chair, sinking into one of his habitual, somber reveries. Dixon had enlisted Mary Higgins to manage the kitchen; however, her scolding lacked its usual volume, delivered instead in an angry whisper. To speak above one's breath seemed irreverent while a corpse lay within the walls.

Margaret resolved not to burden her father with the final, crowning fright of the ordeal. There was no utility in recounting it; the crisis had concluded favorably. Her sole fear remained that Mr. Lenards might somehow secure funds to pursue Frederick to London, hunting him down in the city. Yet, the odds against such a scheme succeeding were immense. Margaret decided not to torment herself with thoughts of prevention. Frederick would remain vigilant, and within a day or two, he would be safely out of England.

"I suppose we shall hear from Mr. Bell tomorrow," Margaret observed quietly.

"Yes," her father replied, his voice flat. "I suppose so."

"If he is able to travel, he should arrive by this evening."

"If he cannot come, I shall ask Mr. Thornton to accompany me to the funeral. I cannot go alone; I believe I would break down entirely."

"Do not ask Mr. Thornton, Papa. Let me go with you," Margaret pleaded, her tone impulsive.

"You?" Mr. Hale looked surprised. "My dear, women rarely attend funerals."

"No," Margaret corrected gently. "Because they lack the capacity to control themselves. Women of our station do not go because they possess no mastery over their emotions, yet feel too ashamed to display them. Poorer women often attend, unbothered by the appearance of being overcome with grief. But I promise you, Papa, if you permit me, I shall cause no trouble. Do not bring a stranger and exclude me. Please, dear Papa! If Mr. Bell cannot come, I shall go. I will not press my wishes against your will if he does."

Mr. Bell, unfortunately, could not come. He suffered from gout. His letter was profoundly affectionate, expressing deep regret for his absence. He hoped to visit them soon if invited, though his Milton estate demanded his immediate attention; his agent had insisted on his presence. Alternatively, he had avoided Milton as long as possible, finding solace only in the notion that seeing his old friend might allow him to offer comfort.

Margaret struggled immensely to dissuade her father from inviting Mr. Thornton. An indescribable repulsion rose within her at the prospect. The night before the funeral arrived, bearing a formal note from Mrs. Thornton to Miss Hale. Written at her son's request, it stated that their carriage would attend the funeral, provided it did not inconvenience the family.

Margaret tossed the note onto the table. "Oh, let us avoid these formalities," she said sharply. "Let us go alone—you and I, Papa. They do not truly care for us, or he would have offered to drive himself rather than propose sending an empty carriage."

"I thought you were so vehemently opposed to his involvement, Margaret," Mr. Hale remarked, surprised.

"And I am," she admitted. "I do not wish him to come at all; I particularly dislike the idea of asking him. But this... this mockery of mourning surprises me. I did not expect it from him."

Startled, she burst into tears. Having been so subdued in her grief, so considerate of others, and so gentle in all things, her sudden agitation confused her father. He could not comprehend her restless demeanor tonight. As he lavished tender reassurances upon her, her crying intensified. She endured a sleepless night, leaving her ill-prepared for the fresh anxiety brought by a letter from Frederick.

Mr. Lennox was out of town, with his clerk predicting a return by Tuesday at the latest, perhaps Monday. Consequently, Frederick decided to remain in London for a day or two longer. He had considered returning to Milton; the temptation had been strong. However, the image of Mr. Bell residing in his father's house, combined with the alarming incident at the railway station, compelled him to stay put. Margaret could be certain he would take every precaution against Leonards' tracking.

Margaret was grateful to receive this letter while her father was absent in his mother's room. Had he been present, he would have demanded she read it aloud, triggering a state of nervous alarm she would find impossible to soothe. Beyond the disturbing fact of Frederick's detention in London, the letter contained allusions to the near-recognition at Milton and the potential for pursuit, chilling her blood. How would such news affect her father?

Many times, Margaret repented of urging the consultation with Mr. Lennox. At the time, it seemed to entail minimal delay and added little risk of detection. Yet, every subsequent event proved it highly undesirable. She fought hard against this regret, battling the self-reproach for having spoken wisely at the moment, only to prove foolish in hindsight. Her father, however, was too physically and mentally depleted to engage in healthy reflection; he would succumb to morbid regrets over the past.

Summoning all her strength, Margaret supported her father. He seemed oblivious to the expectation of Frederick's letter, consumed by a single thought: that the last tangible sign of his wife's presence was to be removed from his sight and hidden forever. He trembled pitifully as the undertaker arranged the crape drapery around him. Looking wistfully at Margaret, he tottered toward her, murmuring, "Pray for me, Margaret. I have no strength left. I cannot pray. I surrender her because I must. I strive to endure it; indeed, I do. I know it is God's will. But I cannot comprehend why she died. Pray for me, Margaret, that I may find the faith to pray. It is a great trial, my child."

Seated beside him in the carriage, Margaret almost supported him in her arms, reciting noble verses of holy comfort and texts of faithful resignation until her voice never faltered. Paradoxically, she drew strength from the act. Her father's lips moved in rhythm with hers, repeating the familiar texts as her words prompted them. It was terrifying to witness his patient, struggling effort to achieve a resignation he lacked the inner strength to accept fully.

Margaret's fortitude nearly crumbled when Dixon, with a subtle gesture, drew her attention to Nicholas Higgins and his daughter, standing slightly apart but deeply attentive to the ceremony. Nicholas wore his usual fustian coat, save for a strip of black cloth sewn around his hat—a mark of mourning he had never displayed for his daughter Bessy's sake. Yet, Mr. Hale saw nothing. He continued to repeat the funeral service mechanically as the clergyman read it, sighing twice or thrice before concluding. Then, placing his hand on Margaret's arm, he silently begged to be led away, acting as if blind, requiring her as his faithful guide.

Dixon sobbed openly, covering her face with a handkerchief, so absorbed in her own grief that she failed to notice the dispersing crowd until someone spoke directly to her. It was Mr. Thornton. He had stood throughout the entire service, head bowed behind a cluster of mourners, unrecognized by anyone.

"I beg your pardon—but, can you tell me how Mr. Hale is? And Miss Hale, too? I should like to know how they both are."

"Of course, sir. They are much as is to be expected. Master is terribly broken down. Miss Hale bears up better than likely."

Mr. Thornton preferred to hear that she suffered naturally. First, there was enough selfishness in him to derive pleasure from the thought that his profound love might come to comfort and console her—the same passionate joy that stings a mother's heart when a drooping infant nestles close, dependent entirely upon her. This delicious vision of what might have been, which he had indulged in despite her rejections only days prior, was miserably shattered by the memory of the handsome young man with whom she had stood in such familiar confidence near the station.

"Miserably disturbed!" was not strong enough to describe it. He was haunted by the recollection, an agony that made him clench his fists tightly to subdue the pain. So far from home, at such a late hour! It required a monumental moral effort to revive his trust—once so absolute—in Margaret's purity and innocence. The moment the effort ceased, that trust collapsed, dead and powerless. Wild fantasies chased one another through his mind. Here was a fragment of miserable, gnawing confirmation.

"She bore up better than likely" under this grief. She must therefore harbor some hope, so bright that even in her affectionate nature, it could lighten the dark hours of a daughter newly bereft. Yes! He knew how she would love. He had loved her without failing to gain that instinctive understanding of her capabilities. Her soul would walk in glorious sunlight if any man, worthy through his power of love, could win her back. Even in mourning, she would rest with peaceful faith upon his sympathy. *His* sympathy! Whose? That of another man. The realization alone turned Mr. Thornton's pale, grave face doubly wan and stern at Dixon's reply.

"I suppose I may call," he said coldly

"Truth will fail thee never, never! Though thy bark be tempest-driven, Though each plank be rent and riven, Truth will bear thee on for ever!"

These words rang hollow in Margaret's ears. The "bearing up better than likely" was becoming a terrible strain upon her spirit. Occasionally, amidst what passed for cheerful conversation with her father, a sudden, sharp thought pierced her defenses: she realized with a jolt that she no longer had a mother. Beyond this grief lay the heavy cloud surrounding Frederick. The Sunday post had failed to deliver their London correspondence, and by Tuesday, Margaret was disheartened to find silence once more. She was left in total darkness regarding his plans, while her father suffered miserably under the weight of this uncertainty. It shattered his recently acquired habit of sitting quietly in one armchair for hours; instead, he paced restlessly up and down the room, then out into the hall, only to return and bang bedroom doors shut with no discernible purpose.

Margaret attempted to soothe him by reading aloud, but it became clear he could not concentrate for long. In those moments, she felt profound gratitude that she had kept secret the additional source of anxiety stemming from her encounter with Leonard. Her relief was compounded when Mr. Thornton arrived. His presence alone served to redirect her father's troubled thoughts. He approached Mr. Hale immediately, taking his hands and wringing them tightly without uttering a word. For a minute or two, holding them within his own, his face and eyes conveyed a depth of sympathy that transcended language.

Turning then to Margaret, he found her looking far from "better than likely." Her stately beauty was dimmed by sleeplessness and weeping. An expression of gentle, patient sadness settled upon her features—a sadness that bordered on immediate physical suffering. He had intended to greet her with the studied coldness of his recent demeanor, yet he could not resist stepping toward her as she stood slightly apart, rendered timid by his unpredictable behavior lately. With a voice tender enough to bring tears to her eyes, he spoke only the necessary commonplace words, causing her to turn away to conceal her emotion.

She retreated to her work, sitting down in profound silence. Mr. Thornton's heart beat quickly and strongly; for the moment, he entirely forgot the events of Outwood Lane. He attempted to converse with Mr. Hale, finding unusual delight in the interaction. As Margaret watched, she noted how Mr. Hale, usually pleased by Thornton's power and decisiveness, found in him today a particularly safe and sure harbor.

Suddenly, Dixon appeared at the door, his manner so flustered that Margaret felt a sickness rise in her stomach. Something had happened to Fred. There was no doubt of it. Fortunately, both her father and Mr. Thornton were deeply engrossed in their conversation.

"What is it, Dixon?" Margaret asked the moment she had closed the drawing-room door behind her.

"Come this way, miss," Dixon replied, ushering her into what had formerly been Mrs. Hale's bedchamber, now serving as Margaret's quarters since her father refused to sleep there following his wife's passing.

"It's nothing, miss," Dixon choked out. "Only a police inspector. He wishes to see you, miss. But I dare say, it concerns nothing serious."

"Did he name—" Margaret began, her voice barely audible.

"No, miss; he named nothing. He merely asked if you resided here and if he might speak with you. Martha admitted him and showed him into Master's study. I went to inquire if that would suffice, but no—it is you, miss, whom he seeks."

Margaret remained silent until her hand rested on the study door lock. Turning then, she warned, "Take care Papa does not come down. Mr. Thornton is with him now."

The inspector was nearly intimidated by the haughtiness of her manner as she entered. Indignation flickered in her countenance, yet it was so suppressed and controlled that it lent her an air of superb disdain. There was no surprise, no curiosity. She simply awaited the opening of his business. She asked no questions.

"I beg your pardon, ma'am, but my duty compels me to ask you a few plain questions," the inspector stated. "A man died at the Infirmary due to a fall sustained at Outwood Station between five and six o'clock on Thursday evening, the twenty-sixth. Initially, the fall seemed inconsequential, but physicians state it proved fatal due to an underlying internal condition and the victim's habitual drinking."

Her large, dark eyes widened slightly as they locked onto his face. Otherwise, she remained immovable. Her lips curved into a fuller line than usual, a result of muscle tension, though he lacked the knowledge of their normal state to recognize the sullen defiance in their sweep. She neither blanched nor trembled. She fixed him with an unblinking gaze. When he paused, she encouraged him, almost as if inviting him to finish his tale: "Well—go on!"

"It is suspected an inquest will be required," the inspector continued. "There is slight evidence suggesting the blow, push, or scuffle that caused the fall was provoked by the deceased's half-drunk insolence toward a young lady walking with the man who shoved him off the platform. This was witnessed by someone on the platform, who, however, deemed the incident minor and paid it no further heed. Furthermore, there is reason to identify the lady as yourself; in which case—"

"I was not there," Margaret interrupted, maintaining her expressionless stare with the vacant look of a sleepwalker.

The inspector bowed but said nothing. The woman before him displayed no emotion, no fluttering fear, no anxiety, and no desire to conclude the interview. The information he possessed was vague: a porter, rushing to prepare for a train, had seen a scuffle at the far end of the platform between Leonard and a gentleman accompanied by a lady, hearing no sound. Before the train reached full speed, the porter was nearly knocked down by the headlong rush of the enraged, half-intoxicated Leonard, who swore and cursed violently. The porter had not dwelt on it until the inspector unearthed his testimony. Subsequent inquiries at the station revealed that a young lady and gentleman had indeed been present around that hour; the lady was described as remarkably handsome and identified by a grocer's assistant as Miss Hale of Crampton, whose family shopped at his establishment. While certainty was lacking, the probability was high.

Leonard himself, driven mad by rage and pain, had fled to the nearest gin palace for solace. His drunken ravings went unnoticed by the busy waiters, who only recalled him rising abruptly and cursing himself for failing to utilize the electric telegraph sooner, for some purpose unknown. They believed he departed intending to use it. Overcome by pain or drink en route, he collapsed in the street, where police found him and transported him to the Infirmary. He never regained sufficient consciousness to provide a coherent account, though brief lucid intervals prompted authorities to summon a magistrate in hopes of securing a dying deposition. However, the magistrate found Leonard rambling about being at sea, confusing the names of captains and lieutenants with those of his fellow porters. His final words were a curse against the "Cornish trick," which he claimed had cost him a hundred pounds.

The inspector reviewed these facts in his mind: the vagueness of the evidence placing Margaret at the station, contrasted with her unflinching, calm denial. She stood awaiting his next word with a composure that seemed supreme.

"Then, madam, I have your denial that you were the lady accompanying the gentleman who struck the blow, or gave the push, resulting in the death of this poor man?"

A quick, sharp pain shot through Margaret's brain. *Oh God! that I knew Frederick were safe!*

A deep observer of human faces might have perceived the momentary agony flashing through her gloomy eyes, akin to the torture of a creature cornered. Yet, the inspector, though keen, was not profound. Still, he was struck by the nature of her response, which sounded like a mechanical repetition of her initial denial, unchanged and unmodified to address his specific query.

"I was not there," she said slowly and heavily. Throughout this exchange, she never closed her eyes or ceased that glassy, dream-like stare.

His quick suspicions were aroused by this dull echo of her former denial. It was as if she had forced herself into a single falsehood and been stunned into an inability to vary it. He deliberately closed his notebook of notes, then looked up. She had not moved an inch, appearing as solid as a great Egyptian statue.

"I hope you will not deem me impertinent when I say I may need to call upon you again. I may have to summon you to appear at the inquest to prove an alibi should my witnesses"—he gestured to the single individual who had recognized her—"persist in testifying to your presence at this unfortunate event."

He scrutinized her sharply. She remained perfectly quiet, showing no change of color or darkening shadow of guilt upon her proud face. He thought he saw a wince, but he did not truly know Margaret Hale. He felt somewhat abashed by her genuine composure. It must have been a mistake of identity.

"It is very unlikely, ma'am, that I shall have to proceed to such measures," he added. "I trust you will excuse me for performing my duty, even if it appears impertinent."

Margaret bowed her head as he turned toward the door. Her lips were stiff and dry; she could not muster even the common words of farewell. Suddenly, however, she stepped forward, opened the study door, and preceded him

"There's nought so finely spun But it cometh to the sun."

Mr. Thornton sat on and on. He sensed that his presence brought a quiet comfort to Mr. Hale, and he was moved by the silent, earnest plea within his friend's gaze that he stay a little longer—the soft, lingering "Don't go yet" that escaped his weary lips from time to time. He wondered where Margaret had gone, yet he lingered not with the hope of seeing her. In the hour of profound sorrow, and in the company of a soul so acutely aware of earthly transience, Mr. Thornton found himself unusually reasonable and self-possessed. He listened with deep attention to everything his friend said.

"Of death, and of the heavy lull, And of the brain that has grown dull."

It was remarkable how Mr. Thornton's mere presence unlocked secrets in Mr. Hale that remained tightly guarded even from Margaret. Perhaps Mr. Hale feared that Margaret's sympathy, so keen and vividly expressed, would overwhelm him; perhaps, to his speculative mind, doubts arose at such a critical juncture, clamoring to be resolved into certainty, and knowing she would recoil from hearing such uncertainties—or from believing him capable of conceiving them. Whatever the reason, he could unload his frozen thoughts, fears, and fancies onto Mr. Thornton more easily than onto anyone else. Mr. Thornton spoke little, yet every sentence he offered strengthened Mr. Hale's trust and affection. Did Mr. Hale pause while expressing a remembered agony? Mr. Thornton's two or three words would complete the thought, revealing how deeply he understood its weight. Was it a doubt, a fear, a wandering uncertainty seeking rest but finding none, its eyes blinded by tears? Instead of shock, Mr. Thornton seemed to have traversed that very mental landscape himself, able to point toward the precise ray of light that would illuminate the darkest corners. Though a man of action, busy in the world's great battles, there existed within him a deeper, quieter religion binding him to God, persisting through his wilfulness and mistakes, a depth Mr. Hale had never imagined. They never discussed such matters again, but this single conversation transformed them into unique companions, knitting them together in a bond that casual religious discourse could never forge. When all doors are open, where remains the Holy of Holies?

All the while, Margaret lay as still and pale as death upon the study floor. She had succumbed to her burden. It had been heavy in weight and long carried; she had been meek and patient until, suddenly, her faith faltered, and she groped in vain for aid. A pitiful contraction of suffering marred her beautiful brow, though no other sign of consciousness remained. The mouth, which had recently projected defiantly, was now relaxed and livid.

"E par che de la sua labbia si mova Uno spirto soave e pien d'amore, Chi va dicendo a l'anima: sospira!"

The first sign of returning life was a tremor around the lips—a mute, soundless attempt at speech—but her eyes remained closed, and the trembling sank back into stillness. Then, leaning feebly on her arms to steady herself, Margaret gathered her strength and rose. Her comb had fallen from her hair. With an instinctive desire to erase the marks of weakness and restore order, she searched for it, occasionally sitting down to regain her strength during the quest. Her head drooped forward; her hands lay meekly one upon the other. She tried to summon the memory of the temptation that had thrown her into such terror, recalling the specific details, but she could not. Only two facts remained clear: Frederick had been in danger of pursuit and detection in London, accused not merely of manslaughter but of leading a mutiny; and she had lied to save him. Yet there was solace: her lie had bought him time. If the inspector returned tomorrow, after she received the letter she longed for confirming her brother's safety, she would endure the shame, standing before a crowded courtroom as "a dog" who had done this thing. But if he returned sooner—as he had half-threatened, within hours—why! She would tell the lie again.
