Though she could not foresee how those words would emerge after such terrible introspection without betraying her falsehood, she knew repeating it would buy time for Frederick.

Her reverie broke with Dixon's entry into the room; she had just dismissed Mr. Thornton.

Hardly ten steps into the street had Mr. Thornton taken when an omnibus halted beside him. A man alighted and approached, tipping his hat. It was the police inspector.

Mr. Thornton had secured the inspector's first position in the force and had heard updates on his protégé's career, yet they rarely met. Initially, Mr. Thornton did not recognize him.

"My name is Watson—George Watson, sir, the one you employed—"

"Ah, yes! I've heard you're doing wonderfully."

"Yes, sir. I owe you thanks. But I ventured to speak to you regarding a small matter of business. I believe you were the magistrate who recorded the deposition of the poor man who died in the Infirmary last night."

"Yes," replied Mr. Thornton. "I heard a rambling account the clerk deemed useless. I'm afraid he was drunk, though undoubtedly he died violently. One of my mother's servants was betrothed to him, I believe, and she is distraught today. What of him?"

"Why, sir, his death is strangely connected with someone in the house I saw you leaving moments ago. I believe it was Mr. Hales's residence."

"Yes!" Mr. Thornton turned sharply, fixing the inspector with sudden interest. "What connection?"

"Well, sir, it appears I possess a fairly distinct chain of evidence implicating a gentleman who was walking with Miss Hale that night at Outwood Station as the man who struck or pushed Leonard off the platform, causing his death. However, the young lady denies being there at that time."

"Miss Hale denies she was there!" Mr. Thornton repeated, his voice altered. "Tell me, what evening was it? What time?"

"About six o'clock, Thursday the twenty-sixth."

They walked in silence for a minute or two. The inspector broke the quiet first.

"You see, sir, a coroner's inquest is likely. I have a young man who was quite positive—at least, initially. Since learning of the young lady's denial, he hesitates to testify, yet he remains convinced he saw Miss Hale at the station, walking with a gentleman, just minutes before a porter witnessed a scuffle. The porter attributed it to Leonard's impudence, but it resulted in the fall that killed him. Seeing you exit the very house, sir, I thought I might ask if... well, it is awkward dealing with disputed identities, and one does not doubt a respectable young woman's word without strong proof."

"And she denied being at the station that evening!" Mr. Thornton repeated in a low, brooding tone.

"Yes, sir, twice over, distinctly. I told her I would return, but since I saw you just as I left the young man who claimed it was her, I sought your advice, both as the magistrate who saw Leonard on his deathbed and as the gentleman who hired me."

"You were quite right," said Mr. Thornton. "Take no steps until you see me again."

"The young lady expects me to call, based on what I said."

"I only wish to delay you an hour. It is now three. Come to my warehouse at four."

"Very well, sir!"

They parted ways. Mr. Thornton hurried to his warehouse, sternly ordering his clerks to leave him undisturbed. He retreated to his private room and locked the door. Then he subjected himself to the torture of revisiting every detail. How could he have allowed himself the unsuspicious calm in which her tearful image had reflected only two hours prior, pitying her and forgetting the savage, distrustful jealousy that the sight of her—an unknown figure at such an hour, in such a place—had ignited? How could one so pure stoop from her dignified bearing? Yet, was it decorous? Was it? He hated himself for the fleeting thought, yet it thrilled him with its old power of attraction. And then this falsehood—how dreadful must the fear of shame be to drive truthful Margaret to lie! He could almost pity her. What would be the outcome? She could not have grasped the full extent of her peril; if an inquest occurred and the young man testified. Suddenly, he sprang up. There must be no inquest. He would save Margaret. He would assume the responsibility of preventing it. The medical testimony was vague; he had heard the attending surgeon mention an advanced internal disease destined to kill, noting that death might have been hastened by the fall or subsequent exposure. If he had known how Margaret would be ensnared, how he would stain her purity with a falsehood, he could have saved her with a single word. The decision regarding the inquest had hung in the balance the night before. Miss Hale might love another, be indifferent to him, but he would perform faithful acts of service she would never know. He might despise her, but the woman he once loved must be shielded from shame. To pledge oneself to a lie in a public court, or to admit preferring darkness to light, would be shameful.

Mr. Thornton appeared gray and stern as he emerged from his office, his clerks watching in wonder. He was absent for half an hour, and no less stern when he returned, despite the success of his mission.

He wrote two lines on a scrap of paper, enclosed it in an envelope, and sealed

"The steps of the bearers, heavy and slow, / The sobs of the mourners, deep and low." — Shelley.

At the appointed time, Margaret and Mr. Hale set out for their walk to visit Nicholas Higgins and his family. Both felt a peculiar, almost shy hesitation regarding their outing; it was the first time in many weeks they had deliberately ventured forth together, and their new clothes served as a constant, silent reminder of their recent bereavement. Yet, they drew close, bound by an unspoken sympathy.

When they arrived, Nicholas was seated by the fireside in his usual corner, though without his customary pipe. Leaning his head upon his hand, his elbow resting on his knee, he remained motionless until they entered. Though Margaret perceived the warm welcome in his gaze, he offered no greeting. Instead, he vigorously poked the dying embers, muttering, "Sit ye down, sit ye down. Fire's welly out," seemingly attempting to deflect attention from his own state. He appeared disheveled, sporting a black, unshaven beard that made his pale face look even paler, wearing a jacket that desperately needed patching.

"We thought we should have a good chance of finding you, just after dinner-time," Margaret said gently.

"We have had our sorrows too, since we saw you," Mr. Hale replied.

"Ay, ay. Sorrows is more plentiful than dinners just now; I reckon, my dinner hour stretches all o'er the day; yo're pretty sure of finding me," Nicholas grunted.

"Are you out of work?" Margaret asked.

"Ay," he replied shortly. After a moment's silence, he looked up. "I'm not wanting brass. Dunno yo' think it. Bess, poor lass, had a little stock under her pillow, ready to slip into my hand at the last moment, and Mary is fustian-cutting. But I'm out of work a'the same."

"We owe Mary some money," Mr. Hale interjected quickly, sensing the sting in Nicholas's pride.

"If hoo takes it, I'll turn her out o'doors. I'll bide inside these four walls, and she'll bide out. That's a'l," Nicholas declared fiercely.

"But we owe her many thanks for her kind service," Mr. Hale began again.

"I ne'er thanken your daughter theer for her deeds o'love to my poor wench. I ne'er could find th'words. I'Ise have to begin to try now, if yo' start making an ado about what little Mary could sarve yo'," Nicholas retorted, his voice thick with emotion.

"Is it because of the strike you're out of work?" Margaret asked softly.

"Strike's ended. It's o'er for this time. I'm out o'work because I ne'er asked for it. And I ne'er asked for it, because good words is scarce, and bad words is plentiful." He fell into a mood of surly riddling, yet Margaret sensed he craved an explanation.

"And good words are—"

"Asking for work. I reckon them's almost the best words that men can say. 'Gi'me work' means 'and I'll do it like a man.' Them's good words."

"And bad words are refusing you work when you ask for it."

"Ay. Bad words is saying, 'Aha, my fine chap! Yo've been true to yo'r order, and I'll be true to mine. Yo'did the best yo'could for them as wanted help; that's yo're way of being true to yo're kind: and I'll be true to mine. Yo've been a poor fool, as knowed no better nor be a true faithful fool. So go and be d—dto yo'. There's no work for yo'here.'"

"They're bad words," Nicholas continued, his bitterness mounting. "I'm not a fool; and if I was, folk ought to ha'taught me how to be wise after their fashion. I could m'appen ha'learnt, if any one had tried to teach me."

Mr. Hale ventured, "Would it not be worth while to ask your old master if he would take you back again? It might be a poor chance, but it would be a chance."

Nicholas looked up with a sharp, piercing glance, followed immediately by a low, bitter laugh. "Measter! if it's no offence, I'll ask yo'aquestion or two in my turn."

"You're quite welcome," Mr. Hale said.

"I reckon yo'n some way of earning your bread. Folk seldom lives i'Milton just for pleasure, if they can live anywhere else."

"You are quite right. I have some independent property, but my intention in settling in Milton was to become a private tutor."

"To teach folk. Well! I reckon they pay yo'for teaching them, dunnot they?"

"Yes," replied Mr. Hale, smiling. "I teach in order to get paid."

"And them that pays yo', dun they tell yo'what ten to do, or whatten not to do wi' the money they gives you in just payment for your pains—in fair exchange like?"

"No; to be sure not!"

"They dunnot say, 'Yo'may have a brother, or a friend as dear as a brother, who wants this here brass for a purpose both yo'and he think right; but yo'mun promise not give it to him. Yo'may see a good use, as yo'think, to put yo'r money to; but we don't think it good, and so if yo'spend it a-thatens we'll just leave off dealing with yo'.' They dunnot say that, dun they?"

"No: to be sure not!"

"Would yo'stand it if they did?"

"It would be some very hard pressure that would make me even think of submitting to such dictation."

"There's not the pressure on all the broad earth that would make me," Nicholas declared. "Now yo've got it. Yo've hit the bull's eye. Hampers—that's where I worked—makes their men pledge 'emselves they'll not give a penny to help th'Union or keep turn-outs fro'clemming. They may pledge and make pledge," he continued scornfully, "they nobbut make liars and hypocrites. And that's a less sin, to my mind, to make men's hearts so hard that they'll not do a kindness to them as needs it, or help on the right and just cause, though it goes again the strong hand. But I'll ne'er forswear mysel'for a'all the work the king could gi'me. I'm a member o'the Union; and I think it's the only thing to do the workmen any good. And I've been a turn-out, and known what it was to clem; so if I get a shilling, sixpence shall go to them if they axe it from me. Consequence is, I dunnot see where I'm to get a shilling."

"Is that rule about not contributing to the Union in force at all the mills?" Margaret asked.

"I cannot say. It's a new regulation at ours; and I reckon they'll find that they cannot stick to it. But it's in force now. By-and-by they'll find out, tyrants makes liars."

A brief pause ensued. Margaret hesitated, fearing to irritate a man already steeped in gloom, but finally, her thoughts escaped her. Her tone was soft, her manner reluctant, showing her unwillingness to speak unpleasantly, yet it only perplexed Higgins further.

"Do you remember poor Boucher saying that the Union was a tyrant? I think he said it was the worse tyrant of all. And I remember, at the time I agreed with him."

For a long while, Nicholas said nothing. He rested his head on his two hands, staring down into the fire, hiding his expression from view.

"I'll not deny but what th'Union finds it necessary to force a man into his own good. I'll speak truth. A man leads a dree life who's not i'the Union. But once i'the Union, his interests are taken care on better nor he could do it for himsel', or by himsel', for that matter. It's the only way working men can get their rights, by all joining together. More the members, more chance for each one separate man having justice done him. Government takes care o'fools and madmen; and if any man is inclined to do himsel'or his neighbour a hurt, it puts a bit of a check on him, whether he likes it or no. That's all we do i'the Union. We can't clap folk into prison; but we can make a man's life so heavy to be borne, that he's obliged to come in, and be wise and helpful in spite of himsel'. Boucher were a fool all along, and ne'er a worse fool than at th'last."

"He did you harm?" Margaret asked.

"Aye, that did he. We had public opinion on our side, till he and his sort began rioting and breaking laws. It were all o'er wi'the strike then."

"Then would it not have been far better to have left him alone, and not forced him to join the Union? He did you no good; and you drove him mad."

"Margaret," Mr. Hale warned in a low tone, sensing the storm gathering on Higgins's face.

"A spade! a rake! a hoe! A pickaxe or a bill! A hook to reap, or a scythe to mow, A flail or what ye will— And here's a ready hand To ply the needful tool, And skill'd enough, by lessons rough, In Labour's rugged school." — Hood.

Higgins's door remained locked the following day when Margaret and her father attempted to pay their call on the widow Boucher. However, an officious neighbor informed them that Higgins was actually away on business of some sort. Before departing, he had indeed visited Mrs. Boucher, though the encounter proved deeply unsatisfactory. The widow viewed herself as a grievously wronged woman due to her husband's suicide, a sentiment containing enough truth to make refutation nearly impossible. Nevertheless, it was distressing to witness how entirely her thoughts were consumed by her own plight and status. This selfishness even permeated her relationship with her children, whom she regarded merely as burdens despite possessing a somewhat animalistic affection for them.

Margaret endeavored to connect with one or two of the children, while Mr. Hale struggled to elevate the widow's thoughts above mere helpless querulousness. He discovered that the children were far truer and simpler mourners than their mother. "Daddy" had been kind to them; each could recount, in an eager, stammering voice, instances of tenderness or indulgence shown by the lost father. "Is yon thing upstairs really him?" one asked timidly. "It doesn't look like him. I'm feared on it, and I never was feared o' daddy." Margaret's heart bled to learn that the mother, in her desperate demand for sympathy, had dragged her children upstairs to gaze upon their dead, disfigured father. It was a cruel intermingling of coarse horror with the profundity of natural grief. She tried to redirect their minds toward practical matters: what they could do for their mother, or, phrased differently, what their father would have wished of them.

Margaret proved more successful than Mr. Hale in these efforts. Seeing their small duties lay within reach, the children began attempting to tidy the slatternly room under her guidance. Yet, Mr. Hale set a standard too high and offered a view too abstract for the indolent invalid. He could not rouse her torpid mind to imagine her husband's misery prior to his final, terrible step; she could only perceive the tragedy through the lens of her own suffering. Unable to comprehend the enduring mercy of God who had not intervened to save her prostrate husband from drowning, she simultaneously blamed her husband for falling into such drear despair, denied him any excuse for his rash act, yet remained inveterate in abusing anyone who might have driven him to such desperation.

She listed the masters—Mr. Thornton in particular, whose mill had been attacked by Boucher and who had subsequently withdrawn the warrant for his arrest on charges of rioting—the Union, of which Higgins was a representative, the numerous, hungry, and noisy children. To the widow, these constituted a single great army of personal enemies responsible for her current state as a helpless widow. Margaret heard enough of this unreasonableness to become thoroughly disheartened, finding it impossible to comfort her father upon their return.

"It is the town life," Margaret observed. "Their nerves are quickened by the haste and bustle and speed of everything around them, to say nothing of the confinement in these pent-up houses, which of itself induces depression and worry of spirits. In the country, people live so much more outdoors, even the children, even in winter."

"But people must live in towns," Mr. Hale replied. "And in the country, some develop such stagnant habits of mind that they become almost fatalists."

"Yes," Margaret acknowledged. "Each mode of life produces its own trials and temptations. The dweller in towns finds it as difficult to be patient as the country-bred man finds it to be active and equal to unwonted emergencies. Both struggle to realize a future: the urbanite because the present is so living, hurrying, and close around him; the rural inhabitant because his life tempts him to revel in mere animal existence, unaware of, and unconcerned with, any pungent pleasure attainable through planning and self-denial."

"And thus, both the necessity for engrossment and the stupid contentment in the present produce similar effects. But this poor Mrs. Boucher! How little we can do for her!"

"And yet we dare not leave her without effort, though it may seem futile. Oh, Papa! It's a hard world to live in!"

"So it is, my child. We feel it acutely just now, certainly; but we have known happiness even amidst our sorrow. What a pleasure Frederick's visit was!"

"Yes, it was," Margaret said brightly. "Such a charming, snatched, forbidden thing."

Suddenly, she stopped speaking. She had spoiled the memory of Frederick's visit through her own cowardice. Of all faults, she most despised in others the lack of bravery; the meanness of heart that leads to untruth. Here, she had been guilty of it! Then came the thought of Mr. Thornton's knowledge of her falsehood. She wondered if she would have minded detection half so much from anyone else. She mentally rehearsed confessing to her Aunt Shaw, Edith, her father, Captain and Mr. Lennox, and finally, Frederick. The thought of the last knowing what she had done, even in his own behalf, was the most painful, considering the fresh flush of mutual regard between brother and sister. Yet, even a fall in Frederick's opinion paled before the shame, the shrinking shame, she felt at the prospect of meeting Mr. Thornton again.

Still, she longed to see him, to get it over, to understand where she stood in his estimation. Her cheeks burned as she recalled how proudly she had previously implied an objection to trade, citing the deceit inherent in passing off inferior goods or assuming credit for nonexistent wealth. She remembered Mr. Thornton's look of calm disdain, how in few words he conveyed that in the great scheme of commerce, all dishonest methods inevitably proved injurious in the long run. Testing such actions by the poor standard of success revealed folly rather than wisdom; every form of deceit in trade, as in other things, was condemned. She remembered herself, then strong in her own untempted truth, asking if buying cheapest and selling dearest lacked the transparent justice intimately connected with truth. She had used the word *chivalric*, and her father had corrected her with the higher term *Christian*, drawing the argument onto himself while she sat silent beside him with a slight feeling of contempt.

*No more contempt for her! No more talk about the chivalric!* Henceforth, she must feel humiliated and disgraced in his sight. But when would she see him? Her heart leaped with apprehension at every ring of the doorbell, yet when silence returned, she felt strangely saddened and sick at heart at each disappointment.

It was evident her father expected to see him and was surprised by his absence. The truth was, there were points in their previous conversation they had no time to expand upon, but it had been understood that if possible, the following evening—or at least the first evening Mr. Thornton could spare—they should meet for further discussion. Mr. Hale had looked forward to this meeting eagerly since they parted. Having not yet resumed instruction to his pupils, which he had ceased at the onset of his wife's serious illness, he had fewer occupations than usual; the intense interest generated by Boucher's suicide had driven him back with renewed vigor to his speculations. He was restless all evening, muttering, "I quite expected to have seen Mr. Thornton. I think the messenger who brought the book last night must have had some note and forgot to deliver it. Do you think there has been any message left today?"

"I will go and inquire, Papa," Margaret said.

"Stay, there's a ring!" she exclaimed, sitting down instantly and bending her head attentively over her work. She heard a step on the stairs, but it was only one, and she knew it was Dixon's. Lifting her head, she sighed, believing she felt glad.

"It's that Higgins, sir. He wants to see you, or else Miss Hale. Or it might be Miss Hale first, and then you, sir; for he's in a strange kind of way."

"He had better come up here, Dixon; then he can see us both and choose which he likes for his listener."

"Oh! Very well, sir. I've no wish to hear what he's got to say, I'm sure; only, if you could see his shoes, I'm sure you'd say the kitchen was the fitter place."

"He can wipe them, I suppose," Mr. Hale said.

Dixon flung off to bid him walk upstairs. Margaret was slightly mollified when Higgins looked at his feet with a hesitating air. Sitting down on the bottom stair, he removed the offending shoes and, without a word, walked upstairs.

"Sarvant, sir!" he said, slicking his hair down upon entering the room. "If hoo'l excuse me (glancing at Margaret) for being i' my stockings; I've been tramping a' day, and streets is none o' th' cleanest."

Margaret thought fatigue might account for the change in his manner; he was unusually quiet and subdued, evidently struggling to articulate why he had come. Mr. Hale's ever-ready sympathy for shyness or hesitation prompted him to assist.

"We shall have tea up directly, and then you'll take a cup with us, Mr. Higgins

"Then proudly, proudly up she rose,
Tho' the tear was in her eye,
Whate'er ye say, think what ye may,
Ye's get na word from me!" — *A Scotch Ballad.*

To Mr. Thornton, Margaret's deception was not merely a breach of truth; it was a specific betrayal that pointed toward another lover. He could not shake the memory of the intimate glance exchanged between her and another man, nor the posture of confident familiarity—or perhaps even endearment—that had marked their interaction. This image haunted him relentlessly, a phantom picture that followed him everywhere. Compounding this sting was the setting: the dusky twilight, the distance from home, and the relative solitude of the location. While his higher nature initially rationalized these factors as accidental or innocent, the reality gnawed at him. Had he any grounds to deny her the right to love? Her rejection of his affections had been explicit. Yet, granting her that freedom, she could easily have been led astray into lingering walks and delayed hours. But the lie itself was the poison. It revealed a fatal consciousness of wrongdoing, a need to conceal something unlike the open honesty she usually displayed.

Thornton granted her the credit of knowing she had erred, yet it offered no comfort. The misery stemmed from his passionate love; he viewed her, despite her flaws, as superior to any other woman. Yet, he believed her heart belonged entirely to another—a dark, slight, elegant, and handsome figure—while he remained rough, stern, and heavily built. The very falsehood that stained her character served as proof of her blind devotion to this rival. He festered in an agony of jealous rage, recalling her tender glances and fond detainment. He mocked his own earlier gratitude for the way she had mechanically shielded him from the mob's fury; now he saw how soft and bewitching she appeared when with a man she truly loved. He replayed her sharp words in his mind: *"There was not a man in all that crowd for whom she would not have done as much, far more readily than for him."* He had shared the mob's desire to prevent bloodshed, but this hidden lover claimed her loyalty exclusively, sharing in her looks, her words, her physical touch, and her lies.

Thornton realized he had never been so irritable. Every query elicited a curt, bark-like response, a behavior that wounded his pride. He prided himself on self-control, and he resolved to master it. Consequently, his demeanor shifted to a cold deliberation, though his thoughts remained harsher than usual. He spent his evenings pacing restlessly around the house, a habit that would have exasperated anyone else, including his mother, who struggled to maintain patience with her beloved son.

"Can you stop? Can you sit for a moment?" Mrs. Thornton asked, interrupting his endless stride. "I have something to say to you if you'd abandon that perpetual walking."

He seated himself instantly on a chair against the wall.

"I wish to speak of Betsy. She insists on leaving us. Her lover's death has shattered her spirits, rendering her unable to focus on her work."

"Very well. Presumably, other cooks can be found."

"That is so typical of a man. It isn't just the cooking; she knows every corner of our household. Moreover, she mentioned something concerning your friend, Miss Hale."

"Miss Hale is no friend of mine. Mr. Hale is my friend."

"I am glad to hear you say that, for if she were your friend, Betsy's account would surely annoy you."

"Speak," he commanded, maintaining the extreme quietude he had adopted recently.

"Betsy claims that the night her lover—whose name she forgets, though she always refers to him simply as 'he'—"

"Leonards," he supplied.

"The night Leonards was last seen at the station, performing his duties, Miss Hale was present. She was walking with a young man whom Bessy believes struck or pushed Leonards, causing his death."

"Leonards was not killed by a blow or a push."

"How can you be certain?"

"Because I questioned the infirmary surgeon directly. He stated there was a chronic internal disease resulting from Leonards' excessive drinking. The rapid deterioration during his intoxicated state confirmed that the final fatal episode was caused by alcoholism or a fall precipitated by it."

"A fall! What fall?"

"Caused by the blow or push Betsy describes."

"So, there was a blow or push?"

"I believe so."

"And who delivered it?"

"As no inquest was held due to the doctor's assessment, I cannot disclose the identity."

"But Miss Hale was there?"

Silence.

"And with a young man?"

Still silence. Finally, he snapped, "I tell you, mother, there was no inquest. No judicial inquiry, I mean."

"Betsy asserts that Woolmer, a man she knows working in a grocery store in Crampton, can testify that Miss Hale was at the station at that hour, pacing back and forth with a young man."

"I see no relevance to us. Miss Hale is free to conduct herself as she pleases."

"I am relieved to hear you say that," Mrs. Thornton replied eagerly. "It matters little to us, especially to you given recent events. However, I made a solemn promise to Mrs. Hale that I would not permit her daughter to stray without offering advice and remonstrance. I intend to convey my views on such conduct."

"I perceive no harm in her actions that evening," Mr. Thornton said, rising and moving closer to the fireplace, turning his face away from the room.

"You would not have approved of Fanny being seen out after dark in such a secluded place with a young man. Let alone choosing a time when her mother lay unburied for such a stroll. Would you have relished having your sister noticed by a grocer's assistant for such behavior?"

"First, as I was once a draper's assistant myself, the opinion of a grocer's clerk holds no special weight regarding the morality of an act. Second, there is a profound distinction between Miss Hale and Fanny. I can imagine Miss Hale possesses weighty reasons that compel her to overlook apparent improprieties. I never knew Fanny to have such motives. Others must guard her. I believe Miss Hale guards herself."

"A charming characterization of your sister! Truly, John, one would think Miss Hale's actions had rendered you clear-eyed. She seduced you into an offer through a bold display of feigned affection, likely playing you off against this very young man, I have no doubt. Her entire conduct is now transparent to me. You believe he is her lover, do you? You agree to that?"

He turned abruptly, his face pale and grim. "Yes, mother. I do believe he is her lover." Upon speaking, he twisted in pain, leaning his forehead against his hand. Before she could respond, he spun back around with renewed sharpness:

"Mother. He is her lover, whoever he may be; yet she requires help and feminine counsel. There are difficulties, temptations unknown to me. I fear they exist. I do not wish to know their specifics, but since you have always been a good, tender mother to me, go to her. Gain her confidence and advise her on the proper course. I know something is wrong; some dread that must be a terrible torment to her."

"For God's sake, John!" Mrs. Thornton exclaimed, genuinely shocked. "What do you mean? What do you know?"

He offered no reply.

"John! Unless you speak, I shall assume the worst. You have no right to accuse her thus."

"Not against her, mother! I _could_ not speak against her."

"Well! You have no right to imply accusations without elaboration. Such half-truths destroy a woman's reputation."

"Her reputation! Mother, you do not dare—" He faced her, his eyes blazing. Then, composing himself into determined dignity, he declared, "I will say no more than this: it is the simple truth, and I trust you believe me. I have compelling reason to believe Miss Hale is in distress connected to an attachment which, based on my knowledge of her character, is perfectly innocent and righteous. My reasons remain confidential. But never let me hear anyone suggest anything more serious than that she requires the counsel of a kind and gentle woman. You promised Mrs. Hale to be that woman!"

"No!" Mrs. Thornton retorted. "I am happy to admit I did not promise kindness and gentleness, for I felt at the time I lacked the capacity to extend them to someone of Miss Hale's character and disposition. I promised counsel and advice, exactly as I would give to my own daughter. I shall speak to her as I would to Fanny if she were wandering with a young man at dusk. I shall base my words solely on the circumstances I know, uninfluenced by the 'strong reasons' you refuse to share. Thus, I shall fulfill my promise and discharge my duty."

"She will never bear it," he cried passionately.

"She will have to bear it if I speak in her deceased mother's name."

"Well!" he broke away, frustrated. "Do not lecture me further. I cannot endure thinking of it. It is preferable that you speak to her than that she remains unspeaken to. Oh, that look of love! And that cursed lie, hinting at a terrible shame she sought to hide from the light in which I believed she lived perpetually! Oh, Margaret, Margaret! Mother, how you have tormented me! Oh, Margaret, could you not

"Nay, I have done; you get no more of me; And I am glad, yea glad with all my heart, That thus so clearly I myself am free." — Drayton.

Margaret shut herself up in her own room after leaving Mrs. Thornton's. She began to pace back and forth, her usual physical manifestation of inner turmoil, but soon recalled that in the small, thin-walled house, every footstep echoed from chamber to chamber. She sat down, waiting until she was certain Mrs. Thornton had safely departed. Only then did she force herself to replay the entire conversation, speech by speech, compelling her memory to traverse the painful dialogue once more.

Finally, rising, she spoke to herself in a mournful tone: "At least, her words do not strike home; they slide off me. For I am innocent of every motive she imputes to me. Yet, it is bitter to realize that anyone—any woman—could so readily believe such things of another. It is hard and sad. Where I have erred, she does not accuse me; she simply does not know. He never told her. I should have guessed he wouldn't!"

She lifted her chin, feigning pride in the delicacy of feeling Mr. Thornton had displayed. Then, a fresh thought struck her, causing her to press her hands tightly together: "He, too, must suspect poor Frederick of being some lover." A flush crept up her neck as the word passed her lips. "I see it now. It is not merely that he knows of my deceit, but that he believes someone else cares for me; and that I... Oh, dear! Oh, dear! What am I to do? What do I mean? Why do I care what he thinks, beyond the mere loss of his regard for my honesty? I cannot say. But I am wretched! Oh, how unhappy this past year has been! I have skipped from childhood straight into old age. I have had no youth, no womanhood; the hopes of womanhood have closed forever for me, for I shall never marry. I anticipate cares and sorrows as if I were already an elder woman, with the same tearful spirit. I am weary of this constant demand for strength. I could endure it for Papa, for that is a natural, pious duty. I think I could also endure—or possess the energy to resent—Mrs. Thornton's unjust, impertinent suspicions. But it is crushing to feel how completely he must misunderstand me. What has happened to make me so morbid today? I do not know. I only know I cannot help it. I must yield sometimes. No, I will not," she declared, springing to her feet. "I will not—I *will* not dwell on myself or my position. I won't dissect my own feelings. It serves no purpose now. Someday, if I live to be an old woman, I may sit by the fire, gazing into the embers, seeing the life that might have been."

Throughout this internal storm, she hastily dressed to leave the house, pausing only to wipe her eyes with impatient gestures, knowing the tears would return despite her bravado. "There are surely many women who make as sad a mistake as I have, discovering it only too late," she muttered. "And how proudly and impertinently I spoke to him that day! But I did not know then. It crept upon me gradually, and I know not where it began. Now, I shall not yield. It will be difficult to treat him as before, burdened by this misery, but I will remain calm, quiet, and say very little. Though, to be sure, I may not see him; he seems determined to avoid us. That would be worse than anything. And yet, no wonder he shuns me, believing what he must about me."

Stepping out, she moved rapidly toward the countryside, attempting to drown her reflections in the speed of her motion. As she paused on the doorstep upon her return, her father approached.

"Good girl!" he said. "You've been to Mrs. Boucher's. I was just intending to go there, if I had time, before dinner."

"No, Papa; I have not," Margaret replied, flushing. "I never thought of her. But I shall go directly after dinner; I'll go while you are taking your nap."

True to her word, Margaret proceeded to the Bouchers'. Mrs. Boucher was truly ill, dangerously so—not merely ailing. The kind, sensible neighbor who had visited earlier seemed to have assumed total charge of the household. Some children had been sent to neighbors; Mary Higgins had fetched the three youngest at dinner time, and since then, Nicholas had gone for the doctor. He had not yet returned. Mrs. Boucher was dying, and there was nothing left to do but wait.

Margaret felt she needed the doctor's opinion and decided to visit the Higginses in the interim. Perhaps she could learn if Nicholas had managed to approach Mr. Thornton. She found Nicholas busily crafting a penny top on the dresser, entertaining three tiny children who clung to him fearlessly. He, along with them, smiled at a particularly long spin, and Margaret noted that this look of absorbed happiness was a promising sign.

When the penny finally halted, little Johnny began to cry.

"Come here," Margaret said, lifting the child from the dresser and cradling him. Holding her watch to his ear, she asked Nicholas if he had seen Mr. Thornton.

Nicholas's expression shifted instantly. "Ay!" he said. "I've seen him and heard far too much about him."

"He refused you, then?" Margaret asked, sorrowfully.

"To be sure. I knew he'd do it all along. There's no mercy to be expected from masters like him. You're a stranger and a foreigner, unlikely to understand their ways; but I knew it."

"I am sorry I asked you. Was he angry? Did he not speak to you as Hamper did?"

"He wasn't overly civil!" Nicholas spun the penny again, seemingly for his own amusement as much as the children's. "Never mind your fretting. I'm exactly where I was. I'll go tramping tomorrow. I gave him as good as I got. I told him I didn't hold the high opinion of him that would lead me to seek him out a second time on my own; but you advised me to come, and I was beholden to you."

"You told him you were sent by me?"

"I dunno if I called you by your name. I don't think I did. I said a woman who knew no better had advised me to come and see if there was a soft spot in his heart."

"And he—?" Margaret prompted.

"Said I should tell you to mind your own business. That's the longest spin yet, my lads. And those are civil words compared to what he used to me. But never mind. We're where we were, and I'll break stones on the road before I let these little ones starve."

Margaret placed the struggling Johnny back onto the dresser. "I am sorry I asked you to go to Mr. Thornton's. I am disappointed in him."

A faint noise sounded behind her. Both she and Nicholas turned simultaneously to find Mr. Thornton standing there, his face etched with displeased surprise. Acting on an instant impulse, Margaret stepped aside before him, offering not a word, only a deep bow to conceal the sudden pallor that washed over her face. He bowed equally low in return before closing the door after her.

As she hurried toward Mrs. Boucher's, she heard the distinct sound of the latch clicking shut, a noise that seemed to complete the measure of her humiliation. He, too, was annoyed to find her there. Within his heart lay tenderness—a "soft place," as Nicholas had termed it—but he possessed a fierce pride in guarding it. He kept it sacred and safe, jealous of any circumstance that might breach its defenses. While he dreaded exposing this tenderness, he was equally eager that all men recognize his justice. He felt unjustified in having given such a scornful hearing to anyone who had waited with humble patience for five hours to speak to him. Whether the man had spoken saucily when given the chance mattered little to Mr. Thornton; he actually admired that audacity and felt conscious of his own irritability at the time, believing they were even-handed.

It was the five hours of waiting that struck him deeply. He had no five hours to spare himself, yet he had surrendered one or two hours of his rigorous intellectual and physical labor to investigate Higgins's story, examining the nature of his character and the tenor of his life. He tried not to admit it, but he was convinced Higgins spoke the truth. This conviction entered his mind like a spell, touching the latent tenderness of his heart. The patience of the man, the simple generosity of his motive (having learned of the quarrel between Boucher and Higgins), caused him to forget entirely the cold calculations of justice, leaping over them via a higher instinct.

He had come to offer Higgins employment, but he was more irritated to discover Margaret present than by hearing her final words. Upon realizing she was the woman who had urged Higgins to approach him, he dreaded admitting that thoughts of her—even as a motive for doing what was right—had influenced his actions.

"So that was the lady you spoke of as 'a woman'?" he said indignantly to Higgins. "You might have told me who she was."

"And then, perhaps, you would have spoken of her more civilly than you did. You'd have gotten a mother who might have kept your tongue in

Margaret had not anticipated deriving much personal joy from Mr. Bell's visit; she had merely looked forward to it for her father's sake. However, when her godfather arrived, she instantly assumed the most natural posture of friendship. He declared that she possessed no merit in being exactly what she was—a girl entirely to his liking—and attributed her charm to an hereditary power that allowed her to step into his regard and claim it. In response, she generously credited him with remaining so fresh and youthful despite wearing his Fellow's cap and gown.

"Fresh and young in warmth and kindness, I mean," Mr. Bell added. "I must admit, however, that I consider your opinions to be the oldest and mustiest I have encountered in a long time."

"Hear this daughter of yours, Hale! Her residence in Milton has thoroughly corrupted her. She is a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—"

"Papa, it is simply because I am advocating for the progress of commerce. Mr. Bell would have preferred keeping trade stagnant, exchanging wild-beast skins for acorns."

"No, no. I would dig the earth and grow potatoes. I would shave the wild-beast skin and turn the wool into broadcloth. Do not exaggerate, missy. But I am weary of this bustle. Everyone rushing over one another in their frantic haste to get rich."

"It is not everyone who can sit comfortably in college rooms and let their wealth accumulate without any exertion of their own. Undoubtedly, there are many men here who would be grateful if their property increased as yours has, without them needing to take any trouble," Mr. Hale remarked.

"I do not believe they would. It is the bustle and the struggle they crave. As for sitting still, learning from the past, or shaping the future through faithful work done in a prophetic spirit—Pooh! I do not believe there is a single man in Milton who knows how to sit still; and it is a great art."

"The people of Milton, I suspect, believe that Oxford men do not know how to move. It would be a very good thing if they mixed a little more."

"It might benefit the Miltoners. Many things might be beneficial for them that would be quite disagreeable for others."

"Are you not a Milton man yourself?" Margaret asked. "I should have thought you would be proud of your town."

"To be honest, I cannot see what there is to be proud of. If you will only come to Oxford, Margaret, I will show you a place worthy of glory."

"Well!" Mr. Hale interjected. "Mr. Thornton is coming to drink tea with us tonight, and he is as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. The two of you must try to make each other a little more liberal-minded."

"I do not wish to be more liberal-minded, thank you," Mr. Bell replied stiffly.

"Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, Papa?" Margaret whispered.

"Either to tea or shortly thereafter. He could not decide. He instructed us not to wait."

Mr. Thornton had resolved not to ask his mother how far she had progressed in her project of confronting Margaret regarding the impropriety of her conduct. He felt reasonably certain that if such an interview occurred, his mother's account of it would only serve to annoy and chagrin him, albeit colored by her own perspective. He shrank from hearing Margaret's name mentioned. While he blamed her, while he was jealous of her, while he renounced her, he nonetheless loved her sorely, in spite of himself. He dreamed of her approaching him with outstretched arms, radiating a lightness and gaiety that made him loathe her even as it allured him. Yet, the impression of this figure—devoid of Margaret's actual character, as if some evil spirit had possessed her form—was so deeply stamped upon his imagination that upon waking, he struggled to distinguish the Una from the Duessa. His dislike for the latter seemed to envelop and disfigure the former. Nevertheless, he was too proud to acknowledge his weakness by avoiding her sight. He neither sought opportunities to be in her company nor avoided them. To prove his self-control, he lingered over every task that afternoon, forcing every movement into unnatural slowness and deliberation. Consequently, it was past eight o'clock before he reached Mr. Hale's.

There were business arrangements to be settled in the study with Mr. Bell. The latter continued to sit by the fire, talking wearily long after all business was concluded, suggesting they might as well retire upstairs. Mr. Thornton refused to suggest moving; he chafed, finding Mr. Bell a most tedious companion. Secretly, Mr. Bell considered Mr. Thornton as brusque and curt as anyone he had ever met, lacking both intelligence and manners. Finally, a slight noise from the room above indicated it was time to move. They found Margaret with a letter open before her, eagerly discussing its contents with her father. Upon the gentlemen's entrance, the letter was immediately set aside, but Mr. Thornton's keen senses caught a few words exchanged between Mr. Hale and Mr. Bell.

"A letter from Henry Lennox. It makes Margaret very hopeful."

Mr. Bell nodded. Margaret turned red as a rose when Mr. Thornton looked at her. He had the strongest urge to rise and leave the room instantly, never to set foot in the house again.

"We were thinking," said Mr. Hale, "that you and Mr. Thornton had taken Margaret's advice and were each trying to convert the other, given how long you remained in the study."

"And you thought there would be nothing left of us but an opinion, like the Kilkenny cat's tail? Whose opinion did you suppose would possess the most obstinate vitality?"

Mr. Thornton had no idea what they were discussing and disdained to inquire. Mr. Hale politely explained.

"Mr. Thornton, we were accusing Mr. Bell this morning of a certain Oxonian medieval bigotry against his native town; and we—Margaret, I believe—suggested that it would do him good to associate a little with Milton manufacturers."

"I beg your pardon. Margaret thought it would do the Milton manufacturers good to associate a little more with Oxford men. Was that not so, Margaret?"

"I believe I thought it would do both parties good to see a little more of the other—I did not realize it was my idea any more than Papa's."

"So you see, Mr. Thornton, we ought to have been improving each other downstairs, instead of talking over vanished families of the Smiths and Harrisons. However, I am willing to do my part now. I wonder when you Milton men intend to live. All your lives seem spent gathering materials for existence."

"By 'living,' I suppose you mean enjoyment."

"Yes, enjoyment—I do not specify the type, because I trust we should both consider mere pleasure as very poor enjoyment."

"I would prefer the nature of the enjoyment be defined."

"Well! Enjoyment or leisure—enjoyment of the power and influence which money grants. You are all striving for money. What do you want it for?"

Mr. Thornton fell silent. Then he said, "I really do not know. But money is not what *I* strive for."

"What then?"

"It is a home question. I shall have to submit myself to such a catechist, and I am not sure I am prepared to do it."

"No!" said Mr. Hale. "Let us not be personal in our catechism. You are neither of you representative men; you are each too individual for that."

"I am not sure whether to consider that a compliment or not. I should like to be the representative of Oxford, with its beauty, its learning, and its proud old history. What do you say, Margaret; ought I to be flattered?"

"I do not know Oxford. But there is a difference between being the representative of a city and the representative man of its inhabitants."

"Very true, Miss Margaret. Now I recall, you were against me this morning, and were quite Miltonian and manufacturing in your preferences."

Margaret perceived the quick glance of surprise Mr. Thornton gave her and was annoyed at the construction he might place on Mr. Bell's remark. Mr. Bell continued—

"Oh! I wish I could show you our High Street—our Radcliffe Square. I am omitting our colleges, just as I give Mr. Thornton leave to omit his factories when speaking of the charms of Milton. I have a right to abuse my birthplace. Remember, I am a Milton man."

Mr. Thornton was annoyed more than he should have been by Mr. Bell's remarks. He was not in a mood for joking. At another time, he could have enjoyed Mr. Bell's half-testy condemnation of a town where life varied so greatly from his habits; but now, he was sufficiently galled to attempt defending what was never meant to be seriously attacked.

"I do not set up Milton as a model of a town."

"Not in architecture?" Mr. Bell asked slyly.

"No! We have been too busy to attend to mere outward appearances."

"Do not say *mere* outward appearances," Mr. Hale said gently. "They impress us all, from childhood upward—every day of our lives."

"Wait a little while," said Mr. Thornton. "Remember we are of a different race from the Greeks, to whom beauty was everything, and to whom Mr. Bell might speak of a life of leisure and serene enjoyment, much of which entered through their outward senses. I do not mean to despise them, any more than I would ape them. But I belong to Teutonic blood;

Winter was deepening, yet the days grew longer without bringing the promised brightness of hope that usually greets a February sun. Mrs. Thornton had entirely withdrawn from the household. Mr. Thornton visited occasionally, but his calls were strictly limited to the study and addressed solely to her father. Mr. Hale remarked that Mr. Thornton remained unchanged; indeed, the scarcity of their meetings seemed to heighten the value Mr. Hale placed upon them. From what Margaret could discern of their conversations, there was no trace of resentment or vexation in Mr. Thornton's conduct regarding the cessation of his visits. Rather, his business affairs, complicated by the recent strike, demanded a closeness of attention he had neglected the previous winter. Even Margaret noted that he spoke of her from time to time, always in a calm, friendly manner, neither avoiding nor actively seeking mention of her name.

Margaret herself lacked the spirits to lift her father's mood. The dreary peace of the present moment followed a prolonged period of anxiety and turmoil, interspersed with storms, which had drained her mind of its elasticity. She attempted to occupy herself by teaching the two younger Boucher children and striving diligently toward goodness. Yet, she worked hard, truly hard, for her heart seemed dead to the fruits of her efforts. Though she performed her duties punctually and painfully, she remained as far removed from cheerfulness as ever; her life continued to feel bleak and desolate. The only task she executed well was born of unconscious piety: the silent comforting and consoling of her father. Every mood of his found a ready sympathizer in Margaret; every wish of his, however quiet or hesitant, she strove to foresee and fulfill. Her spirit of obedience was meek and complete.

March brought news of Frederick's marriage. Letters arrived from him and Dolores; hers were written in a mix of Spanish and English, natural enough, while his were peppered with linguistic inversions showing how deeply his bride's idiom was taking root. Upon receiving Henry Lennox's letter—which announced the slim hope of Frederick clearing himself at a court-martial due to missing witnesses—Frederick had penned a vehement missive to Margaret. He renounced England as his country, wishing to "unnative" himself, declaring he would refuse a pardon or live in the land if permitted. At first, these words caused Margaret to weep bitterly, seeming so unnatural. However, upon reflection, she perceived them merely as the poignant expression of a hope crushed by disappointment. She realized there was nothing left but patience. In his subsequent letters, Frederick spoke so joyfully of the future that he showed no thought for the past, allowing Margaret to find a purpose for the patience she had been cultivating.

The charming, timid letters from Dolores began to hold a fascination for both Margaret and her father. The young Spaniard was clearly eager to make a favorable impression on her lover's English relations; her feminine care peeked out at every erasure. The announcement of the marriage was accompanied by a magnificent black lace mantilla, chosen by Dolores herself for her unseen sister-in-law—a woman Frederick described as a paragon of beauty, wisdom, and virtue. This union elevated Frederick's worldly status to heights they could scarcely desire. Barbour and Company, one of Spain's most extensive trading houses, welcomed him as a junior partner. Margaret smiled faintly and then sighed as memories of her old tirades against trade resurfaced. Here was her *preux chevalier*, her knightly brother, transformed into a merchant and trader! Yet, she immediately rebelled against herself, protesting silently against the confusion implied between a Spanish merchant and a Milton mill-owner. Regardless, trade or no trade, Frederick was exceedingly happy. Dolores must be charming, and the mantilla was exquisite! With that, she returned to her present reality.

That spring, her father occasionally suffered from a difficulty in breathing that distressed him greatly. Margaret was less alarmed, noting that the condition vanished completely during intervals, yet she remained so desperate to see him rid of it entirely that she urged him urgently to accept Mr. Bell's invitation to visit him in Oxford in April. Mr. Bell's invitation extended to Margaret as well; indeed, he wrote a special letter commanding her attendance. Still, Margaret felt it would be a greater relief to remain quietly at home, entirely free from responsibility, resting her mind and heart in a way she had not managed for over two years.

When her father finally drove off toward the railroad station, Margaret acutely felt the immense weight of pressure that had previously borne down on her time and spirits. It was astonishing, almost staggering, to realize how much liberty she possessed: no one depended on her for cheering care or even happiness; there was no invalid to plan for or think for. She could be idle, silent, and forgetful—and, most surprisingly, she could be unhappy if she chose. For months, her personal cares and troubles had been stuffed into a dark cupboard, but now she had the leisure to retrieve them, to mourn over them, to study their nature, and to seek the true method of subduing them into elements of peace. For weeks, she had been vaguely conscious of their existence, hidden from view. Now, she would confront them all at once, assigning each its proper role in her life. Sitting almost motionless for hours in the drawing-room, she reviewed the bitterness of every memory with unwavering resolve. Once, at the stinging thought of the faithlessness that spawned such an abasing falsehood, she cried aloud.

She refused to acknowledge the force of the temptation anymore. Her plans for Frederick had all failed; the temptation lay there as a dead mockery, never possessing life within it. The lie had been despicably foolish in the light of subsequent events, proving that faith in the power of truth was infinitely wiser.

In her nervous agitation, she unconsciously opened a book belonging to her father lying on the table. The words that caught her eye seemed crafted specifically for her current state of acute self-abasement:

*"Je ne voudrois pas reprendre mon cœur en ceste sorte... mais je voudrois le corriger par voye de compassion. Or sus, mon pauvre cœur, nous voilà tombe dans la fosse... Revelons-nous, et quittons-la pour jamais, reclamons la misericorde de Dieu... remettons-nous au chemin de l'humilité. Courage, soyons meshuy sur nos gardes..."*

"The way of humility," Margaret thought. "Ah, that is what I have missed! But courage, little heart. We will turn back, and by God's help, we may find the lost path."

Rising immediately, she determined to engage in work that would draw her out of herself. Passing the drawing-room door on her way upstairs, she summoned Martha and tried to pierce the grave, respectful, servant-like manner that had crusted over her individuality with almost mechanical obedience. It was difficult to coax Martha into speaking of personal matters, but eventually, mentioning Mrs. Thornton struck the right chord. Martha's face brightened, and under slight encouragement, she spun a long tale of how her father, in his early life, had been connected to Mrs. Thornton's husband—perhaps even doing him a kindness, though Martha barely knew the details as it happened when she was a small child. Circumstances had separated the families until Martha was nearly grown, when her father, having sunk lower from his original occupation as a warehouse clerk, and her mother deceased, faced ruin. It was Mrs. Thornton who sought them out, providing for them and caring for them.

"I had the fever and was delicate," Martha explained. "Mrs. Thornton and Mr. Thornton never rested until they nursed me up in their own house and sent me to the sea. The doctor said the fever was catching, but they cared not for that—not even Miss Fanny, who went visiting the folks she intended to marry into. So, though she was afraid at the time, it all ended well."

"Miss Fanny going to be married!" Margaret exclaimed.

"Yes; to a wealthy gentleman, though he is much older than she. His name is Watson, and his mills are somewhere beyond Hayleigh. It's a very good match, despite his gray hair."

At this news, Margaret fell silent long enough for Martha to regain her propriety and habitual brevity. She swept up the hearth, asked when to prepare tea, and departed with the same wooden countenance with which she had entered. Margaret had to check herself from indulging a habit she had recently acquired: imagining how every event concerning Mr. Thornton would affect him, wondering if he would like or dislike it.

The next day she taught the little Boucher children, took a long walk, and concluded with a visit to Mary Higgins. To Margaret's surprise, she found Nicholas already home; the lengthening daylight had deceived her regarding the late hour. He, too, seemed to have entered further into the path of humility; he was quieter and less self-asserting.

"So the old gentleman is away on his travels, is he?" Nicholas said. "The little ones told me so. Eh! but they're sharp ones, I almost think they beat my own wenches for sharpness, though 'tis wrong to say so, and one of 'em is in her grave. There's something in the weather, I reckon, that sets folk wandering. My master, the one at the shop yonder, is spinning about the world somewhere."

"Is that why you're home so early tonight?" Margaret asked innocently.

"You know nought about it, that's all," he replied contemptuously. "I'm not one with two faces—one for my master and another for his back. I counted all the clocks in the town striking before I'd leave my work. No! Yon

"When some beloved voice that was to you
Both sound and sweetness, faileth suddenly,
And silence, against which you dare not cry,
Aches round you like a strong disease and new,—
What hope? what help? what music will undo
That silence to your sense?"

The blow had struck with terrible force. Margaret collapsed into a state of utter prostration. It was not a breakdown marked by sobs or tears, nor did she find solace in words. She lay upon the sofa, her eyes closed, speaking only in faint whispers when addressed directly. Mr. Bell sat beside her, deeply perplexed. He dared not abandon her, yet he dared not suggest returning to Oxford immediately; her physical exhaustion was absolute, rendering any such journey impossible, regardless of the painful sights awaiting her there.

Seated by the dying fire, Mr. Bell wrestled with his dilemma. Margaret remained motionless, almost breathless, beside him. He refused to leave her even to dine with Dixon, who had prepared a meal downstairs and offered it with sobbing hospitality. A plate of food was carried up to him. Usually fastidious and discerning regarding flavor, Mr. Bell found the devilled chicken tasting now of sawdust. He minced some of the fowl for Margaret, seasoning it heavily, but when Dixon attempted to feed her according to his instructions, her languid shake of the head indicated that in her current condition, food would only choke her rather than nourish her.

Mr. Bell sighed heavily, rose from his easy chair with stiffened legs from the journey, and followed Dixon out of the room. "I cannot leave her," he muttered. "I must write to them at Oxford to ensure preparations are underway; they can proceed until my arrival. Could not Mrs. Lennox come to her? I shall write instructing her to do so. The girl needs a female friend nearby, someone to coax her into a proper release of grief."

Dixon was weeping profusely, enough for two, but after composing herself, she informed Mr. Bell that Mrs. Lennox was too close to her confinement to travel. "Very well," Mr. Bell replied. "We must summon Mrs. Shaw. Has she returned to England?"
"Yes, sir, she has; but I suspect she will not wish to leave Mrs. Lennox during such a critical time," Dixon said, disapproving of a stranger sharing her burden of caring for Margaret.
"Critical time, be—" Mr. Bell cut himself off with a cough. "She could have contentedly resided in Venice or Naples, or some of those Papal territories, during the last 'critical period' which occurred in Corfu, I believe. And what significance does that little prosperous woman's 'critical time' hold compared to this poor creature there—this helpless, homeless, friendless Margaret—lying as still on that sofa as if it were an altar-tomb, and she the marble statue upon it. Tell Mrs. Shaw she must come. Arrange a room for her by tomorrow night. I will ensure her arrival."

Consequently, Mr. Bell penned a letter. Mrs. Shaw later declared, with many tears, that it resembled one written by the General when preparing for a gout attack, promising to cherish it forever. Had he merely requested or urged her attendance, implying refusal was an option, she might have hesitated despite her genuine sympathy. It required the sharp, unceremonious command to overcome her inertia, prompting her to pack her belongings under the supervision of her maid.

Edith, dressed in cap, shawl, and tears, emerged onto the landing as Captain Lennox assisted his mother toward the carriage. "Do not forget, Mother," she cried. "Margaret must come to live with us. Sholto departs for Oxford on Wednesday; you must instruct Mr. Bell to notify him of our expected arrival. And if Sholto requires you, he can travel from Oxford to Milton. Do not forget, Mother; you are to bring Margaret back."

Edith retreated into the drawing-room, where Mr. Henry Lennox sat cutting open a new review. Without looking up, he remarked, "If you dislike Sholto's prolonged absence, Edith, I hope you will permit me to descend to Milton and render whatever assistance I can."
"Oh, thank you," Edith replied. "Old Mr. Bell will undoubtedly manage everything; additional aid may not be necessary. One rarely expects much *savoir-faire* from a resident Fellow. However, dearest, darling Margaret—it will be wonderful to have her here again. You were both great allies years ago."
"Were we?" he asked indifferently, feigning interest in the review. "Well, perhaps not—I forget. I was so consumed with thoughts of Sholto. But surely it is fortunate that if my uncle were to pass away, it should be precisely now, when we have returned home, settled in the ancestral house, and fully prepared to receive Margaret? Poor soul! What a transition awaits her from Milton! I shall procure new chintz for her bedroom, make it appear fresh and bright, and lift her spirits somewhat."

Mrs. Shaw traveled to Milton in a similar vein of benevolence, oscillating between dread of the initial reunion and fantasies of swiftly removing Margaret from "that dreadful place" to the comforting amenities of Harley Street. "Oh dear!" she murmured to her maid, gazing at the smokestacks. "Look at those chimneys! My poor sister Hale! I scarcely could have rested in Naples had I foreseen such a scene! I ought to have come and fetched her and Margaret away." Internally, she admitted she had always considered her brother-in-law somewhat weak, but never so frail as now, witnessing the exchange of the lovely Helstone home for this setting.

Margaret remained unchanged: pale, immobile, silent, and tearless. They had informed her that Aunt Shaw was arriving, yet she displayed neither surprise, joy, nor aversion. Mr. Bell, whose appetite had returned and whom Dixon diligently catered to, vainly urged her to taste sweetbreads stewed with oysters. She shook her head with the same quiet stubbornness as the previous day, forcing him to consume them himself.

However, Margaret was the first to detect the cab halting outside, bringing her aunt from the railway station. Her eyelids quivered, her lips flushed and trembled. Mr. Bell descended to greet Mrs. Shaw. Upon their ascent, Margaret stood, striving to steady her dizzy frame. Seeing her aunt, she stepped forward into open arms, finding immediate, passionate relief in the embrace, her tears flowing freely onto her aunt's shoulder.

All notions of quiet, habitual affection, of years-long tenderness, of kinship to the deceased—all those inexplicable similarities in gaze, tone, and gesture that seemed inherent to one family, reminding Margaret so vividly of her mother—conspired to melt and soften her numb heart into an overflow of warm tears.

Mr. Bell slipped out of the room and descended to the study, ordering a fire and attempting to distract himself by examining various books. Each volume evoked memories or suggestions of his departed friend. While this represented a shift from his two days of vigilantly watching Margaret, it was no diversion of thought. He welcomed hearing Mr. Thornton's voice inquire at the door. Dixon dismissed him rather cavalierly; with the arrival of Mrs. Shaw's maid, visions of former grandeur, the Beresford lineage, and the "station" (as she termed it) from which her young mistress had been cast, and to which she hoped to restore her, filled her mind. Dwelling on these thoughts with complacency while conversing with Mrs. Shaw's maid—skillfully extracting details of status and consequence connected to the Harley Street establishment for the instruction of the listening Martha—rendered Dixon somewhat haughty in her treatment of Milton's inhabitants. Thus, although she usually respected Mr. Thornton, she was as curt as she dared in informing him that none of the house's residents could be seen that evening.

It became slightly awkward when Mr. Bell opened the study door and called out, "Thornton! Is that you? Enter for a moment or two; I wish to speak with you."

Mr. Thornton entered, leaving Dixon to retreat to the kitchen, restoring her self-esteem with a grand tale of Sir John Beresford's coach-and-six during his tenure as High Sheriff. "I hardly knew what I intended to say to you anyway. It is merely dull enough to sit in a room where everything whispers of a dead friend. Yet Margaret and her aunt must have the drawing-room to themselves!"
"Has Mrs.—has her aunt arrived?" Mr. Thornton asked.
"Arrived? Yes! Maid and all. One might have expected her to come alone at such a time! Now I shall have to vacate and find my way to the Clarendon."
"You must not go to the Clarendon. We possess five or six vacant bedrooms at home."
"Well ventilated?"
"I trust my mother to ensure that."
"Then I shall merely ascend to bid that wan girl goodnight, offer my respects to her aunt, and depart with you immediately."

Mr. Bell lingered upstairs for some time. Mr. Thornton began to consider it excessive, as he was preoccupied with business and had barely spared time to visit Crampton to inquire about Miss Hale.

As they commenced their walk, Mr. Bell remarked, "I was detained by those women in the drawing-room. Mrs. Shaw is eager to return home—citing concerns for her daughter—and wishes Margaret to accompany her forthwith. Now, she is no more fit for travel than I am for flight. Furthermore,

"The meanest thing to which we bid adieu, Loses its meanness in the parting hour." — Elliott.

Mrs. Shaw harbored a dislike for Milton that was as vehement as her gentle nature allowed. To her, the town was a cacophony of noise and smoke; the poor walking the streets were filthy, the wealthy ladies overdressed, and not a single man, regardless of station, wore clothes that fitted properly. She was convinced that Margaret would never recover her lost strength while remaining in Milton, and she feared for herself, anticipating one of her own recurrent nervous attacks. "Margaret must return with me," she insisted, "and that quickly."

Though this was not necessarily the precise wording of her arguments, it was certainly the spirit behind them. Until Margaret, weak, weary, and broken-hearted, finally yielded a reluctant promise to accompany her aunt back to town immediately after Wednesday concluded. That Wednesday was to be mournful—the day Mr. Hale was to be interred, far from the homes he had known and far from the wife who lay lonely among strangers. This separation weighed heavily on Margaret; she lamented that if she had not succumbed to the overwhelming stupor during those initial tragic days, she might have managed the arrangements differently.

Before that sorrowful Wednesday arrived, Margaret received a letter from Mr. Bell.

"MY DEAR MARGARET:—I intended to return to Milton on Thursday, but unfortunately, circumstances arise wherein we, as Plymouth Fellows, are called upon to perform a specific duty, preventing my absence from my post. Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are currently here. The former appears to be a smart, well-meaning individual; he has proposed visiting Milton to assist you in searching for the will. Of course, there is no will, or you would have discovered it by now had you followed my previous instructions. Furthermore, the Captain declares he must take you and his mother-in-law home. Given his wife's current condition, I do not see how he can remain away longer than Friday.

However, your Dixon is trustworthy and can hold the fort until I arrive. Should there prove to be no will, I shall entrust the matter to my attorney in Milton, for I suspect this 'smart' Captain lacks significant business acumen. Nevertheless, his moustaches are splendid. A sale will inevitably follow, so please select whatever items you wish to reserve, or send a list later.

There are two further points before I conclude. You know, or if you do not, your late father surely did, that you are entitled to my money and goods upon my death. Do not mistake this for a premonition of my demise; I mention it merely to clarify the arrangement. The Lennoxes appear very fond of you now, and perhaps they will continue to be; perhaps not. Therefore, it is prudent to establish a formal agreement: you shall pay them two hundred and fifty pounds annually, as long as you and they find it agreeable to live together. (Note: This figure naturally includes Dixon; ensure you are not cajoled into paying more for her services.) Thus, you will not be cast adrift should the Captain eventually desire his house exclusively, allowing you to depart with your person and your two hundred and fifty pounds elsewhere—or, indeed, if I have not already claimed you to serve as my housekeeper. Regarding attire, Dixon, and personal expenses, including confectionery (a necessity for all young ladies until wisdom arrives with age), I intend to consult a lady acquaintance regarding your allowance from your father before finalizing this.

Now, Margaret, have you already flown into a rage upon reading thus far, wondering what right the old man possesses to settle your affairs so cavalierly? I make no doubt you have. Yet, the old man has a right. He has loved your father for thirty-five years; he stood beside him on his wedding day; he closed his eyes in death. Moreover, he is your godfather. Since he cannot offer you much spiritual guidance, possessing a hidden awareness of your superiority in such matters, he seeks instead to provide you with material aid. Furthermore, he has no known relatives on earth—"Who is there to mourn for Adam Bell?"—and his entire heart is devoted to this singular endeavor. Margaret Hale is not the type of girl to deny him. Write back within the day, even if only with two lines, to inform me of your decision. But *no thanks*."

Margaret picked up a pen and scrawled with a trembling hand: *"Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay."*

In her frail state, she could conceive of no other response, yet she felt vexed by using these words. However, she was so utterly fatigued by this slight exertion that, had she been capable of devising another form of acceptance, she could not have remained seated long enough to write a single syllable of it. She was compelled to lie down again and attempt to cease thinking.

"My dearest child! Has that letter vexed or troubled you?"

"No!" Margaret replied feebly. "I shall be better when tomorrow is over."

"I feel sure, darling, you will not improve until I remove you from this horrid air. I cannot imagine how you have endured it for these two years."

"Where could I possibly go? I could not leave Papa and Mama."

"Well, do not distress yourself, my dear. I daresay it was all for the best, though I had no conception of your living conditions. Our butler's wife resides in a superior house to this."

"It is sometimes very charming—in summer; one cannot judge by its appearance now. I have been very happy here," Margaret said, closing her eyes to halt the conversation.

The house, teeming with comforts now compared to its earlier state, held a different atmosphere. The evenings were chilly, and fires burned brightly in every bedroom under Mrs. Shaw's orders. She doted on Margaret in every conceivable way, purchasing every delicacy and soft luxury in which she herself would have sought refuge. Yet, Margaret remained indifferent to these provisions, or if they captured her attention, it was solely as grounds for gratitude toward her aunt, who was making such sacrifices for her sake.

She was restless despite her weakness. Throughout the day, she avoided contemplating the ongoing ceremonies at Oxford by wandering from room to room, lethargically setting aside articles she wished to retain. Dixon followed her, acting on Mrs. Shaw's instruction to receive directives, though privately instructed to soothe Margaret into repose as soon as possible.

"These books, Dixon, I shall keep. All the rest you will send to Mr. Bell? They are of a kind he will value for themselves, as well as for Papa's sake. This... I should like you to take this to Mr. Thornton after I am gone. Wait; I will write a note with it."

She sat down hastily, as if afraid of giving herself time to think, and penned:

Resuming her tour of the house, she turned over objects familiar from her childhood, touching them with a caressing reluctance to part. Yet, she spoke hardly at all. Dixon reported to Mrs. Shaw that she suspected Miss Hale heard not a word spoken, though the maid talked continuously in an effort to distract her intent.

The result of keeping her upright all day was excessive physical exhaustion by evening, granting her a better night's rest than she had enjoyed since hearing of Mr. Hale's death.

At breakfast the following morning, she expressed a desire to bid farewell to one or two friends. Mrs. Shaw demurred: "I am sure, my dear, you have no friends here sufficiently intimate to warrant calling upon them so soon, before attending church."

"But today is my only opportunity; if Captain Lennox comes this afternoon, and if we must—if I truly must go tomorrow..."

"Oh, yes, we shall go tomorrow. I am increasingly convinced this air is detrimental to you, causing you to look so pale and ill. Besides, Edith expects us; she may be waiting for me. And you cannot be left alone, my dear, at your age. No; if you insist on making these calls, I will accompany you. Dixon can arrange a coach, I presume?"

Thus, Mrs. Shaw took charge of Margaret, bringing her maid along to manage the shawls and air-cushions. Margaret's face was too sorrowful to break into a smile amidst this preparation for two visits she had frequently made alone at all hours. She was half-afraid to admit that one of the destinations was Nicholas Higgins' cottage. All she could do was hope her aunt would be too indisposed to alight from the carriage and walk up the court, risking having her face slapped by wet laundry hanging on ropes strung from house to house.

A small internal conflict arose in Mrs. Shaw's mind between ease and a sense of matronly propriety, but the former prevailed. With many admonitions for Margaret to be careful and avoid catching a fever—a constant threat in such locales—her aunt permitted her to visit a place she had frequented before without precautions or permission.

Nicholas was out; only Mary and a couple of the Boucher children were at home. Margaret chided herself for not timing her visit better. Mary possessed a blunt intellect, though her feelings were warm and kind. The moment she grasped Margaret's purpose, she began to cry and sob with such unrestrained emotion that Margaret found it futile to utter any of the thousand comforting thoughts that had occurred to her during the ride. She could only vaguely suggest the possibility of

"A dull rotation, never at a stay,
Yesterday's face twin image of to-day." — Cowper.

"Of what each one should be, he sees the form and rule,
And till he reach to that, his joy can ne'er be full." — Rückert.

For Margaret, the profound stillness of the Harley Street residence during Edith's convalescence proved a necessary balm. It afforded her the rare luxury of time to process the seismic shift in her fortunes over the preceding two months. Suddenly, she was an inhabitant of a sumptuous mansion where the mere awareness of worry or hardship seemed alien to the atmosphere. The intricate gears of domestic life here ran with a frictionless, oily smoothness. Mrs. Shaw and Edith treated Margaret with an abundance of affection, insisting on calling it "home," yet a faint pang of ingratitude occasionally stirred within her. Could she truly prefer this opulent sanctuary to the Helstone rectory—or even the modest cottage at Milton, surrounded by her anxious father, her frail mother, and the humble, tangible cares of a poorer existence? That was the definition of home for her soul.

Edith, eager to reclaim her health, rushed to furnish Margaret's former bedroom with the soft linens and decorative trinkets that adorned her own rooms. Mrs. Shaw and her maid delighted in restoring Margaret's wardrobe to a state of fashionable elegance. Captain Lennox was a model of gentle amiability; he spent hours in Edith's dressing room, played with his young son, and otherwise idled at his club or attended dinners.
