Just as Margaret began to crave the quietude she had previously endured, Edith descended the stairs, resuming her role as the household's center of gravity. Margaret reverted to her old instinct of service, eagerly assuming the burdens of correspondence, scheduling, and nursing whenever Edith's mood turned somber. Consequently, she often feigned illness simply to justify her caretaking role. Yet, the rest of the family was immersed in the frenetic energy of the London Season, leaving Margaret frequently solitary. In these moments, her thoughts drifted back to Milton, struck by a poignant contrast between the vibrant struggle of her past and the stagnant ease of her present. She feared she was becoming surfeited by a life requiring no effort, worried that she might slowly drift into a numb oblivion, unaware of anything beyond the velvet cushion of luxury surrounding her. Though toils existed in London, she rarely glimpsed them; the servants inhabited a shadowy underworld of their own, appearing only to serve the whims of their masters, invisible in their hopes and fears. A hollow void began to open in Margaret's heart.

One evening, she tentatively voiced this unease to Edith. Exhausted from a night of dancing, Edith languidly stroked Margaret's cheek as the younger woman sat on a footstool beside her on the sofa.

"Poor child!" Edith sighed. "It is somewhat melancholy to be left alone night after night when the whole world is so festive. But soon we shall host dinner parties—once Henry returns from circuit—and there will be delightful variety. No wonder you are moping, my darling!"

Margaret sensed that dinner parties were no cure-all. Edith, however, prided herself on them, boasting they were vastly superior to the stiff, dowager-led gatherings of her mother's era. Even Mrs. Shaw seemed to derive genuine pleasure from these modern social circles, tailored to the tastes of Captain and Mrs. Lennox, mirroring her own enjoyment of the formal events she once hosted.

Captain Lennox remained consistently kind and brotherly toward Margaret. She genuinely loved him, save for those moments when he became overly preoccupied with Edith's attire, striving to ensure his sister-in-law's beauty made a lasting impression on society. At such times, the dormant pride of a Vashti rose within Margaret, threatening to spill forth.

Her days followed a predictable rhythm: a couple of quiet hours before a belated breakfast; a leisurely, half-asleep meal followed by discussions of plans that concerned her only insofar as she was expected to offer sympathetic agreement; an endless stream of notes to compose, which Edith delegated to her with flattering remarks on her "eloquence"; playful interactions with Sholto upon his return from walks; childcare duties during the servants' meals; drives or receiving callers; and finally, engagements for her aunt and cousins. These activities, while freeing her technically, left her exhausted by the day's inertia, her spirits depressed, and her delicate health taxed.

With longing, Margaret awaited Dixon's return from Milton, where he had been diligently settling the Hale family's affairs. Her heart felt a sudden famine at the cessation of news regarding the people she had known so intimately. True, Dixon's businesslike letters occasionally cited Mr. Thornton's opinions on furniture or landlords, but references to Milton itself were sparse. One evening, sitting alone in the Lennox drawing-room, holding Dixon's letters unread, Margaret reflected on the bustling life from which she had been plucked. She wondered if anyone in that whirlwind missed her—not Higgins, whom she wasn't considering—but questioned silently whether the crowd cared if she and her father were gone.

Suddenly, Mr. Bell was announced. Margaret hastily shoved the letters into her workbasket, rising with a blush that suggested guilt.

"Oh, Mr. Bell! I never thought of seeing you!"

"But I trust you extend a welcome as warm as that startled surprise," he replied cheerfully.

"Have you dined? How did you arrive? Shall I order dinner?"

"If you're planning to eat. Otherwise, nothing pleases me less than dining. But where are the others? Out? Leaving you alone?"

"Oh, yes! It is quite refreshing. I was just contemplating—"

"Shall I risk dinner? I'm unsure if there's anything suitable in the house."

"To tell the truth, I dined at my club. Their cooking hasn't improved, so I thought if you were dining, I might attempt to manage. But forget it! There aren't ten trustworthy cooks in England for impromptu meals. If their skills hold, their tempers surely won't. You shall make me some tea, Margaret. Now, what were you thinking of? You were about to tell me. Whose letters were those you concealed so quickly, god-daughter?"

"Only Dixon's," Margaret admitted, turning crimson.

"Phew! Is that all? Who do you suppose arrived on the train with me?"

"I don't know," Margaret lied, refusing to guess.

"Your... cousin-in-law's brother? What is the precise term?"

"Mr. Henry Lennox?" Margaret ventured.

"Yes," Mr. Bell confirmed. "You knew him formerly, did you not? Describe him, Margaret."

"I liked him long ago," she said, glancing downward briefly before lifting her eyes to her natural composure. "We have corresponded regarding Frederick, but I haven't seen him in nearly three years; he may have changed. What is your impression of him?"

"I am uncertain. He was so intent on discovering who I was initially, and what I was subsequently, that he revealed nothing of himself. Unless, of course, that veiled curiosity regarding the nature of his conversational partner was not a virtue, hinting at his character. Do you consider him handsome, Margaret?"

"No, certainly not. Do you?"

"Not I. Yet, I presumed you might. Does he frequent this area much?"

"He seems to be in town when he is. He has been on circuit since my arrival. However, Mr. Bell, have you come from Oxford or Milton?"

"Milton. Don't you see I am smoke-stained?"

"Certainly. Though I assumed it might be the result of Oxford's antiquities."

"Come now, be sensible! In Oxford, I could have managed every landlord and dictated terms with half the trouble your Milton landlord has inflicted upon me, only to be thwarted in the end. He refuses to release the house until next June. Fortunately, Mr. Thornton secured a tenant. Why do you not inquire after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He has proven a diligent friend. He has alleviated more than half my burden."

"And how fares he? How is Mrs. Thornton?" Margaret asked urgently, lowering her voice despite her efforts to sound casual.

"They are presumably well. I stayed at their home until I was expelled by the incessant chatter concerning that Thornton girl's marriage. It was too much for Thornton himself, though she was his sister. He retreated to his room constantly. He is past the age for such concerns, whether as protagonist or accessory. I was astonished to find the older lady swept up in the current, carried away by her daughter's fervor for orange blossoms and lace. I had imagined Mrs. Thornton possessed sterner material."

"She would assume any emotion to conceal her daughter's fragility," Margaret whispered.

"Perhaps so. Have you studied her closely? She does not appear particularly fond of you, Margaret."

"I know it," Margaret replied flatly.

"Oh, here is tea, at last!" she exclaimed, as if relieved.

The arrival of tea coincided with Mr. Henry Lennox, who had walked up from a late dinner, expecting to find his brother and sister-in-law at home. Margaret suspected he was as grateful as she was for the presence of a third party, easing the tension of their first encounter since his proposal at Helstone and her subsequent refusal. She struggled to find words, thankful for the distractions of the tea table that allowed her silence and gave him space to regain his composure. Indeed, he had likely forced himself to Harley Street to navigate an awkward situation, doubly difficult now that she was the sole lady

"Where are the sounds that swam along / The buoyant air when I was young; / The last vibration now is o'er, / And they who listened are no more; / Ah! let me close my eyes and dream." —W. S. Landor.

The vision of Helstone had taken root in Mr. Bell's waking consciousness during his talk with Mr. Lennox, and throughout the long hours of the night, it ran riot through his dreams. Once again, he found himself a tutor in the college where he currently held the rank of Fellow; once again, it was a long vacation, and he was staying with his newly married friend—the proud husband and happy Vicar of Helstone. Together, they leaped across babbling brooks, impossible jumps that seemed to suspend them whole days in mid-air. Time and space ceased to exist, though everything else felt undeniably real. Every event was measured not by its physical duration, but by the intensity of emotion, for in this realm, nothing possessed true existence. Yet, the trees blazed with gorgeous autumnal foliage; the warm scents of flowers and herbs drifted sweetly upon the senses; and the young wife moved about the house with that specific blend of irritation regarding her financial station, mixed with deep pride in her handsome and devoted husband—a dynamic Mr. Bell had observed in real life nearly twenty-five years prior.

The dream was so vividly lifelike that upon awakening, his current reality felt like a phantom. Where was he? In a cramped, handsomely furnished room of a London hotel! Where were the voices that had spoken to him, the movements around him, the touches upon his skin, mere moments ago? Gone! Buried! Lost forever, as far as "earth's forevermore" could stretch. He was an old man, so recently exulting in the full vigor of manhood. The utter loneliness of his life became insupportable to contemplate.

He rose hastily, attempting to banish these thoughts by rushing into a hurried dressing for breakfast in Harley Street. He could not fully attend to the lawyer's intricate details. As he watched, Margaret's eyes widened and her lips grew pale; one by one, fate seemed to decree, or so it appeared, that every scrap of evidence capable of exonerating Frederick fell from beneath her grasp and vanished. Even Mr. Lennox's usually regulated professional voice softened into a tender murmur as he approached the extinction of their last hope.

It was not that Margaret had been unaware of the outcome beforehand. Rather, the relentless minutiae of each successive disappointment poured in to extinguish all remaining hope until she finally broke down in tears. Mr. Lennox paused his reading.

"I had better not go on," he said, his voice filled with concern. "It was a foolish proposal of mine. Lieutenant Hale..." Even granting him the title of the service from which he had so harshly been expelled proved soothing to Margaret. "Lieutenant Hale is happy now; more secure in fortune and future prospects than he could ever have been in the navy; and has, doubtless, adopted his wife's country as his own."

"That is it," Margaret replied. "It seems so selfish in me to regret it," she managed a faint smile, "and yet he is lost to me, and I am so lonely."

Mr. Lennox turned over his papers, silently wishing he were as wealthy and prosperous as he believed he someday would be. Mr. Bell blew his nose but remained silent, as did Margaret, who within a minute or two appeared to recover her usual composure. She thanked Mr. Lennox very courteously for his trouble—courteousness heightened precisely because she was acutely conscious that her behavior might lead him to believe he had caused her needless pain. Yet, it was pain she would not have been without.

Mr. Bell stepped forward to bid her farewell.

"Margaret!" he exclaimed, fumbling with his gloves. "I am going down to Helstone tomorrow to inspect the old place. Would you like to come with me? Or would it give you too much pain? Speak out, do not be afraid."

"Oh, Mr. Bell," she began, but could find no further words. Instead, she took his old, gout-afflicted hand and kissed it.

"Come, come; that's enough," he said, flushing with awkwardness. "I suppose your Aunt Shaw will trust you with me. We'll start tomorrow morning and arrive there about two o'clock, I fancy. We'll take a snack and order dinner at the little inn—the Lennard Arms, it used to be—and go for a walk to whet our appetites in the forest. Can you manage it, Margaret? I know it will be a trial for both of us, but it will be a pleasure to me, at least. There we'll dine—we may get doe-venison, if we're lucky—and then I'll take my nap while you go out to visit old friends. I'll bring you back safe and sound, barring any railway accidents, and I'll insure your life for a thousand pounds before we start, which might offer some comfort to your relations; but otherwise, I'll return you to Mrs. Shaw by lunchtime on Friday. So, if you agree, I'll just go upstairs and propose it formally."

"It's no use my trying to tell you how much I shall like it," Margaret whispered through her tears.

"Well, then, prove your gratitude by keeping those fountains of yours dry for the next two days," Mr. Bell insisted, trying to lighten the mood. "If you don't, I shall feel quite queasy about my own lachrymal ducts, and I dislike that."

"I won't cry a drop," Margaret promised, winking her eyes vigorously to shake the moisture from her lashes and forcing a smile.

"There's my good girl. Then we'll go upstairs and settle the arrangements."

Margaret was in a state of almost trembling eagerness as Mr. Bell discussed his plan with her Aunt Shaw. The aunt was initially startled, then doubtful and perplexed, but ultimately yielded to the rough force of Mr. Bell's persuasion rather than her own convictions. To the very end, she could not decide whether it was right or wrong, proper or improper, until Margaret's safe return and the happy fulfillment of the project gave her sufficient peace of mind to declare, "She was sure it had been a very kind thought of Mr. Bell's, and exactly what she herself had been wishing for Margaret, providing her with the very change she required after all the anxious times she had endured."

"So on those happy days of yore,
Oft as I dare to dwell once more,
Still must I miss the friends so tried,
Whom Death has severed from my side.

But ever when true friendship binds,
Spirit it is that spirit finds;
In spirit then our bliss we found,
In spirit yet to them I'm bound." —UHLAND.

Margaret was ready long before the appointed hour, granting herself a brief, quiet moment to shed a tear unseen, only to compose a radiant smile whenever anyone glanced her way. Her final anxiety was merely the fear of missing the train; yet, relief washed over her instantly. Seated opposite Mr. Bell in the carriage, she watched the landscape blur past. The familiar stations of the south country slid by, bathed in the warm, golden light of a pure sun that reddened the tiled roofs, a stark contrast to the cold slate of the north. Pigeons circled the quaint, peaked gables, settling softly to preen their shimmering feathers in the heat.

The stations themselves were surprisingly empty, devoid of the bustling crowds Margaret recalled from her previous journeys on the London and North-Western line. This route, later in the year, would likely swarm with pleasure-seekers, but currently, it remained the domain of steady industry. Spectators lounged at platforms, hands in pockets, seemingly lost in the simple act of watching the trains depart, leaving behind only tracks, sheds, and distant fields. The hot air danced over the golden stillness of the land. Farm after farm rolled by, each evoking memories of German idylls, of *Hermann and Dorothea*, of *Evangeline*.

From this waking reverie, she was abruptly roused. It was time to alight and take the fly to Helstone. Suddenly, sharp emotions pierced her heart, a mixture of pain and pleasure she could barely define. Every mile was thick with associations she would trade the world for, yet each triggered a profound grief for "the days that are no more." The last time she traversed this road, she had departed with her parents; the season had been gloomy, her spirits crushed, but they had been beside her. Now, she was alone—an orphan. They had vanished from the earth, leaving her solitary witness to the scene. It wounded her deeply to see the Helstone road flooded in sunlight, every turn and familiar tree identical in its summer glory to years past. Nature felt no loss; it remained eternally young.

Mr. Bell understood the turmoil within her and wisely maintained a respectful silence. They arrived at the Lennard Arms, a structure half-farmhouse, half-inn, standing slightly apart from the road as if to declare that the host did not actively court travelers. Facing the village green stood an ancient lime-tree, its branches forming a bench-like canopy beneath which hung the faded Lennard escutcheon. The inn's door stood wide, yet there was no hurried hospitality.

When the landlady finally appeared, she offered a warm, almost familial welcome, apologizing profusely for her delay due to the demands of hay-making and the preparation of provisions for the fields. She had been too busy packing baskets to notice the wheels until it was too late.

"Why, bless me!" she exclaimed, her eyes catching the glint of sunshine on Margaret's face, previously obscured in the shade. "It's Miss Hale, Jenny!" she cried, rushing to the door to summon her daughter. Then, approaching Margaret, she shook her hand with maternal affection.

"And how are you all? How's the Vicar and Miss Dixon? The Vicar above all! God bless him! We've never ceased to mourn his departure."

Margaret attempted to speak of her father's death; regarding her mother, Mrs. Purkis clearly assumed she was deceased given the omission of her name. Choking on her words, Margaret could only gesture to her deep mourning and whisper, "Papa."

"Surely, sir, it's never so!" Mrs. Purkis turned to Mr. Bell, confirming the dark suspicion in her mind. "A gentleman visited us in the spring—or perhaps last winter—who spoke much of Mr. Hale and Miss Margaret. He mentioned Mrs. Hale had passed, poor soul, but never a word of the Vicar being ill!"

"It is so, however," Mr. Bell replied gently. "He died quite suddenly while visiting me in Oxford. He was a good man, Mrs. Purkis, and many of us would be grateful for such a calm ending. Come, Margaret, my dear! Your father was my oldest friend, and she is my goddaughter, so I thought we should journey down together to see the old place. I know you can provide comfortable rooms and a fine dinner. You don't recall me, I see, but my name is Bell. On occasion, when the parsonage was full, I've slept here and enjoyed your excellent ale."

"To be sure! I ask your pardon; I was so preoccupied with Miss Hale." Mrs. Purkis led Margaret to a room, suggesting she remove her bonnet and wash her face. She noted that earlier that morning she had submerged fresh roses in a water-jug, hoping someone might arrive to enjoy the scent. "To think the Vicar is dead! Well, we must all die; that gentleman said he was recovering from the sorrow of Mrs. Hale's passing."

"Mrs. Purkis, please attend to Miss Hale first. I wish to consult with you regarding dinner."

Later, in Margaret's bedroom, the casement window was nearly obscured by climbing roses and vines. Pushing them aside, she caught a glimpse of the parsonage chimneys rising above the trees, recognizable even through the foliage.

"Aye!" Mrs. Purkis said, straightening the bed and sending Jenny for lavender-scented towels. "Times have changed, miss. Our new Vicar has seven children and is building a nursery where the old arbour and tool-house once stood. He has installed new grates and a plate-glass window in the drawing-room. He and his wife are energetic folk, doing much 'good,' though I suspect they are merely turning things upside down for little reason. The new Vicar is a teetotaler and a magistrate; his wife knows recipes for economical cooking and makes bread without yeast. They talk so incessantly, both at once, that one is knocked down before one can form a counter-argument. He'll be nosing into the hay-fields for the men's cans, causing a fuss because it isn't ginger-beer. My mother and grandmother before me sent good malt liquor to haymakers and administered salts and senna for ailments. I must continue in their ways, though Mrs. Hepworth wants to replace my medicines with comfits, claiming they are pleasanter—I simply lack faith in them. I must go now, miss, though I have much to hear; I'll return shortly."

Mr. Bell prepared a rustic lunch for Margaret upon her descent: strawberries and cream, brown bread, milk, Stilton cheese, and a bottle of port for himself. After this simple meal, they set out for a walk, unsure which path to take amidst so many familiar attractions.

"Shall we pass the vicarage?" Mr. Bell asked.

"No, not yet," Margaret replied. "We will circle this way and return via it."

Old trees felled the previous autumn lay bare; a squatter's dilapidated cottage had vanished. Margaret mourned each loss as if bidding farewell to an old friend. They passed the site where she and Mr. Lennox had once sketched. The white, lightning-struck trunk of the venerable beech, beneath whose roots they had sat, was gone. The elderly inhabitant of the ruined cottage was dead, replaced by a tidy, respectable new dwelling. A small garden occupied the space where the beech had stood.

"I did not realize I had grown so old," Margaret murmured after a pause, turning away with a sigh.

"Yes!" Mr. Bell agreed. "It is the initial changes among familiar things that create such a mystery of time for the young. Later, we lose the sense of the miraculous. I accept change as inevitable. The instability of human affairs is familiar to me, but to you, it feels new and oppressive."

"Let us go see little Susan," Margaret suggested, guiding her companion up a grassy lane shaded by a forest glade.

"With all my heart, though I have no idea who little Susan is. But I hold a kindness for all Susans, for simple Susan's sake."

"My little Susan was disappointed when I left without saying goodbye; it weighs on my conscience that I caused her pain which a little more effort might have avoided. But it is a distance. Are you sure you won't tire?"

"Quite sure, provided you don't walk too fast. Here, there are no scenic views to justify pausing for breath. One might think it romantic to walk with someone 'fat and scant o' breath' if I were Hamlet, Prince of Denmark. Have compassion on my infirmities for his sake."

"I shall walk slower for your own sake. I like you twenty times better than Hamlet."

"On the principle that a living ass is preferable to a dead lion?"

"Perhaps so. I do not analyze my feelings."

"I am content to accept your liking for me without scrutinizing its origins. Only let us not walk at a snail's pace."

"Very well. Lead the way, and I will follow. Or, if I move too quickly, we may stop and meditate, like the Hamlet you compare yourself to."

"Thank you. But since my mother did not

"Experience, like a pale musician, holds / A dulcimer of patience in his hand; / Whence harmonies we cannot understand, / Of God's will in His worlds, the strain unfolds / In sad, perplexed minors." — *Mrs. Browning*

Just as these lines found their echo in the quiet corners of the Hale household, Dixon returned from Milton, bringing with her the fresh air of the town and assuming her station once more as Margaret's maid. Her arrival was accompanied by an endless stream of local gossip, delivered with the enthusiasm of one who believes the world revolves around the latest tidings. She recounted how Martha had departed to reside with Miss Thornton following the latter's marriage, detailing the bridesmaids, the gowns, and the elaborate breakfasts of that memorable ceremony. There were whispers that Mr. Thornton had thrown a wedding far too grand for his circumstances, especially considering the financial blow dealt by the recent strike and the heavy costs incurred from failed contracts.

Dixon also shared her dismay over the auction of household goods. She marveled at the paltry sums fetched for items she had long cherished, deeming it a shame that such treasures yielded so little, particularly given the apparent wealth of Milton's inhabitants. Yet, she noted with amusement how Mrs. Thornton had managed to secure a few good bargains before Mr. Thornton arrived the very next day. Driven by a singular desire to acquire just one or two specific pieces, he had ended up bidding against himself, providing ample entertainment for the onlookers. As Dixon observed, this maneuver served to "make things even"; if Mrs. Thornton had paid too little previously, Mr. Thornton ensured he paid too much now.

The narrative of Mr. Bell was equally complex. He had issued various orders regarding the books, displaying a fastidiousness that left Dixon utterly bewildered. "There is no understanding him," she remarked, noting that if he had visited personally, matters might have been clearer, but letters remained perpetually more puzzling than worthwhile. When pressed for details concerning the Higginses, Dixon's memory betrayed her. Possessed of an aristocratic bias, her recollections faltered whenever she attempted to recall anything pertaining to those beneath her social standing. She asserted that Nicholas was doing well, citing his repeated visits to inquire after Miss Margaret—as had Mr. Thornton, though rarely. As for Mary, she dismissed her as a "great, stout, slatternly thing." However, a rumor persisted, perhaps merely a dream born of her imagination, that Mary had secured employment at Mr. Thornton's mill under the instruction of her father to learn the art of cooking. The logic behind such a command escaped Dixon, and Margaret, finding the tale incoherent, agreed it sounded suspiciously like a dream. Nevertheless, it was a relief to have someone with whom she could discuss Milton and its people.

While Dixon preferred to dwell on the flattering implications of Mr. Bell's speeches—which hinted at a design to make Margaret his heiress—Margaret offered no encouragement. She deflected his insinuating inquiries, regardless of how they were disguised as suspicion or assertion. Throughout this period, Margaret harbored a strange, undefined yearning to hear that Mr. Bell had traveled to Milton for business. It was a mutual understanding established during their conversation at Helstone that the explanation she craved should be delivered orally to Mr. Thornton, and even then, it should never be forced upon him. Though Mr. Bell corresponded occasionally, his letters lacked substance. Margaret received them with a lingering sense of disappointment, for he showed no sign of heading to Milton. Patience, she decided, was the only recourse; sooner or later, the mist would clear.

His correspondence had changed, becoming shorter, more complaining, and tinged with an unusual bitterness. He seemed less focused on the future and more weighed down by regrets of the past and weariness of the present. Margaret feared he was ill, prompting her to ask about his health. His response was a brief note mentioning an "old-fashioned complaint called the spleen," leaving it to her to determine whether it was mental or physical, while graciously declining to issue regular bulletins on his condition. Consequently, Margaret ceased her inquiries.

One day, Edith inadvertently revealed fragments of a conversation with Mr. Bell from his last stay in London. These words sparked a vivid hope in Margaret: that he intended to take her to visit her brother and new sister-in-law in Cadiz during the autumn. She interrogated Edith relentlessly until the girl grew weary and confessed that Mr. Bell had only half-thought of the trip. He viewed it as an opportunity to hear Frederick's firsthand account of the mutiny and for Margaret to meet her new relative. After all, he traveled annually during the long vacation; why not Spain?

Edith, hoping Margaret did not wish to depart, expressed anxiety, finally breaking down in tears to declare that she cared for Margaret far more than the reverse. Margaret comforted her gently, though she struggled to convey how this potential journey to Spain—a mere *Château en Espagne*—charmed her spirit. Edith, however, interpreted any joy experienced away from her as a tacit insult or proof of indifference. Thus, Margaret was forced to suppress her delight, venting it instead through Dixon during dressing preparations.

"She's a Papist, Miss, isn't she?" Dixon asked.
"I believe—oh, yes, certainly!" Margaret replied, momentarily dampened by the thought.
"And they live in a Popish country?"
"Yes."
"Then I'm afraid I must say that my soul is dearer to me than even Master Frederick, his own dear self. I should be in a perpetual terror, Miss, lest I should be converted."
"Oh," said Margaret, "I do not know that I am going. And if I do, I am not such a fine lady as to be unable to travel without you. No! Dear old Dixon, you shall have a long holiday if we go. But I'm afraid it is a long 'if.'"

Dixon bristled at this sentiment. First, she disliked the affectation of "Dear old Dixon," a term of endearment Margaret reserved for those she favored. To Dixon, nearing fifty and in the prime of her life, the label felt diminutive. Second, she resented being so readily convinced; despite her professed terror, a latent curiosity about Spain, the Inquisition, and Catholic mysteries lingered within her. Clearing her throat to dispel the tension, she queried whether avoiding priests and church entrances would suffice to prevent conversion.

"Master Frederick, to be sure, went over unaccountably," Margaret sighed.
"I fancy it was love that first predisposed him to conversion," Margaret continued.
"Indeed, Miss!" Dixon exclaimed. "Well! I can preserve myself from priests and churches; but love steals in unawares! I think it's as well I should not go."

Margaret feared indulging too deeply in this Spanish fantasy, as it distracted her from her impatience regarding the need for clarity with Mr. Thornton. For the moment, Mr. Bell remained stationary at Oxford, showing no immediate intent to visit Milton. A secret restraint held Margaret back from even alluding to the possibility of his departure or the fleeting mention of Spain Edith had heard. Had Mr. Bell truly considered it at Helstone? Likely just a passing fancy—but if true, it represented a radiant escape from the monotonous stagnation of her current existence.

A distinct pleasure in Margaret's life at this juncture was Edith's son. He was the pride and plaything of both parents when compliant, but his strong will often erupted into stormy passions. In such moments, Edith would collapse in despair, sighing, "Oh dear, what shall I do with him! Do, Margaret, please ring the bell for Hanley." Paradoxically, Margaret found the child more engaging in these displays of character than in his placid, blue-sashed moods. She would whisk him away to a private room where the two of them engaged in a silent battle. With a firm power that subdued him into peace, she countered every charm and wile he employed, guiding him toward righteousness until he would bury his hot, tear-streaked face against hers, kissing and caressing until sleep claimed him in her arms. These were Margaret's sweetest moments, offering a foretaste of affection she feared would forever elude her.

Mr. Henry Lennox introduced a new, albeit cool, element to the household dynamic. Margaret found him more brilliant but colder than before, yet his intellect and vast knowledge seasoned the otherwise insipid conversations. She detected in him a subtle contempt for his brother and sister-in-law, viewing their lifestyle as frivolous and aimless. On occasion, he addressed his brother sharply in Margaret's presence, questioning if he truly intended to abandon his profession. When Captain Lennox replied that his income sufficed, Mr. Lennox curled his lip, remarking, "And is that all you live for?" Despite this friction, the brothers remained attached, bound by the dynamic of the clever leading the patient follower.

Mr. Lennox advanced steadily in his career, calculatingly cultivating connections for future utility. Keen-sighted, far-seeing, intelligent, sarcastic, and proud, he maintained a certain distance from Margaret. Following their initial deep conversation regarding Frederick's affairs, their interactions had diminished to the necessities of shared domestic life. This proximity, however, had worn down her initial shyness and his signs of mortified pride. They met frequently, yet she sensed he actively avoided solitude with her. Both seemed aware they had drifted apart from their former alignment of opinions and tastes. Still, when he spoke with exceptional brilliance or epigrammatic force, his eyes would seek the reflection of her countenance for a

"My own, my father's friend! I cannot part with thee! I ne'er have shown, thou ne'er hast known, How dear thou art to me." — *Anon.*

The atmosphere of the dinner parties hosted by Mrs. Lennox was defined by a specific alchemy: her guests supplied the beauty, Captain Lennox provided the effortless mastery of current topics, and Mr. Henry Lennox, accompanied by a select group of young men regarded as his intimates, contributed wit, sharp intellect, and a breadth of knowledge wielded with such finesse that it appeared neither pedantic nor burdensome. These gatherings were undeniably delightful. Yet, even within this glittering circle, Margaret's dissatisfaction inevitably surfaced. Every talent, every sentiment, every acquired skill—and indeed, every inclination toward virtue—was consumed as fuel for fireworks; the sacred, hidden flame burned itself out in mere sparkle and crackle. They discussed art through a purely sensual lens, fixated on external effects rather than seeking its deeper lessons. They fanned themselves into an artificial fervor regarding lofty subjects in the company of others, only to abandon those thoughts entirely once they were alone. Their capacity for genuine appreciation was squandered in a torrent of socially appropriate words.

One evening, after the gentlemen had retired to the drawing-room, Mr. Lennox approached Margaret. It was nearly the first voluntary address he had directed toward her since her return to Harley Street.

"You did not appear pleased by what Mr. Shirley said at dinner," he observed.

"Did I not?" Margaret replied smoothly. "My countenance must be remarkably expressive. It seems to have retained its eloquence."

"I did not approve," Margaret stated quickly, "of his manner of championing what he knew to be erroneous—so blatantly wrong, even in jest."

"But it was exceedingly clever. How precisely each word landed! Do you recall the felicitous epithets?"

"Yes."

"And yet, you would likely disdain them if you considered them seriously. Please, do not hesitate to express that, despite his being my friend."

"There!" she exclaimed, halting abruptly. She paused, expecting him to complete the thought she had started, but he remained silent, watching to see if she would elaborate. Instead, she simply flushed crimson and turned away. Before she could withdraw completely, she caught the soft, clear murmur of his voice: "If my tone, or my mode of thinking, is what you dislike, will you grant me the courtesy of telling me so directly? That would give me the opportunity to learn how to please you."

For several weeks thereafter, there was no news of Mr. Bell's departure for Milton. He had mentioned the possibility during his stay at Helstone, suggesting it might happen soon, yet he must have handled any necessary arrangements via correspondence. Margaret reasoned that, if able, he would avoid traveling to a place he disliked and which he would struggle to comprehend, especially given the intimate nature of the explanation she felt required personal delivery. She knew he understood the necessity of the meeting, yet the timing mattered less to her than the fact that it had not occurred. It was now August, and there had been no mention of the Spanish journey he had hinted at for Edith. Margaret struggled to reconcile herself to the fading of this hope.

Then, one morning, a letter arrived. It announced that Mr. Bell intended to come to town the following week to discuss a plan forming in his mind. Furthermore, he planned to indulge in some medical treatment, having begun to accept her view that his irritability stemmed from physical frailty rather than character flaws. Reading between the lines, Margaret noted a forced cheerfulness in his tone—a detail she only recognized in retrospect. At the time, her attention was captured by Edith's exclamations.

"He's coming to town! Oh dear! And I am so utterly drained by the heat that I doubt I possess the strength for another dinner. Besides, everyone has departed except our dear, dull selves, who cannot decide where to go next. There will be no one to meet him."

"I am certain he would prefer dining with us in private company rather than among the most agreeable strangers you could possibly assemble. Moreover, if he is unwell, he will not seek invitations. I am glad he has finally admitted it. I was convinced he was ill from the entire tenor of his letters, yet he refused to answer my inquiries, leaving me with no confidant to update me."

"Oh! He is not severely ill, or he would not entertain thoughts of Spain."

"He never mentions Spain."

"No! But the scheme he intends to propose clearly relates to that region. However, would you truly venture out in weather like this?"

"Oh, it will cool down daily. Yes! Consider it. I am only afraid that I have indulged in such absorbing, wilful wishing—a habit destined either for disappointment or for satisfaction that feels hollow in spirit. It is akin to the prayer, 'Give me children, or else I die.' I fear my cry is, 'Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.'"

"My dearest Margaret! You will surely be persuaded to stay put; and then what shall I do? Oh! I wish I could find someone suitable for you to marry here, ensuring your presence remains secure!"

"I shall never marry."

"Nonsense! Double nonsense! As Sholto remarks, you are such an attraction to the household that he knows countless men who would gladly visit next year solely for your sake."

Margaret straightened her posture with haughty dignity. "Do you know, Edith, I sometimes suspect your life in Corfu has imparted..."

"Well?"

"Just a shade or two of coarseness."

Edith immediately burst into bitter sobs, declaring with vehemence that Margaret had lost all affection for her and ceased to regard her as a friend. Overwhelmed by the sting of wounded pride, Margaret realized she had voiced an opinion too harsh for the relief she sought, and by the end of the day, she had become Edith's devoted slave. Meanwhile, the little lady, overwhelmed by her hurt feelings, lay like a fallen victim upon the sofa, heaving deep sighs until sleep claimed her.

Mr. Bell failed to appear on the day he had postponed his visit a second time. The following morning, a letter from Wallis, his servant, reached them. It stated that his master had been unwell for some time—the true cause of his delay—and that just as he was preparing to depart for London, he suffered an apoplectic seizure. Wallis added that the attending physicians believed he could not survive the night, making it highly probable that by the time Miss Hale received the missive, her former master would already be gone.

Margaret received this letter at breakfast. Her complexion drained instantly as she read it. Silently handing the note to Edith, she rose and withdrew from the room. Edith was profoundly shaken, reading the news with a sobbing, frightened, childlike demeanor that distressed her husband greatly. Mrs. Shaw was eating breakfast in her own quarters, and the responsibility of reconciling his wife to the terrifying proximity of death—for the first time in her life—fell upon him. Here was a man scheduled to dine with them today, now potentially dead or dying! It took considerable time before she could summon the resolve to think of Margaret. Suddenly, she sprang up and followed her daughter upstairs to Margaret's room.

Dixon was packing a few toiletry items, while Margaret hastily donned her bonnet, weeping continuously. Her hands trembled so violently she could barely fasten the ribbons.

"Oh, dear Margaret! How shocking! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto would telegraph or do anything you desire."

"I am going to Oxford. A train departs in half an hour. Dixon has offered to accompany me, but I could manage alone. I must see him again. Besides, he may be recovering and require care. He has been like a father to me. Do not detain me, Edith."

"But I must insist. Mother will disapprove entirely. Come and consult her; you do not know where you are going. I would not mind if he had his own residence, but staying in his Fellow's rooms! Come to Mother and ask her permission before you leave. It will not take a moment."

Margaret relented, missing her connection. Caught in the suddenness of the tragedy, Mrs. Shaw became bewildered and hysterical, causing the precious minutes to slip away unnoticed. However, another train departed in two hours. After various debates concerning propriety and impropriety, it was settled that Captain Lennox would accompany Margaret. The one constant in her resolve was her determination to travel by the next available train, regardless of what anyone said about the suitability of the step. Her father's friend, her own friend, lay on the brink of death; the realization struck her with such vivid force that she was astonished at the firmness with which she asserted her right to act independently. Five minutes before departure, she found herself seated in a railway carriage opposite Captain Lennox.

It was always a comfort to her to believe she had acted correctly, even if the outcome was merely to confirm his passing during the night. She recalled the rooms he had occupied, associating them forevermore with fond memories of her father and his single, cherished, faithful companion. They had promised Edith prior to leaving that, should their fears prove true, they would return for dinner. Thus, the long, lingering gaze around the chamber where her father had breathed his last had to be interrupted, replaced by a quiet farewell to the kindly old face that had so often offered pleasant words, merry quips, and eccentricities.

Captain Lennox fell asleep during

"And down the sunny beach she paces slowly, / With many doubtful pauses by the way; / Grief hath an influence so hushed and holy." — Hood.

"Is not Margaret the heiress?" Edith whispered to her husband later that evening, once they were safely locked inside their room following the somber journey to Oxford. She had pulled his tall head down, standing on tiptoes, imploring him not to be shocked before she dared to ask. Captain Lennox, however, remained completely in the dark; if he had ever heard anything, he had surely forgotten it. After all, what could a Fellow of a small college possibly possess? Yet, he had never charged her for her board, and two hundred and fifty pounds a year sounded absurdly generous, especially since she didn't even drink wine.

Edith descended onto her feet a fraction sadder, her romantic illusions deflated. A week later, however, she bounded toward her husband, performing a low curtsey. "I am right, and you are wrong, most noble Captain," she declared. "Margaret has received a lawyer's letter; she is the residuary legatee. The specific legacies total about two thousand pounds, with the remainder valued at approximately forty thousand pounds, depending on current property rates in Milton."

"Indeed!" he exclaimed. "How does she take her good fortune?"

"Oh, it appears she suspected she was due something all along, simply unaware of the magnitude. She looks terribly pale and white, claiming she fears it, but that is merely nonsense—it will pass quickly. I left Mother pouring congratulations down her throat and slipped away to inform you."

It became the universal consensus that Mr. Lennox was naturally destined to serve as Margaret's legal advisor. Being entirely ignorant of business formalities, she referred to him for nearly every matter. He selected her attorney, brought papers requiring her signature, and derived genuine joy from instructing her on the cryptic symbols of the law.

"Henry," Edith teased one day, "what do you suppose all these lengthy conversations with Margaret will eventually lead to?"

"No, I don't," he replied, flushing slightly. "And I request you not to tell me."

"Oh, very well; then I needn't warn Sholto not to invite Mr. Montagu so frequently to our home."

"Just as you choose," he retorted with forced composure. "Whatever you are contemplating may or may not occur. However, before I commit myself, I intend to survey the terrain clearly. Choose whoever you wish. It may seem uncivil, Edith, but if you interfere, you will ruin it. She has been exceedingly stubborn with me for a long time; she is only just beginning to thaw from her 'Zenobia' mannerisms. She possesses the makings of a Cleopatra within her, provided she sheds a bit of her paganism."

"For my part," Edith remarked with malice, "I am delighted she remains a Christian. I know so very few!"

There was no trip to Spain for Margaret that autumn. Although she clung to the hope that a fortunate circumstance might summon Frederick to Paris—a destination where she could easily arrange a meeting with a convoy—she was instead confined to Cromer. Her Aunt Shaw and the Lennoxes were bound for that coastal town. Having always wished her to join them, they made only sluggish attempts to facilitate her separate desires. Perhaps Cromer was, in a certain sense, the ideal location for her. She required physical strengthening and bracing alongside rest.

Among the hopes that had evaporated was the trust she had placed in Mr. Bell revealing the simple facts regarding the family circumstances preceding the tragic accident that claimed Leonard's life. Regardless of how his opinion might have shifted from what Mr. Thornton once held, Margaret had desired it to be founded on a truthful understanding of her actions and motivations. It would have brought her relief; it would have granted her peace on a subject that would otherwise haunt her forever, unless she could learn to stop dwelling on it.

Time had passed so long since those events that explanation was impossible except through the lens of Mr. Bell's death. She might resign herself, like countless others, to being misunderstood. Yet, despite reasoning herself into accepting this as an uncommon fate, her heart still ached with a longing that, years hence, before he perished, he might finally know how deeply she had been tempted. She believed she did not wish to hear that everything was explained to him, provided only that he understood. This wish, however, proved vain, like so many others. Once she had schooled herself into this conviction, she turned her entire heart and strength toward the immediate future, resolving to strive and make the best of it.

She spent long hours sitting on the beach, staring intently at the waves as they churned perpetually against the pebbly shore—or gazing at the distant swell sparkling against the sky, listening unconsciously to the eternal psalm rising continuously. She was soothed without knowing how or why. Listlessly seated on the ground, hands clasped around her knees, she watched while her Aunt Shaw conducted minor shopping expeditions, and Edith and Captain Lennox rode far and wide across the coast and inland. Nurses sauntering with their charges would pass and repass her, whispering in wonderment about what she could possibly find captivating enough to gaze upon for days on end.

When the family gathered for dinner, Margaret was so silent and absorbed that Edith deemed her moody, enthusiastically supporting her husband's proposal to invite Mr. Henry Lennox to stay at Cromer for a week upon his return from Scotland in October.

Yet, this period of solitude allowed Margaret to reorder events in their proper sequence, regarding both their origins and significance in relation to her past and future. Those hours by the seaside were not wasted, as anyone with the perception to observe or the care to understand the transformation in Margaret's countenance could attest. Mr. Henry Lennox was profoundly struck by the change.

"The sea has undoubtedly done Miss Hale an immense amount of good," he remarked when she first entered the room after his arrival in their family circle. "She looks ten years younger than she did in Harley Street."

"That's the bonnet I procured for her!" Edith announced triumphantly. "I knew it would suit her the moment I laid eyes on it."

"I beg your pardon," Mr. Lennox replied in his characteristic half-contemptuous, half-indulgent tone usually reserved for Edith. "However, I believe I discern the distinction between the charms of a dress and the charms of a woman. No mere bonnet could have rendered Miss Hale's eyes so lustrous yet so soft, or her lips so ripe and red—and her face altogether so filled with peace and light. She is like, and yet more..." he lowered his voice "...like the Margaret Hale of Helstone."

From this moment forth, the clever and ambitious man devoted all his energies to winning Margaret's affection. He adored her sweet beauty and perceived the latent breadth of her mind, which he believed could easily be guided to embrace all the objectives on which he had set his heart. He viewed her fortune merely as a component of her complete and superb character and position, yet he was acutely aware of the social elevation it would instantly afford him, the poor barrister. Ultimately, he envisioned earning such success and honors that he could repay her, with interest, that initial financial advancement he owed to her.

He had visited Milton recently on business concerning her property, returning from Scotland. With the keen eye of a skilled lawyer, ever ready to assess contingencies, he observed that significant additional value was accruing annually to the lands and tenements she owned in that thriving, expanding town. He was pleased to note that the current relationship between Margaret and himself, as client and legal advisor, was gradually erasing the memory of that ill-fated, poorly managed day in Helstone. Thus, he possessed unusual opportunities for intimate interaction with her, beyond those arising from the familial connections.

Margaret was only too willing to listen as long as he spoke of Milton, even though he mentioned none of the people she particularly knew. It had been the prevailing sentiment among her aunt and cousin to speak of Milton with disdain and contempt—feelings Margaret was ashamed to recall having expressed and felt upon first moving there. Conversely, Mr. Lennox surpassed Margaret in his appreciation of Milton's character and its inhabitants. Their energy, their power, their indomitable courage in struggle and combat; their lurid vividness of existence—all captivated and arrested his attention. He never grew weary of discussing them, failing to perceive until Margaret pointed it out—even amidst her delight—that too many of the ends they pursued were selfish and materialistic, tainted sins within so much that was noble and admirable.

Still, when other topics bored her and she offered brief replies to numerous inquiries, Henry Lennox discovered that asking about peculiarities of Darkshire character restored the light to her eyes and the glow to her cheeks.

Upon returning to town, Margaret fulfilled one of her seaside resolutions and took control of her own destiny. Prior to departing for Cromer, she had been as obedient to her aunt's dictates as if she were still the frightened little stranger who wept herself to sleep during her first night in the Harley Street nursery. But those solemn hours of reflection had taught her that she must one day answer for her own life and how she had lived it. She endeavored to solve the most difficult problem facing women: determining how much should be wholly subsumed in obedience to authority and how much might be reserved for freedom of action.

Mrs. Shaw was as good-natured as could be expected, and Edith had inherited this charming domestic quality. Margaret, conversely, likely possessed the worst temper of

"Here we go up, up, up; And here we go down, down, down!" sang the nursery rhyme, a stark contrast to the grim reality unfolding in Milton. While the song spoke of whimsical motion, the town's chimneys belched thick smoke, and the ceaseless roar, mighty beat, and dazzling whir of machinery fought a perpetual, mechanical battle against the air. Wood, iron, and steam labored endlessly, seemingly without sense or purpose, yet their monotonous persistence was rivaled only by the tireless endurance of the crowds below. These were not aimless throngs; driven by urgent intent, they moved with restless energy, searching desperately for answers to a single, terrifying question: *What?*

The streets of Milton held no loiterers, no strollers seeking mere pleasure. Every face was etched with lines of eagerness or deep anxiety. News was hunted with fierce avidity in the Mart and the Exchange, where men shoved one another aside with the same ruthless self-interest they displayed in life itself. A heavy gloom settled over the town. Buyers were scarce, and those who ventured to purchase were viewed with suspicion by the sellers. Credit had evaporated; even the most financially stable families faced ruin if the great neighboring port suffered further losses among its shipping houses.

For years, Milton had known no failures, but recent revelations of disastrous American speculations had shattered that illusion. Whispers circulated that prominent local businesses were doomed. Daily, men's expressions screamed what their tongues dared not speak: *"Who has fallen? How does this affect me?"* When small groups gathered, they cautiously listed the names of the survivors, terrified to hint at those likely to fail. Idle gossip could drag a solvent man down; one collapse often pulled many others into the abyss.

*"Thornton is safe,"* the crowd murmured. *"His business is vast, expanding yearly. With a head like his and such prudence mixed with daring, he cannot fail."*

Yet, behind this public reassurance lay a darker truth. One man would draw another aside, leaning close to whisper, *"Thornton's business is large, true, but he has poured all his profits back into expansion. He holds no reserve capital. His machinery is brand new, costing him a fortune we dare not mention to the uninitiated."*

Mr. Harrison, a critic who inherited his wealth and feared change, grumbled constantly, begrudging every penny earned by bolder competitors. But the truth was, Mr. Thornton was under severe pressure. He felt it keenly in his most vulnerable spot: his pride. As the architect of his own fortune, he believed his success stemmed not from innate genius, but from the power commerce granted to every brave, honest, and persevering man—the ability to rise above the fray and command influence. He dreamed of being a merchant prince, known from the East to the West, whose word passed like gold. This was the ideal instilled in him by his mother, who had read scripture aloud as a call to arms.

He had lived for distant glory, neglecting the immediate world, until the accidental friendship with Higgins bridged the gap between master and man. That connection revealed a shared humanity, a "fine point of the wedge" that allowed them to view each other's positions with newfound charity. However, the current economic crisis threatened to sever this bond. The fear of losing the trust of the very workers he had begun to respect added a painful sting to his existing troubles.

Reflecting on the strike of a year and a half prior, Thornton realized how much he had changed. Once young and impulsive, he now bore the weight of age and regret. That strike had stalled his orders, forcing him to lock up capital in expensive machinery and purchase vast quantities of cotton. Much of the work produced by his newly imported Irish hands was defective, damaging the reputation of a firm that prided itself on excellence. The resulting financial strain had been immense. Often, catching sight of Higgins, Thornton felt a surge of angry resentment, blaming the worker for the delays. Yet, he forced himself to curb this impulse. He resolved not merely to avoid conflict, but to demonstrate his mastery over his temper by granting Higgins access whenever business permitted. Slowly, the resentment faded, replaced by wonder at how two men bound by the same trade could perceive their duties so differently.

Now, however, the tide had turned violently. Bad trade had crashed the market, halving the value of Thornton's stock. Orders dried up, leaving his invested capital useless. Expenses continued to bleed his resources, and when bills for cotton came due, he could only borrow at exorbitant rates. Despair did not visit him; instead, he worked day and night, calculating every emergency. At home, he remained calm and gentle toward his wife and children. In the mill, his few words carried a weight of sympathy rather than the harsh judgment of the past.

One afternoon, Higgins observed his master's distress. Hearing a suppressed sigh as Thornton walked past a workshop, Higgins later mused, *"I reckon I know who'd be sorry to see our measter sitting so stiff as a piece of grey calico! The old parson would've fretted his wife to death if he'd seen the woeful look on our measter's face."* That evening, Higgins and a colleague stayed late, unseen, to finish a neglected task, ensuring the overseer credited the work to himself.

Later, on Marlborough Street, Higgins intercepted Thornton.
*"Measter,"* he called out, startling the hurried businessman. *"Have ye heard aught of Miss Marget lately?"*

Thornton paused, confused. *"Miss—who?"*

*"Miss Marget—Miss Hale, the old parson's daughter. Ye know who I mean, if ye'll just think a bit."* His tone was respectful, devoid of disrespect.

Suddenly, the winter frost that had frozen Thornton's features melted away, replaced by a soft, benevolent smile. *"Oh, yes! She is my landlord now. I hear through her agent occasionally. She is well and among friends. Thank you, Higgins."*

The lingering warmth in that "thank you" signaled something deeper to the astute Higgins. *"And she hasn't married, measter?"*

*"Not yet."* The cloud returned to Thornton's face. *"There is talk of a match with a family connection."*

Higgins stepped closer, lowering his voice conspiratorially. *"Is the young gentleman cleared? The one they called Master Frederick, her brother who was over here?"*

Thornton froze. *"Her brother?"*

*"Sure enough. I reckoned ye knew, or I wouldn't have mentioned it. We knew all along, Mary and me, but we held our peace."*

Thornton recovered quickly, breaking away to resume his business. *"It was her brother,"* he muttered to himself as he walked away, a strange mixture of relief and sorrow in his heart. *"I am glad. I may never see her again, but knowing she is safe... it is a comfort."*

This tiny thread of golden hope ran through the darkening web of his fortunes. His agent had heavily invested in a failing American house, leading to a cascade of collapses. Night after night, Thornton sat in his private study, poring over accounts long after his family slept.

One morning, exhausted and unable to sleep, he was interrupted by his mother, who stood in the doorway, dressed as she had been the previous day. They had neither rested nor slept properly. Their faces were pale and rigid from vigilance.

*"Son John,"* she said softly, *"can I sleep with an easy mind while you stay awake in care? You haven't told me your trouble, but you have had sore trouble for many days."*

*"Trade is bad,"* he replied, lifting his chin defiantly. *"I dread nothing. I know no man suffers because of me."*

*"But how do you stand? Will it be a failure?"* Her voice trembled.

*"And if it fails?"* She stepped forward, taking his arm, her eyes bright with urgency.

*"Honest men are ruined by rogues,"* he said gloomily. *"My creditors' money is safe—every farthing. But I don't know where my own is; it may be gone, and I penniless. Therefore, it is their money I should risk."*

*"No; but I would risk ruining many for my own paltry aggrandizement. Mother, I have decided! You won't grieve over us leaving this house, will you?"*

*"Remain the same John Thornton, making mistakes and trying to be brave in starting anew. But it is hard, mother. I have worked and planned, discovering new powers only to realize it is too late. All is over. I am too old to begin again with the same heart."*

Turning away, he covered his face with his hands

"Bear up, brave heart! We will be calm and strong;
Sure, we can master eyes, or cheek, or tongue,
Nor let the smallest tell-tale sign appear
She ever was, and is, and will be dear."

The air was heavy with the heat of a summer's evening. Edith entered Margaret's bedroom, arriving first in her dressing gown, then a second time fully attired for dinner. Initially, the room stood empty. Upon returning, Edith discovered Dixon arranging Margaret's gown upon the bed, though Margaret herself was nowhere to be seen. Edith paced anxiously, fidgeting with her preparations.

"Oh, Dixon! Not those dreadful blue flowers against that dead gold-colored gown. What taste! Wait a moment; I shall fetch some pomegranate blossoms instead."

"It isn't a dead gold color, ma'am," Dixon retorted, clearly irritated by the criticism of her selection. "It's a straw color. And blue matches straw perfectly."

However, Edith had already secured the brilliant scarlet blooms before Dixon could finish her objection. Once Edith had arranged the garland, she finally located her aunt.

"Where is Miss Hale?" Edith asked immediately upon trying on the flowers. "I can't make sense of it," she continued, her tone petulant, "that my aunt permitted her to adopt such wandering habits in Milton! I am constantly fearing she has encountered some horror amidst those wretched districts she wanders into. I should never dare to traverse certain streets without a servant; they are simply unfit for ladies."

Dixon, still stung by the insult to her aesthetic judgment, replied curtly: "It is no wonder, in my opinion, when one hears ladies discussing 'ladies' so incessantly—especially when they are such frightfully delicate, dainty creatures. Truly, it explains why there are no longer any saints upon earth..."

"Oh, Margaret! Here you are!" Edith exclaimed, rushing forward. "I have been searching everywhere for you. But look at your cheeks, flushed red with the heat, poor child! Think of what that tiresome Henry has done; he truly exceeds the bounds of a brother-in-law. Just when my party was assembled so beautifully, fitting Mr. Colthurst with precision, Henry arrives with an apology, using your name as an excuse, and asks if he may bring Mr. Thornton of Milton—your tenant, you know—who is currently in London attending to legal business. It will completely ruin my arrangement."

"I do not mind dinner," Margaret said softly. "I do not wish to eat. Dixon can bring me a cup of tea here, and I shall join you in the drawing-room once you return. I would greatly prefer to lie down."

"No, no! That is absolutely impossible. You certainly look dreadfully pale, though I attribute it solely to the heat. Besides, we cannot manage without you. (Lower those flowers slightly, Dixon. They look like glorious flames against your dark hair.) Remember, we planned for you to discuss Milton with Mr. Colthurst. Oh, yes! And now this man comes directly from Milton. I believe it will turn out splendidly after all. Mr. Colthurst can interrogate him thoroughly on all topics that interest him, and it will be delightful to trace your experiences alongside Mr. Thornton's insights for Mr. Colthurst's upcoming speech in the House. Truly, I consider this a fortunate stroke of Henry's. I asked him if he was a man one would be ashamed to introduce, and he replied, 'Not if you possess any sense, my little sister.' Therefore, I assume he possesses the ability to articulate his thoughts—a rare achievement in Darkshire, eh, Margaret?"

"Did Mr. Lennox mention why Mr. Thornton has come to town? Is it regarding the property?" Margaret asked, her voice tight with constraint.

"Oh! He has failed, or something similar," Edith dismissed lightly. "Henry told you about that the day you suffered such a severe headache—do you recall? (There, perfect, Dixon. Miss Hale adds so much distinction to our gathering.) I wish I were as tall as a queen and as brown as a gypsy, Margaret."

"But about Mr. Thornton?"

"Oh! I truly lack the capacity for legal matters. Henry would delight in explaining everything to you. The impression he conveyed to me was that Mr. Thornton is in dire straits yet remains a highly respectable individual, and that I should treat him with extreme civility. Since I did not know how to proceed, I came to you to request your assistance. Now, please come downstairs with me and rest on the sofa for fifteen minutes."

Captain Lennox arrived early. As Margaret grew flushed while addressing him, she began to inquire about the details she needed concerning Mr. Thornton.

"He ascended to town regarding the subletting of the property—Marlborough Mills, along with the adjacent house and premises. He is unable to maintain the lease; consequently, deeds and leases require review, and new agreements must be drafted. I trust Edith will receive him appropriately, though I observed she was somewhat displeased by my liberty in soliciting an invitation for him. However, I believed you would appreciate receiving proper attention, and one must be particularly scrupulous in honoring a man whose fortunes are declining."

He lowered his voice as he addressed Margaret, seated beside him. Before he concluded, he rose abruptly to introduce Mr. Thornton, who had just entered, to Edith and Captain Lennox.

Margaret watched Mr. Thornton with an anxious gaze as he was presented. It had been considerably more than a year since she had last seen him, and events had altered him significantly during that interval. His commanding figure still elevated him above the average man, granting him a distinguished presence derived from the natural ease of his movement. Yet, his face appeared aged and wearied. Nevertheless, a noble composure rested upon his features, impressing those who had recently learned of his diminished status with a profound sense of inherent dignity and masculine strength.

Aware from his first survey of the room that Margaret was present—he had noted her intent expression as she listened to Mr. Henry Lennox—he approached her with the measured demeanor of an old friend. His initial calm words sent a vivid flush to her cheeks, which never faded throughout the evening. She seemed to have little to contribute to him. She disappointed him by asking quietly what appeared to him to be merely necessary inquiries regarding her former acquaintances in Milton. Others, more intimately connected to the household, soon joined the circle, and he drifted into the background, conversing intermittently with Mr. Lennox.

"You think Miss Hale looks well," remarked Mr. Lennox, "do you not? Milton evidently disagreed with her, for when she first arrived in London, I believed I had never witnessed anyone so transformed. Tonight, she appears radiant. Yet, she is much stronger. Last autumn, she was exhausted by a walk of several miles. On Friday evening, we strolled up to Hampstead and returned. By Saturday, she looked as robust as she does today."

"We?" Who? The two of them alone?

Mr. Colthurst was a remarkably astute man and a rising parliamentary figure. Possessing a keen eye for character, he was struck by a remark Mr. Thornton made during dinner. He questioned Edith regarding the identity of that gentleman. To her surprise, she detected in his tone of "Indeed!" that Mr. Thornton of Milton was not an obscure name to him as she had anticipated.

Edith's dinner proceeded successfully. Henry was in high spirits, displaying his dry, caustic wit to excellent effect. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Colthurst identified a few shared interests, touching briefly upon them before reserving deeper discussion for private conversation later. Margaret looked exquisite in the pomegranate blossoms. Although she leaned back in her chair and spoke sparingly, Edith was not annoyed, for the conversation flowed smoothly without her participation. Margaret, meanwhile, studied Mr. Thornton's face. He never looked at her; thus, she could observe him unobserved, noting the subtle changes wrought by this brief passage of time.

Only at an unexpected word from Mr. Lennox did his face flash with the familiar look of intense enjoyment. The merry brightness returned to his eyes, his lips parted to hint at the brilliant smile of their youth. For an instant, his gaze instinctively sought hers, seeking her sympathy. But when their eyes met, his entire countenance shifted; he became grave and anxious once more, and he deliberately avoided looking near her again during the meal.

There were only two other ladies besides their party, and as they were engaged in conversation with her aunt and Edith, the group moved upstairs to the drawing-room. Margaret idly attended to some needlework. Soon, the gentlemen ascended, Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton continuing their close discourse. Mr. Lennox approached Margaret and whispered, "I genuinely believe Edith owes me gratitude for my contribution to her party. You have no idea how agreeable and sensible this tenant of yours is. He proved to be the ideal person to coach Colthurst on the facts he required. I cannot comprehend how he managed to mismanage his affairs."

"With his talents and opportunities, you would have succeeded," Margaret replied.

She did not entirely relish the tone of her words, although they merely articulated a thought that had also crossed his mind. Remaining silent, they overheard a rise in volume from the conversation near the fireplace between Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton.

"I assure you, I heard it discussed with considerable interest—perhaps curiosity regarding its outcome would be a fairer description. Your name was frequently mentioned during my

"For joy or grief, for hope or fear,
For all hereafter, as for here,
In peace or strife, in storm or shine."

Edith moved through the house on tiptoe, silently checking every word Sholto spoke the following morning. She acted as if even the faintest clatter of a footstep might shatter the serious conference currently underway in the drawing room. By two o'clock, the doors remained shut, and the silence within seemed absolute. Suddenly, the sound of a man's hurried footsteps echoed down the staircase. Edith peeked out into the hallway.

"Well, Henry?" she asked, her expression questioning.

"Well!" he replied, somewhat curtly. "Come in to lunch."

"No, thank you. I've wasted far too much time here already."

"Then it isn't fully settled?" Edith asked, her spirits visibly drooping.

"Not at all," Henry answered firmly. "It never will be settled, if 'it' refers to whatever I suspect you mean. That simply cannot happen, Edith. Please stop worrying about it."

"But it would be so wonderful for everyone," Edith pleaded softly. "I would feel so secure regarding the children if I knew Margaret was settled nearby. As it stands, I'm constantly terrified she'll drift off to Cadiz."

"I promise," Henry said, "that when I marry, I will seek out a young lady skilled in managing children. That is all I can offer. Miss Hale will not have me, and I shall not ask her."

"Then what exactly have you been discussing?"

"A thousand topics you wouldn't comprehend: investments, leases, and the valuation of land."

"Oh, leave if that's all," Edith dismissed. "You and she will seem dreadfully dull if you've spent hours debating such tedious matters."

"Very well. I shall return tomorrow with Mr. Thornton to discuss further with Miss Hale."

"Mr. Thornton? Why does he need to be involved?"

"He is Miss Hale's tenant," Mr. Lennox stated, turning away. "And he wishes to surrender his lease."

"Oh! Very well. If those are the details, I cannot follow them, so please spare me."

"The only detail I require you to grasp is this: ensure the back drawing room remains undisturbed, just as it was today. Generally, the constant comings and goings of the children and servants prevent me from explaining business matters satisfactorily. The arrangements we must finalize tomorrow are of critical importance."

No one ever discovered why Mr. Lennox failed to appear the following day. True to his word, Mr. Thornton arrived punctually. After waiting nearly an hour, Margaret entered, appearing pale and deeply anxious.

"I am terribly sorry, Mr. Thornton, that Mr. Lennox is absent," she began hurriedly. "He could have handled this far better than I can. He serves as my advisor in this matter—"

"I apologize for having come if it causes you trouble. Should I go to Mr. Lennox's chambers to search for him?"

"No, thank you. I wished to tell you how grieved I am to learn that I must lose you as a tenant. However, Mr. Lennox assures me that circumstances are bound to improve—"

"Mr. Lennox understands little of the situation," Mr. Thornton said quietly. "Happy and fortunate in everything a man desires, he fails to comprehend what it feels like to realize one is no longer young, yet thrust back to the starting line requiring the hopeful energy of youth. To feel half a lifetime vanished, with nothing accomplished, save the bitter memory of wasted opportunities. Miss Hale, I prefer not to hear Mr. Lennox's assessment of my affairs. Those who are content and successful themselves are often too quick to dismiss the misfortunes of others."

"You are being unfair," Margaret said gently. "Mr. Lennox merely spoke of the high probability he believes exists for you to recover—and indeed, surpass—the losses you have suffered. Do not speak until I have finished, pray, do not."

She collected herself, then began rapidly flipping through legal documents and account statements in a trembling, hurried fashion.

"Oh! Here it is! He drafted a proposal for me," she continued, her voice steadying slightly. "It shows that if you were to accept a sum of my own—a thousand eight hundred and fifty-seven pounds currently idle in the bank, yielding me only two and a half percent—you could pay me significantly higher interest and continue operating Marlborough Mills."

Her voice had cleared, becoming more resolute. Mr. Thornton remained silent as she searched for the document outlining the security proposals. She was desperate to view the entire situation strictly as a business transaction, ensuring the primary benefit lay with her. Yet, while searching for the paper, her heartbeat faltered at the tone of Mr. Thornton's voice.

His voice was hoarse, trembling with intense emotion as he called out:

"Margaret!"

For an instant, she lifted her gaze, then quickly tried to hide her luminous eyes by pressing her forehead onto her hands. Stepping closer, he implored her with another tremulous, urgent cry.

"Margaret!"

Lower still sank her head; more completely concealed became her face, nearly resting upon the table before her. He approached her closely. Kneeling beside her, he brought his face level with her ear and whispered, gasping the words:

"Be careful. If you do not speak now, I shall claim you as my own in some strange, presumptuous manner. Send me away immediately if I must go... Margaret!"

At this third call, she turned her face toward him, still veiled by her small, white hands, and pressed it against his shoulder, hiding it there entirely. It was too exquisite a sensation for him to desire seeing either deep blushes or loving eyes. He embraced her tightly. They remained in silence together. Finally, she murmured in a broken voice:

"Oh, Mr. Thornton, I am not good enough!"

"Not good enough! Do not mock my own profound sense of unworthiness."

After a brief pause, he gently released her hands from her face and arranged her arms as he had once positioned them to shield him from the rioters.

"Do you remember, love?" he whispered. "And how I repaid you with my insolence the very next day?"

"I recall how wrongly I spoke to you—that is all."

"Look here! Raise your head. I have something to show you!"

Slowly, she faced him, glowing with a beautiful, radiant shame.

"Do you recognize these roses?" he asked, producing his pocketbook containing preserved, dried flowers.

"No!" she replied with innocent curiosity.

"Did I give them to you?"

"No! Vanity prevented you. You likely wore similar roses elsewhere."

She examined them, puzzled for a moment, then smiled faintly.

"They are from Helstone, are they not? I know the deep indentations around the leaves. Oh! Have you been there? When were you there?"

"I wanted to visit the place where Margaret grew into what she is, even during the darkest times, when I held no hope of ever calling her mine. I went there upon my return from Havre."

"You must give them to me," she said, attempting to take them from his hand with gentle force.

"Very well. Only you must pay me for them!"

"How shall I ever tell Aunt Shaw?" she whispered after a long, delightful silence.

"Let me speak to her."

"Oh, no! I owe her—but what will she say?"

"I can imagine. Her first exclamation will surely be, 'That man!'"

"Hush!" Margaret warned softly, "or I shall attempt to demonstrate your mother's indignant tones when she says, 'That woman!'"
